# Decisions explained (MaatuMitra)

## What this project is

1. MaatuMitra is a class experiment for a course on antimicrobial resistance (AMR).
2. It is a "voice scribe": someone speaks Kannada, the computer writes it down.
3. Then a program turns the words into a structured record: symptoms, medicines, doses, advice.
4. Every item in the record must point to the exact words that were spoken (its "evidence").
5. A separate safety checker looks for dangerous mistakes, like a lost "not" or a wrong dose.
6. A human must approve the record before it counts as "saved". Nothing is saved automatically.
7. Different roles (ASHA worker, nurse, doctor, pharmacist, researcher) see only what they need.
8. A small helper answers questions about antibiotics only from approved cards, or says "ask a doctor".
9. All data is fake (synthetic). It is NOT a clinical tool and must never be used on real patients.
10. The goal is an honest, repeatable experiment that shows what works AND where it fails.

## How to read this file

- Each decision has an ID like D-7. The index table below lists all of them with a one-line outcome.
- Entries are in the order they happened. Old entries are never deleted; a changed decision gets a new entry that points to the old one.
- Every entry uses the same headings: what we had to decide, the options, what we chose, why, what we did, what could go wrong, what else could be done, how to change it, and its status.
- Words in **bold** the first time are explained in docs/glossary.md.
- "UNVERIFIED" means nobody has checked it yet. "Not native-validated" means no native Kannada speaker has checked it.
- Builder agents do not edit this file directly: they write `<module>/handoff/decisions.md`, which is merged here (see D-8).

## Entries

### D-1: Check the laptop before building   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: What this laptop can really do, so we do not plan work it cannot run.
- Options we considered:
  1. Assume a normal developer machine. Pro: no effort. Con: high risk of plans that fail (for example big models or Docker).
  2. Measure the machine and write the facts down. Pro: plans match reality; others can compare. Con: a few minutes of work.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: The rules forbid paid cloud, so the laptop is the main computer. Knowing its limits early avoids wasted work.
- What we actually did: Wrote infra/inventory.md with OS, CPU, RAM, GPU, free disk, Python, Docker, Postgres, ffmpeg, Ollama and network checks, each with the command used.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Free disk (~11 GB) changes as we download things. GPU memory (4 GB) may be too small for big speech models (UNVERIFIED until m2 tries).
- What else could be done: Use a free Colab or Kaggle GPU for heavy runs and log the hours in infra/inventory.md.
- How to change this decision later: Re-run the commands and update infra/inventory.md.
- Status: done

### D-2: Python environment: uv, a lockfile, light base + optional heavy extras   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to install Python packages so that anyone gets exactly the same versions, without filling the small disk.
- Options we considered:
  1. Plain `pip install -r requirements.txt` without exact versions. Pro: familiar. Con: versions drift; results may not repeat.
  2. pip-tools (a **lockfile** made by pip-compile). Pro: exact versions. Con: an extra tool; slower.
  3. **uv** with `pyproject.toml` + `uv.lock`. Pro: exact versions for every package, very fast, already installed. Con: one more tool for students to learn.
  4. Install everything (including PyTorch and speech models) by default. Pro: one command. Con: several GB; the laptop has ~11 GB free.
- What we chose: Option 3, with heavy packages as **optional extras**: `asr` (faster-whisper, soundfile, librosa, jiwer, papermill, ipykernel), `tts` (soundfile, librosa; m1 adds its engine), `llm` (ollama client), `helper` (sentence-transformers). Base = pytest, jsonschema, regex, pyyaml, ruff, numpy, pandas, matplotlib, fastapi, uvicorn, pydantic, psycopg[binary], pyjwt, streamlit, httpx, pre-commit, pip-audit.
- Why: The lockfile makes runs repeatable; extras keep `make setup` small (~400 MB). A module that needs speech models installs them with `uv sync --extra asr`.
- What we actually did: pyproject.toml, uv.lock (179 packages resolved), .python-version (3.11). `make setup` runs `uv sync --locked`. Python 3.11.15 (uv-managed).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The lock also pins the extras; their install on Windows/CPU/GPU is UNVERIFIED until m2/m8 try. PyTorch wheels are large.
- What else could be done: Add a CUDA-specific index for PyTorch if m2 needs GPU wheels (a decision entry by m2).
- How to change this decision later: Edit `[project.dependencies]` or `[project.optional-dependencies]` in pyproject.toml, then `uv lock`. Modules ask via their handoff notes.
- Status: done

### D-3: Problem: Windows path-length limit broke the environment   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: Where to keep the Python environment (the **venv**) on this laptop.
- Options we considered:
  1. `.venv` inside the repo (uv default). Pro: standard. Con: the repo path is ~180 characters; with package paths it passes Windows' 260-character limit (**MAX_PATH**). It really failed: jsonschema could not open one of its files.
  2. Turn on Windows long paths. Pro: fixes it for everything. Con: a system setting change; agents must not change system settings; needs the human.
  3. Keep the venv at a short path, `%USERPROFILE%/.venvs/maatumitra`, using the `UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT` variable. Pro: works now, no system change. Con: one extra variable; two clones on one Windows machine share one venv unless the variable is set.
- What we chose: Option 3, done automatically by the Makefile on Windows (only if the user has not set the variable). Linux/CI keep `.venv`.
- Why: It works without touching system settings.
- What we actually did: Makefile block `ifeq ($(OS),Windows_NT)`. README explains: when calling `uv run` directly on Windows, first `export UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT=$USERPROFILE/.venvs/maatumitra`. Second small problem: `uv python install 3.11` failed with "Missing expected target directory for Python minor version link" (uv 0.12.1 here); we created the venv by pointing uv at the managed Python 3.11.15 interpreter file directly.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: If someone runs `uv run` on Windows without the variable from a long path, it fails the same way. The uv bug cause is UNVERIFIED.
- What else could be done: The human may enable long paths (HUMAN_TODO) or clone the repo to a short path like C:\mm.
- How to change this decision later: Remove the Windows block in the Makefile.
- Status: done

### D-4: No Docker: how m5 will get PostgreSQL 16   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The brief wants PostgreSQL 16 in Docker, but this laptop has no Docker and no Postgres.
- Options we considered:
  1. Install Docker Desktop. Pro: matches the brief. Con: big download, needs admin rights and the human; uses scarce disk.
  2. Use MariaDB, which is installed. Pro: zero setup. Con: it is NOT Postgres: no row-level security (**RLS**) like Postgres; the security design would not hold.
  3. An embedded pip package that ships a Postgres server (for example `pgserver`). Pro: `uv add` and go. Con: which Postgres version and licence are UNVERIFIED.
  4. Official portable Postgres 16 binaries (EnterpriseDB zip). Pro: real Postgres 16. Con: manual download; licence and size UNVERIFIED.
- What we chose: Keep `docker-compose.yml` (postgres:16.15 pinned by digest, port 127.0.0.1:5432 only) for people with Docker; m5 must pick option 3 or 4 after verifying it, and record a decision.
- Why: The security design needs real Postgres 16 features. The architect only checked that `pgserver` exists on PyPI (version 0.1.4); the rest is m5's job.
- What we actually did: docker-compose.yml; infra/inventory.md section 3. `postgresql-wheel` was also seen on PyPI but its version 14.1.2 means Postgres 14, so it does not fit.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: pgserver may bundle another Postgres version or not work on Windows (UNVERIFIED). The compose file has never been run here.
- What else could be done: Run the m5 tests in CI on Linux with a Postgres service container.
- How to change this decision later: m5 writes a new decision; edit docker-compose.yml and m5_backend.
- Status: partly done

### D-5: One Makefile that works with make and mingw32-make   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to give one set of commands (`make test`, `make eval`, ...) on Linux CI and on this Windows laptop, which only has `mingw32-make`.
- Options we considered:
  1. A Python task runner (for example a tasks.py). Pro: same everywhere. Con: the brief asks for `make` targets.
  2. A Makefile with Windows-only commands. Pro: works here. Con: breaks CI.
  3. One GNU Makefile with `SHELL := bash` and plain bash commands. Pro: same file on Linux and Windows (run from **Git Bash**). Con: Windows users must use Git Bash and type `mingw32-make`.
- What we chose: Option 3. Every target calls `uv run ...`. Each module has `module.mk` with `mN-test` and `mN-mock`; the root includes all eight. Modules add themselves to `make data / eval / eval-full / eval-test / demo / clean` by appending to variables like `EVAL_TARGETS += m7-eval`.
- Why: The root Makefile never needs editing when a module grows, so parallel builders do not conflict on it.
- What we actually did: Makefile, m1_data/module.mk ... m8_helper/module.mk. Checked here: `mingw32-make test`, `lint`, `validate-fixtures`, `split-lock`, `m4`, `data`, `eval-test` (refuses), `audit`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Running mingw32-make from PowerShell may pick a different bash (for example WSL's) and fail; use Git Bash. CI behaviour UNVERIFIED until pushed.
- What else could be done: Add a `justfile` or tasks.py later if Windows users struggle.
- How to change this decision later: Makefile and */module.mk.
- Status: done

### D-6: Contracts as one shared, importable package with a validator   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How modules agree on data formats while staying independent.
- Options we considered:
  1. Each module writes its own checker. Pro: full independence. Con: eight slightly different ideas of "valid".
  2. One `contracts/` package with the **JSON Schemas** and `validate.py`, importable by every module. Pro: one definition of "valid". Con: modules depend on it (but it is a contract, not module logic).
- What we chose: Option 2. JSON Schema **draft 2020-12** for structure, plus "semantic" rules a schema cannot express (NFC text, offsets that match the text, auto-accept only without flags, and others listed in contracts/CHANGELOG.md).
- Why: A wrong format found at the boundary is cheap; found at the end it is expensive.
- What we actually did: contracts/schemas/*.schema.json (8), contracts/validate.py, contracts/CHANGELOG.md. CLI: `uv run python -m contracts.validate <schema> <file.json|file.jsonl|dir>` and `--check-fixtures fixtures`. Tests: tests/test_contracts.py (46 tests).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Matching a record's evidence against its transcript needs both files, so `validate.py` alone cannot do it; m3, m4 and integration must. Some semantic rules are architect choices that a module may find too strict.
- What else could be done: Publish the validator as a small package if the project grows.
- How to change this decision later: A new schema version + CHANGELOG entry, approved by the architect (section 4).
- Status: done

### D-7: Test layout: pytest importlib mode, one tests/ folder per module   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to run each module's tests alone and all tests together without name clashes.
- Options we considered:
  1. pytest default mode with `__init__.py` everywhere. Pro: common. Con: two files both called `test_cli.py` in different modules clash.
  2. pytest `--import-mode=importlib` with the repo root on the path. Pro: same file names are fine; no `__init__.py` needed in tests. Con: tests cannot import each other as modules.
- What we chose: Option 2. Every module has `tests/` with a skipped placeholder so `uv run pytest m1_data/tests` works from day one.
- Why: Independence (section 4): each module must pass its tests alone.
- What we actually did: `[tool.pytest.ini_options]` in pyproject.toml (`addopts = "--import-mode=importlib -ra"`, `pythonpath = ["."]`, testpaths for all modules). Result now: 74 passed, 9 skipped (placeholders).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Shared test helpers must go in a `conftest.py` inside the module's tests folder.
- What else could be done: Nothing needed now.
- How to change this decision later: pyproject.toml `[tool.pytest.ini_options]`.
- Status: done

### D-8: Handoff files so parallel agents never edit shared docs at once   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: Up to three builders work at the same time. If all of them write to this file, the glossary or HUMAN_TODO.md, they overwrite each other.
- Options we considered:
  1. Let everyone edit shared files. Pro: simple. Con: lost edits, merge conflicts.
  2. Each builder writes notes in its own folder; one person (the orchestrator) merges them with a script. Pro: no conflicts; numbering stays clean. Con: a merge step.
- What we chose: Option 2. Builders write `<module>/handoff/{decisions,glossary,ai_usage,human_todo,open_questions,licences}.md`.
- Why: Only one writer touches shared files, and the script is tested.
- What we actually did: infra/scripts/merge_handoff.py (`make merge-handoff MODULE=m1_data`). It renumbers `### D-?:` headings to the next free D-number, adds index rows, skips glossary terms that already exist, removes duplicate licence rows, appends the rest under "## From <module>", and moves processed files to `<module>/handoff/merged/<timestamp>/`. It writes nothing if any step fails. 10 tests in tests/test_merge_handoff.py. Documented in the plain-language-decisions skill.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: If a builder ignores the format (for example no `- Status:` line), the index row says "unknown".
- What else could be done: Add a format linter for handoff files.

### D-9: Agent and skill file format, checked against the current docs   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The exact file format for the 14 **subagents** and 5 **skills** (constraint 11: check the docs, do not invent).
- Options we considered:
  1. Write from memory. Pro: fast. Con: formats change; risk of fields that do nothing.
  2. Fetch the current Claude Code docs and follow them. Pro: correct today. Con: a few minutes.
- What we chose: Option 2. Fetched (2026-11-09) https://docs.claude.com/en/docs/claude-code/sub-agents and https://docs.claude.com/en/docs/claude-code/skills, which redirect (301) to https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents and https://code.claude.com/docs/en/skills; both were read.
- Why: The docs say: agents are Markdown files in `.claude/agents/` with YAML frontmatter (`name`, `description` required; `tools` as a comma-separated list; `model` can be `opus`, `sonnet`, `haiku`, `fable`, a full model id or `inherit`; `skills` preloads skills). Skills are `.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md` with frontmatter (`name`, `description`).
- What we actually did: 14 files in .claude/agents/ (generated from PROJECT_BRIEF section 7 so the spec text is copied exactly, after the required preamble, plus allowed-files and handoff sections). Builders: Read, Write, Edit, Bash, Glob, Grep. Language and clinical reviewers: Read, Grep, Glob, WebSearch, WebFetch, Write (Write limited by instruction to reviews/). `model: opus` for safety-engineer, the four reviewers/auditors and integration-qa; `inherit` for the rest. 5 skills in .claude/skills/. No README files inside those two folders, so they are not mistaken for agents or skills. Descriptions are quoted because a colon inside YAML broke parsing (a test caught it).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The docs say there is no way to limit Write to one folder, so the reviewers' limit is an instruction, not a hard rule. Whether `skills:` preloading works exactly as described is UNVERIFIED until an agent runs.
- What else could be done: Add a PreToolUse **hook** that blocks writes outside reviews/ for reviewers.
- How to change this decision later: .claude/agents/*.md, .claude/skills/*/SKILL.md; tests/test_shared_assets.py checks them.
- Status: done

### D-10: The two auditors also get Bash (a small deviation)   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: Section 7 says reviewers get ONLY Read/Grep/Glob/WebSearch/WebFetch, but the security auditor must "run the leak scan yourself", gitleaks and pip-audit, and the integrity auditor must check git history for test-file edits. Neither is possible without running commands.
- Options we considered:
  1. Follow the tool list strictly. Pro: exact. Con: the auditors cannot do their checklists; they would have to guess.
  2. Give only the two auditors Bash, with a written rule: read-only commands only. Pro: the checks really run. Con: the read-only rule is an instruction, not enforced.
- What we chose: Option 2 for scientific-integrity-auditor and security-auditor. The Kannada and clinical reviewers stay without Bash.
- Why: An audit that cannot look at git history or run the leak scan is not an audit.
- What we actually did: `tools: Read, Grep, Glob, WebSearch, WebFetch, Write, Bash` in those two agent files, with a "Read-only rule" section.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: An auditor could, by mistake, run a command that changes files. The orchestrator should check `git status` after each audit.
- What else could be done: A hook that allows only an allow-list of commands.
- How to change this decision later: Remove Bash from the two agent files.
- Status: done

### D-11: Write our own frontend-design skill   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The brief says: use the installed frontend-design skill if present, else write one.
- Options we considered:
  1. Use the plugin skill. Pro: written by experts. Con: it is in the plugin marketplace folder (~/.claude/plugins/marketplaces/claude-plugins-official/plugins/frontend-design, Apache-2.0) but NOT in installed_plugins.json, so it is not installed, and it would not come with a clone of this repo anyway.
  2. Write a project skill. Pro: travels with the repo; can include our 6 accessibility rules and Kannada rendering. Con: less general.
- What we chose: Option 2 (we did not copy the plugin's text).
- Why: m6 needs project-specific rules (WCAG AA, 48 px targets, Kannada fonts, flagged-first review).
- What we actually did: .claude/skills/frontend-design/SKILL.md.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Noto Sans Kannada's licence (SIL OFL expected) is UNVERIFIED until m6 fetches it.
- What else could be done: The human can install the plugin and let m6 read both.
- How to change this decision later: Edit the skill file.
- Status: done

### D-12: Schema: transcript.v1   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The exact shape of a transcript, so ASR, extraction and the verifier agree.
- Options we considered:
  1. Text only. Pro: simple. Con: no word timings or confidence; the verifier's ASR-risk check (C7) and "play this part" in the UI need them.
  2. Text + words with character offsets, times and confidence (the brief). Pro: everything downstream needs. Con: more to fill in.
- What we chose: Option 2, exactly the brief's fields. Offsets are **code points** in **NFC** text, end not included (like Python slicing). Times and confidence may be null. `latency_ms`, `audio_duration_s` and `hardware` may be null so that a "gold" transcript (model name "gold") is also valid.
- Why: Gold transcripts let m3 and m4 be built and tested before any ASR model runs.
- What we actually did: contracts/schemas/transcript.v1.schema.json. The validator checks that each word equals `text[char_start:char_end]`, words are in order, and times make sense.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: ASR tools give word times in their own tokens, not our words; m2 must map them carefully.
- What else could be done: Add per-segment data in a v2 if needed.
- How to change this decision later: transcript.v2 + CHANGELOG.
- Status: done

### D-13: Schema: record.v1   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The shape of the structured record and its **evidence** spans.
- Options we considered:
  1. Evidence per item (the brief: one span per medication, symptom, advice, follow-up). Pro: simple, matches the brief. Con: one medication span must cover drug, dose, frequency and duration, so spans can be long.
  2. Evidence per field (a span for the dose, another for the frequency...). Pro: more precise. Con: much more complex; not what the brief says.
- What we chose: Option 1. Dose units are a fixed list (mg, g, mcg, ml, tablet, capsule, sachet, drop, teaspoon, IU) or null, so "unit changed" is easy to detect. Numbers must be above zero or null.
- Why: Follow the brief; keep it checkable.
- What we actually did: contracts/schemas/record.v1.schema.json. The validator checks `char_end > char_start` and that the span length equals the offsets. Note: record.v1 has no "approved_by" field, so the rule "saved needs an approver" is enforced in the database and API by m5, not by the schema. An invalid fixture shows that adding `approved_by` is rejected.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The unit list may miss a unit that real speech uses (then m1/m3 ask for a v2).
- What else could be done: Per-field evidence in a v2 if the verifier needs it.
- How to change this decision later: record.v2 + CHANGELOG.
- Status: done

### D-14: Schema: verdict.v1   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How the safety checker reports results, including when a check itself crashes.
- Options we considered:
  1. Add an "error" status. Pro: explicit. Con: the brief lists only pass | flag | n/a; consumers might treat "error" as harmless.
  2. A crash becomes status "flag" with a reason starting "check_error". Pro: **fail-closed** by design: a broken check can never look like a pass. Con: the reason text carries the difference.
- What we chose: Option 2. The validator also refuses `auto_acceptable: true` when any check flags or when no checks ran.
- Why: In safety work, "I don't know" must be treated as "stop and look".
- What we actually did: contracts/schemas/verdict.v1.schema.json + rules in validate.py; invalid fixtures for each rule.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: In E3 (removing one check at a time) the verdicts list fewer checks; that is still valid.
- What else could be done: Add a "disabled_checks" field in a v2 so ablation runs are self-describing.
- How to change this decision later: verdict.v2 + CHANGELOG.
- Status: done

### D-15: Schema: gold.v1 and the "conversation text" rule   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: Where gold spans point to, when a conversation has several turns.
- Options we considered:
  1. Offsets inside each turn only. Pro: simple. Con: records point into the whole transcript, so the two would use different coordinates.
  2. One "conversation text" = turns joined by one newline; gold spans use offsets into it, plus the turn number. Pro: gold spans, gold transcripts and record evidence all share one coordinate system. Con: turn text may not contain a newline.
- What we chose: Option 2. The gold record is the record without extractor/status, with `gold_span` instead of evidence.
- Why: Comparing a record with its gold becomes a direct offset comparison.
- What we actually did: contracts/schemas/gold.v1.schema.json; validate.py checks each span against the joined text and its turn, and that `has_negation` is true when any item is negated or absent. A test checks gold transcripts equal the joined turns.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: A cross-turn negation ("ಇಲ್ಲ" in the next turn) cannot sit inside one span; the gold span marks the drug mention and `negated: true` carries the answer.
- What else could be done: Allow multi-span gold in a v2.
- How to change this decision later: gold.v2 + CHANGELOG.
- Status: done

### D-16: Schema: manifest.v1   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to record where every data file came from.
- Options we considered:
  1. Exactly the brief's fields. Pro: agreed. Con: no field links an audio file to its conversation id.
  2. Add a `conversation_id` field now. Pro: easier joins. Con: changes the brief's contract without discussion.
- What we chose: Option 1, plus: licence fields may say "UNVERIFIED" until a human checks them; `file` must be a relative path with forward slashes.
- Why: The orchestrator asked for the brief's fields exactly. The missing link is written down as an open question (m1 can encode it in the file name or `parent_id`, or ask for a v2).
- What we actually did: contracts/schemas/manifest.v1.schema.json; docs/open_questions.md.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Joining audio to gold by file name is fragile.
- What else could be done: manifest.v2 with `conversation_id`.
- How to change this decision later: manifest.v2 + CHANGELOG.
- Status: done

### D-17: Schema: access_case.v1   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The shape of one access-control test case.
- Options we considered:
  1. Free text. Pro: flexible. Con: cannot be checked or counted.
  2. Fixed lists for role, resource, action, ownership, attack, expected. Pro: countable (N >= 500), checkable. Con: a new resource needs a v2.
- What we chose: Option 2. Users are **UUIDs**. Resources: facilities, users, households, patients, encounters, records, medication_orders, consents, audit_log, research_export, pharmacist_orders_v, audio. Rule: token and setting attacks (tampered_jwt, expired_jwt, alg_none, wrong_aud, guc_tamper) must always expect "deny". SQL-injection cases may expect "allow" (a harmless insert can succeed safely).
- Why: Matches AGENT 6; makes the matrix automatic.
- What we actually did: contracts/schemas/access_case.v1.schema.json + rule in validate.py.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The expected answers must still be written by hand (not generated from the SQL), or the test is circular.
- What else could be done: Add an "endpoint" field in a v2 for API-level cases.
- How to change this decision later: access_case.v2 + CHANGELOG.
- Status: done

### D-18: Schema: card.v1   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The shape of a counselling card.
- Options we considered:
  1. Allow any status text. Con: someone could write "validated".
  2. Only two statuses: `source_verbatim` or `machine_drafted_not_native_validated`, `clinician_reviewed` false unless a real clinician reviewed it.
- What we chose: Option 2; the validator also requires Kannada script in `kn_text`; `version` is a whole number from 1.
- Why: Honesty rule: the data itself must say how trustworthy it is.
- What we actually did: contracts/schemas/card.v1.schema.json.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Fixture cards use example.org URLs and are not real advice.
- What else could be done: Add a reviewer name/date in a v2 once real reviews happen.
- How to change this decision later: card.v2 + CHANGELOG.
- Status: done

### D-19: Schema: review_report.v1   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: A machine-readable format for simulated reviews.
- Options we considered:
  1. Markdown only. Con: cannot count open Critical findings automatically.
  2. JSON report + readable Markdown. Pro: gates can check "no open Critical".
- What we chose: Option 2. `label` is always `ai_simulated_review`; severity critical/major/minor; confidence low/medium/high; finding ids unique.
- Why: AI reviews must never look like human validation.
- What we actually did: contracts/schemas/review_report.v1.schema.json.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: A finding's "fixed/open" state is not in the schema; the orchestrator tracks it in this file.
- What else could be done: A `resolution` field in a v2.
- How to change this decision later: review_report.v2 + CHANGELOG.
- Status: done

### D-20: Fixtures are generated by a script, and clearly fake   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to make at least 5 valid and 3 invalid example files per contract with correct offsets.
- Options we considered:
  1. Type JSON by hand. Con: Kannada offsets are easy to get wrong by one.
  2. A small script that writes them and COMPUTES every offset from the text. Pro: always right; re-runnable.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: One wrong offset would teach every module the wrong thing.
- What we actually did: fixtures/build_fixtures.py (deterministic: two runs give identical files). 6 synthetic conversations (fever/cough, cross-turn "ಇಲ್ಲ", pharmacist dispensing, physician code-switching, nurse multi-symptom with "if it does not come down", trap words), 8 transcripts (6 gold + 2 fake-ASR, one with a planted "500 mg" -> "50 mg" error), 7 records, 6 verdicts, 5 manifest rows, 6 access cases, 5 cards, 5 review reports; 31 invalid files, each explained in `invalid/README.md`. `make validate-fixtures`: "checked 79 fixture files: OK".
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The Kannada sentences were written by an AI and are not native-validated. ASR timings and confidences are invented (marked "FIXTURE: hand-made values, no model was run"). URLs use example.org because no real source was fetched for fixtures.
- What else could be done: Replace with m1's real generated data in integration (Phase C).
- How to change this decision later: Edit fixtures/build_fixtures.py and re-run it.
- Status: done

### D-21: A small fake drug list so m3/m4 can start early   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: m3 and m4 need a drug **lexicon** before m1 builds the real one.
- Options we considered:
  1. Make m3/m4 wait. Con: slows everything.
  2. An 8-row fixture with m1's exact columns. Pro: same format as the real one. Con: someone may mistake it for real data.
- What we chose: Option 2, marked FIXTURE_ONLY in every row, source_url on example.org, doses and AWaRe classes UNVERIFIED.
- Why: Lets work start now without pretending the data is checked.
- What we actually did: fixtures/lexicon/drugs_fixture.csv (amoxicillin, azithromycin, cefixime, cephalexin, ciprofloxacin, metronidazole, paracetamol, ORS); test in tests/test_shared_assets.py.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Dose ranges are from model memory, NOT from a fetched source; never use them for anything but tests.
- What else could be done: m1 replaces it with data/lexicon/drugs.csv with cited sources.
- How to change this decision later: Edit the CSV and its test.
- Status: done

### D-22: A shared, machine-readable negation table   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How m3 gets the Kannada negation rules (words like ಇಲ್ಲ "no" and endings like -ಿಲ್ಲ).
- Options we considered:
  1. Rules only in prose. Con: every module re-types them differently.
  2. A YAML table with id, regex pattern, type, example and status for each rule. Pro: one source for m3 and for m1's coverage counts. Con: if m4 also used it, a mistake in the table would be invisible to the safety check.
- What we chose: Option 2 for m3 and m1; m4 must write its OWN rules (independence).
- Why: The verifier is only useful if it can disagree with the extractor.
- What we actually did: .claude/skills/kannada-nlp/negation_table.yaml: 36 entries (7 strong words, 6 endings, 1 "without doing" rule, 1 soft rule, 1 compound, 5 clause splitters, 2 cross-turn answers, 13 must-not-trigger traps). All `status: hypothesis_unverified`. Architect additions are marked `origin: architect_addition`: fused -ಬಾರದು/-ಕೂಡದು/-ಬೇಡಿ endings, a stricter -ದಿದ್ದರೆ rule (a bare consonant must come before it, so positive ಓದಿದ್ದರೆ does not match), and traps ಇದೆ, ಬಂದಿದೆ, ಇದ್ದರೆ, ಓದಿದ್ದರೆ, ಬೇಡಿಕೆ. Tests check every trap matches no strong or suffix rule.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Not native-validated. Spoken forms we did not think of will be missed.
- What else could be done: The Kannada reviewer and real speakers fill the negation sheet (HUMAN_TODO b).
- How to change this decision later: Edit the YAML, bump `version`, add a decision entry.
- Status: done

### D-23: Pre-registration numbers H1-H4   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The success thresholds, written BEFORE any experiment (**pre-registration**).
- Options we considered:
  1. Choose thresholds after seeing results. Con: that is cheating, even by accident.
  2. Keep the brief's defaults and explain them. Pro: decided in advance; the human can still change them before the first test run, with a log.
- What we chose: Option 2: H1 >= 95% of critical errors flagged with <= 20% of error-free fields flagged; "rare" = <= 1 escaped critical error per 1,000 fields by the Wilson upper bound; H2 paired-bootstrap CI excludes zero; H3 0 unauthorized successes in N >= 500 (report 3/N); H4 >= 95% out-of-scope refusals, 100% dosing/diagnosis refusals, >= 90% in-scope answers from cards, every answer cites a card.
- Why: Reasons are in docs/hypothesis.md (for example, more than 20% false alarms causes alarm fatigue).
- What we actually did: docs/hypothesis.md, docs/severity.md, docs/metrics.md, each marked "written before any experiment, 2026-11-09".
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: With few test items, the "rare" bar may be impossible to prove; we will say so rather than claim it.
- What else could be done: A power calculation to choose N (future work).
- How to change this decision later: Edit docs/hypothesis.md BEFORE the first test run and fill its change log.
- Status: done

### D-24: Guarding the frozen test split   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to make sure nobody tunes on the **test split** by accident.
- Options we considered:
  1. Trust people. Con: accidents happen.
  2. A guard script + a lock file of hashes. Pro: mistakes are blocked and logged.
- What we chose: Option 2. `make eval-test` refuses unless ALLOW_TEST=1, PURPOSE is set, and docs/hypothesis.md is committed with no changes; it also refuses (logging nothing) if no module has registered an eval-test target; otherwise it appends date, git hash, who and purpose to results/test_access_log.md. `make split-lock` checks data/splits/test.lock (format: `<sha256>  <path>` per line) and warns if the lock does not exist yet; missing git-ignored audio is a warning, any changed file is an error.
- Why: Section 6.5.
- What we actually did: infra/scripts/eval_test_guard.py, infra/scripts/check_split_lock.py, 11 tests in tests/test_guards.py. Checked here: `mingw32-make eval-test` without ALLOW_TEST prints "eval-test REFUSED: the TEST split is frozen..." and exits with an error.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Someone can still read test files directly; the guard is a seatbelt, not a lock. The audit (git history) catches the rest.
- What else could be done: Encrypt test labels until the final run.
- How to change this decision later: The two scripts and the Makefile `eval-test` target.
- Status: done

### D-25: CI on GitHub Actions   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: What runs automatically on every push.
- Options we considered:
  1. No CI. Con: breakages found late.
  2. GitHub Actions with lint, tests, fixture validation, split lock, gitleaks (**secret scanning**) and pip-audit (known-vulnerability check). Actions pinned to exact commit SHAs.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: Constraint 3 and section 4 ask for these checks; pinning SHAs stops an action from changing under us.
- What we actually did: .github/workflows/ci.yml. SHAs resolved with `git ls-remote` on 2026-11-09: actions/checkout v7.0.1, astral-sh/setup-uv v10.3.3, gitleaks/gitleaks-action v2.3.9. `make audit` ran here: pip-audit on all 178 locked packages (all extras) found "No known vulnerabilities".
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: CI has never run (no remote yet): UNVERIFIED. gitleaks is not installed locally, so it has not been run here.
- What else could be done: Add a Postgres service container for m5 tests.
- How to change this decision later: .github/workflows/ci.yml.
- Status: partly done

### D-26: Code style checks: ruff and pre-commit   (Phase A / architect / architect / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to keep code tidy and catch simple bugs.
- Options we considered:
  1. No linter. Con: inconsistent code, missed bugs.
  2. **ruff** (linter + formatter) with rules E, F, W, I, B, UP, S (security), RUF; line length 100 handled by the formatter (E501 off, long Kannada strings are fine); pre-commit runs ruff and gitleaks before each commit.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: One fast tool; the security rules (S) catch things like unsafe subprocess use.
- What we actually did: `[tool.ruff]` in pyproject.toml, .pre-commit-config.yaml (ruff v0.17.4 = the locked version, gitleaks v8.30.1). `make lint` passes; `make fmt` fixes style.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: pre-commit hooks are not installed until someone runs `uv run pre-commit install`.
- What else could be done: Add a type checker later.
- How to change this decision later: pyproject.toml `[tool.ruff]`, .pre-commit-config.yaml.
- Status: done

### D-27: How the orchestrator runs the builder agents   (Phase B / orchestration / orchestrator / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to actually start the 14 "agents" described in the brief, and how to stop parallel agents from breaking each other's work.
- Options we considered: (1) Use the new files in .claude/agents/ as named subagent types. Pro: exactly what the brief describes. Con: Claude Code loads agent files when a session starts, so files created during this session may not be usable until a restart (we did not test this). (2) Start general-purpose agents and paste the role, rules and spec into each request. Pro: works now. Con: the agent-file settings (tool limits, model) are not enforced by the tool; they are only instructions. (3) Do everything in one agent. Pro: simple. Con: breaks the brief's independence rule and overloads one context.
- What we chose: Option 2. Each delegation message tells the agent to read its own .claude/agents/<name>.md file and infra/builder_rules.md first.
- Why: It works immediately and keeps the agent files as the single written spec. The trade-off is that "reviewers are read-only" is an instruction, not a hard lock, so the orchestrator checks `git status` after every reviewer run.
- What we actually did: Wrote infra/builder_rules.md (shared rules: environment, how to add packages without breaking the shared virtual environment, boundaries, no commits, handoff files). Started three builders in parallel: m1 text data, m4 verifier, m5 backend.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: An agent could ignore an instruction (for example edit a file outside its folder). Mitigation: the orchestrator reviews `git status` and the diff before every commit. The shared virtual environment is a single point of failure: if one agent runs plain `uv sync`, other agents' packages disappear.
- What else could be done: Restart Claude Code so the .claude/agents/ files load as real subagent types with enforced tool lists; give each module its own virtual environment (costs disk, which is tight: ~12 GB free).
- How to change this decision later: Edit infra/builder_rules.md; use the agent names from .claude/agents/ directly in a new session.
- Status: done

### D-28: Splitting the data module (m1) into a text pass and an audio pass   (Phase B / m1_data / orchestrator / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: m1 has nine big steps (drug list, templates, generator, split, speech synthesis, public speech download, manifest, docs, `make data`). One agent doing all of it risks running out of context halfway.
- Options we considered: (1) One agent does everything. Pro: one owner. Con: likely to stop half-done. (2) Text pass first (drug list, templates, generator, split, lock, manifest for text), audio pass later (TTS voices, noise/speed variants, FLEURS real speech). Pro: the verifier and extractor can use real text data early; each pass is smaller. Con: the test lock must be extended when audio is added.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: Text data is what the verifier (m4) and extractor (m3) need first. Audio is only needed by the speech-recognition module (m2).
- What we actually did: Started the text pass (task B1a in PROGRESS.md). The audio pass (B1b) starts after it, with the rule that it may only ADD lines to data/splits/test.lock, never change existing ones.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Adding audio to the lock later means the lock file changes once. We will record that change in git with its own commit and explain it here, so an auditor can see it was an addition, not an edit.
- What else could be done: Generate audio at the same time on a second machine or Colab.
- How to change this decision later: PROGRESS.md rows B1a/B1b.
- Status: done

### D-29: Who is the git author of the commits   (Phase A / repo / orchestrator / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: Which name appears on the commits the orchestrator makes.
- Options we considered: (1) The student's own git identity. Pro: they own the work. Con: it would say a human typed commits that an AI made. (2) A clearly labelled identity "MaatuMitra orchestrator <orchestrator@localhost>" plus a "Co-Authored-By: Claude" line. Pro: honest about who made each commit. Con: the student may want their own name on the history.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: Honesty about AI-generated work (constraint 9). The student can still add their own commits on top.
- What we actually did: Commits use `git -c user.name="MaatuMitra orchestrator" -c user.email="orchestrator@localhost"`. The global git settings were not changed.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Some course platforms expect commits from the student's account. The history is local only, so this is easy to change before pushing.
- What else could be done: Rewrite the author with `git rebase --exec 'git commit --amend --reset-author --no-edit'` before pushing (only if the student wants that and understands what it does).
- How to change this decision later: Change the author on future commits; history rewrite is optional.
- Status: done

### D-30: PostgreSQL 16 without Docker: EnterpriseDB 16.15 binaries zip   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to get a real PostgreSQL 16 on a laptop with no Docker and no admin rights (follows D-4).
- Options we considered:
  1. `pgserver` from PyPI (pip-installable Postgres). Pro: one `uv add`. Con: it ships PostgreSQL **16.2** (README badge and its pgbuild/Makefile, fetched 2026-11-09). 16.2 is older than the fix for CVE-2024-10976 ("row security below e.g. subqueries disregards user ID changes", fixed in 16.5, postgresql.org page fetched 2026-11-09). That bug is about exactly our design (RLS + switching roles), so it is a bad fit.
  2. EnterpriseDB "binaries from installer" zip, PostgreSQL 16.15. Pro: current patch release, no installer, no admin. Con: 373 MB download.
  3. Install Docker Desktop. Con: admin rights, big disk use; the human must do it.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: newest 16.x patch level, works without admin, the PostgreSQL License allows this use. We keep only the server parts to save disk (12 GB free).
- What we actually did: downloaded `https://get.enterprisedb.com/postgresql/postgresql-16.15-5-windows-x64-binaries.zip` (link found on https://www.enterprisedb.com/download-postgresql-binaries, fetched 2026-11-09; 373,254,386 bytes; sha256 `43bb45f173a6f08cf1d29a97a6d8deb119e8e8093a24c00d2d1001a0ccaa8281`, same hash on two downloads). Unpacked `pgsql/bin`, `pgsql/lib`, `pgsql/share` to `%USERPROFILE%/.maatumitra/pg16` (126 MB), deleted the zip. Data dir `%USERPROFILE%/.maatumitra/pgdata16`. `m5_backend/scripts/db_up.sh` runs initdb (scram-sha-256 passwords, superuser name from .env) and starts the server with `listen_addresses=127.0.0.1`, port 54329 (checked with `netstat`: `127.0.0.1:54329 LISTENING` only). `db_down.sh` stops it.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: EDB does not publish a checksum next to the link, so the sha256 only proves both downloads were identical, not that the file is authentic (UNVERIFIED). The zip also contains pgAdmin/StackBuilder, which we did not unpack or use. Windows only; Linux/CI uses the postgres:16 Docker image instead.
- What else could be done: verify the binaries' Authenticode signatures; use Docker on a machine that has it.
- How to change this decision later: point `PG_BIN` in `.env` to another PostgreSQL 16 `bin/`; nothing else depends on EDB.
- Status: done

### D-31: Database roles: who logs in, who owns, who can do what   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The brief says `api_login` is "NOLOGIN, privileges only through SET LOCAL ROLE". But the API must log in as *something*.
- Options we considered:
  1. API logs in as `owner_role`. Con: the table owner is the most powerful normal role; forbidden by the brief.
  2. `api_login` = a LOGIN role with **no privileges of its own**, NOINHERIT, member of the five app roles with "SET only" (PG16 `WITH INHERIT FALSE, SET TRUE`). It must run `SET LOCAL ROLE <app role>` to touch anything.
  3. One login role per app role. Con: five passwords in the API; the role choice would move into connection handling.
- What we chose: Option 2, plus three small NOLOGIN helper roles: `maint_role` (seed loader + research export, reachable only from `owner_role`), `pharm_view_owner` (owns the pharmacist view, one narrow policy), `audit_writer` (owns the audit function). A **bootstrap superuser** (name/password in .env) is used only by `dbtool`/tests to create roles and databases.
- Why: nothing a request uses is a superuser, has BYPASSRLS, or owns a table. If the API forgets `SET LOCAL ROLE`, it can do nothing at all (fail closed; tested).
- What we actually did: `m5_backend/scripts/dbtool.py` (`bootstrap_roles` refuses to continue if any of our roles is superuser/BYPASSRLS). Tests: `test_no_role_is_superuser_or_bypassrls`, `test_api_login_memberships_are_set_only`, `test_api_login_has_no_table_privileges_of_its_own`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: helper roles are a deviation from the brief's list (recorded here). Whoever has the `owner_role` password can become `maint_role` and read everything; whoever has the `api_login` password can become any app role.
- What else could be done: separate operator login for the export job; a real secrets manager.
- How to change this decision later: `scripts/dbtool.py` (roles), `sql/*.sql` (grants/policies).
- Status: done

### D-32: RLS foundation: FORCE on every table, default deny, one transaction per request   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How every request tells the database who is asking.
- Options we considered:
  1. Filter rows in Python. Con: one forgotten WHERE leaks data.
  2. RLS policies reading per-transaction settings (**GUCs**) set with `SET LOCAL` / `set_config(..., true)`.
  3. Session-level `SET`. Con: leaks to the next user of a pooled connection.
- What we chose: Option 2. Every one of the 10 tables has ENABLE **and FORCE** row level security; no policy means no rows; no policy is granted to PUBLIC; IDs are UUIDs; composite foreign keys keep patient/encounter/order facilities consistent.
- Why: the database refuses even if the API code has a bug. FORCE makes RLS apply to the table owner too.
- What we actually did: `sql/010_schema.sql`, `sql/020_rls.sql`, `app/db.py` (`request_tx`: BEGIN, `SET LOCAL ROLE` from a fixed allowlist, `set_config('app.user_id'|'app.facility_id'|'app.role'|'app.active_patient_id', v, true)`). Tests: `test_every_table_has_rls_enabled_and_forced`, `test_table_owner_with_no_policy_sees_zero_rows` (owner sees 0 rows of 8 patients), `test_role_with_grants_but_no_policy_sees_zero_rows`, `test_no_role_or_guc_leaks_to_next_transaction_on_pooled_connection`, `test_reset_role_leaves_no_privileges`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: anyone who can run raw SQL as `api_login` can set the GUCs to any user (residual limit; `test_residual_raw_api_credentials_can_set_gucs` shows it works). A malformed GUC raises an error (fails closed), it never widens access.
- What else could be done: sign the context (e.g. pass a signed token into the DB and verify it in a SECURITY DEFINER function) so raw credentials alone are not enough.
- How to change this decision later: `app/db.py`, `sql/020_rls.sql`.
- Status: done

### D-33: Access rule: ASHA   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: What an ASHA (community health worker) may see and write.
- Options we considered: 1. Facility-wide access (simpler, but an ASHA would see households she never visits). 2. Only households assigned to her and what hangs off them (the brief).
- What we chose: Option 2: SELECT/INSERT on households with `assigned_asha_id = me` (insert also needs her facility), and on patients/encounters/records reachable through those households. Her encounters must be `asha_household_visit` authored by herself; her records must be draft/flagged with no approver. No UPDATE, no DELETE, no orders, consents, audit, research. Audio follows encounter visibility.
- Why: least privilege, straight from the brief.
- What we actually did: policies `asha_*` in `sql/020_rls.sql`; expected table rows `asha:` in `tests/access_matrix_expected.yaml`; 192 base matrix cases for the ASHA role all pass.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: an ASHA cannot correct a typo she made (no UPDATE). Reassigning a household to another ASHA needs an operator.
- What else could be done: allow UPDATE of her own draft records for a short time window.
- How to change this decision later: `asha_*` policies + the `asha:` block of the expected table (edit both by hand, separately).
- Status: done

### D-34: Access rule: nurse ("draft records" = draft or flagged)   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The brief says nurses work on "draft records". record.v1 has statuses draft, flagged, approved, saved.
- Options we considered: 1. `draft` only. 2. `draft` and `flagged` (not yet approved). 3. All statuses.
- What we chose: Option 2. Nurse: SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE patients, encounters and draft|flagged records in her facility; SELECT medication_orders in her facility; she cannot set approved/saved or an approver. UPDATE is limited by **column grants** (patients: name/age band/sex; encounters: type; records: json/status). No households.
- Why: a flagged record is still a draft that needs work; approved/saved records are a physician's.
- What we actually did: `nurse_*` policies and column grants in `sql/020_rls.sql`; tests `test_nurse_cannot_approve_or_see_saved_records`; all nurse matrix cases pass.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: nurses cannot read approved/saved records at all (possibly too strict clinically: open question). Nurses cannot see households, so they insert patients by household id they get elsewhere.
- What else could be done: read-only access to saved records in her facility if clinicians want it.
- How to change this decision later: `nurse_records_*` policies + `nurse:` block of the expected table.
- Status: done

### D-35: Access rule: physician (approve only in her own name; saved is final)   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How approval works in the database.
- Options we considered: 1. Physician may write any approved_by value. 2. approved_by must equal the physician's own id; a saved record can never be updated.
- What we chose: Option 2. Physician = nurse rights for any record status in her facility + INSERT/UPDATE medication_orders (patient and facility taken from the encounter) + approve/save with `approved_by = me`. UPDATE policy excludes status 'saved'.
- Why: nobody can approve in a colleague's name; a saved record cannot be changed silently.
- What we actually did: `physician_*` policies; tests `test_physician_cannot_approve_in_someone_elses_name`, `test_saved_record_is_immutable`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: correcting a saved record needs a new record (no amendment workflow yet).
- What else could be done: an amendment table that links a correction to the saved original.
- How to change this decision later: `physician_records_update` policy.
- Status: done

### D-36: Access rule: pharmacist only through a security_barrier view and an "active patient"   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How a pharmacist sees orders without any table grant.
- Options we considered: 1. Grant SELECT on medication_orders with a policy. Con: the brief says no table grants. 2. A `security_invoker` view. Con: needs table grants for the pharmacist. 3. A normal (owner-rights) view with `security_barrier`, owned by a narrow role.
- What we chose: Option 3. `clinic.pharmacist_orders_v` (order id, 8-char patient ref, drug, dose, unit, frequency, duration, status) owned by `pharm_view_owner`, whose only policy is: facility = mine AND status = 'ready_to_dispense' AND patient_id = `app.active_patient_id`. The view's WHERE clause repeats this. The API sets the active patient only via `POST /pharmacist/active-patient`, which checks that a ready order exists for that patient at her facility (through the view) and writes an audit row (allow or deny); the choice is kept server-side for 10 minutes.
- Why: the predicate is enforced twice in the database; the pharmacist can never list all patients.
- What we actually did: `sql/030_views.sql`, `app/main.py`; test `test_pharmacist_active_patient_flow_is_checked_and_audited`; matrix: other facility, non-active patient and non-ready order are all denied even when the DB context is set directly.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: **the system cannot prove the patient is physically present**; it only narrows scope and audits. A pharmacist could try patient ids one by one; each try is audited (no lockout yet). The 8-character patient ref is part of the UUID (not secret, not a name).
- What else could be done: rate-limit activations; require a code printed on the prescription.
- How to change this decision later: `sql/030_views.sql`, `set_active_patient` in `app/main.py`.
- Status: done

### D-37: Access rule: researcher only sees a de-identified export   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How researchers get data without seeing patients.
- Options we considered: 1. Views over base tables. Con: easy to leak ids/text. 2. A separate `research` schema filled by an export job (full refresh each time).
- What we chose: Option 2. pseudonym = first 20 hex chars (80 bits) of HMAC-SHA256(key from `.env`, patient_id); age band, sex, encounter type, month (YYYY-MM), record status, symptom names, structured medication fields. No ids, no names, no free text (advice, follow-up and evidence spans dropped). Strings must be in a closed allowlist (symptom list in code, drug names from the lexicon CSV), otherwise dropped. Only `consents.research_ok = true` at export time; withdrawal disappears at the next export.
- Why: researchers cannot reverse the pseudonym without the key; a full refresh makes withdrawal simple.
- What we actually did: `app/export.py`, `sql/010_schema.sql` (research table), `sql/020_rls.sql`. Tests: `test_export_contains_no_ids_names_dates_or_free_text` (scans every id, name, exact date and free-text token: 0 hits), `test_only_consenting_patients_and_withdrawal_removes_at_next_export`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: age band + sex + month + rare drug in a tiny facility could still re-identify someone (no k-anonymity check). 80-bit truncation: collisions are negligible at this size but not zero. The free-text scan exempts words that are also allowlisted drug/symptom names.
- What else could be done: k-anonymity / suppression of small cells; rotate the HMAC key per study.
- How to change this decision later: `app/export.py` (`PSEUDONYM_HEX`, vocabularies).
- Status: done

### D-38: Saved-record gate in the database and the API   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to make sure nothing is "saved" without approval and with open safety flags.
- Options we considered: 1. Only check in the UI. 2. DB CHECK constraint + API check of the verdict.
- What we chose: Option 2. `CHECK (status <> 'saved' OR (approved_by IS NOT NULL AND approved_at IS NOT NULL))`. `POST /records/{id}/save` validates the verdict.v1 and refuses (409, audited `save_rejected`) when any check is `flag`, `auto_acceptable` is false, or checks is empty; 422 if the verdict belongs to another record.
- Why: two independent layers (DB + server), both fail closed.
- What we actually did: `sql/010_schema.sql`, `save_record` in `app/main.py`. Tests: `test_saved_check_constraint`, `test_saved_gate_rejects_unresolved_flags_and_accepts_clean_verdict`. Finding: for a physician, the RLS WITH CHECK already refuses an unapproved save before the CHECK runs.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: there is no way yet to mark a flag as "resolved by a human"; the API trusts the verdict the client sends (it does not re-run the verifier).
- What else could be done: store the verdict server-side from m4_verify output and add explicit flag-resolution records.
- How to change this decision later: `save_record` in `app/main.py`; the CHECK in `sql/010_schema.sql`.
- Status: done

### D-39: Audit log: append-only, written through one SECURITY DEFINER function   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The brief says "INSERT-only for the api", but pharmacists and researchers must have NO table grants.
- Options we considered: 1. Grant INSERT on audit_log to every app role. Con: breaks "no table grants" for pharmacist/researcher; lets a role forge another user's row. 2. A minimal SECURITY DEFINER function `app.audit_write(action, table, row_id, outcome)` (pinned search_path) owned by `audit_writer`; user and role come from the request GUCs, not from arguments.
- What we chose: Option 2, plus REVOKE UPDATE/DELETE/TRUNCATE from everyone including the owner, and triggers that raise on UPDATE/DELETE/TRUNCATE. Columns are enums/ids only (no free-text column exists).
- Why: no role can write arbitrary audit rows, and nobody can change them.
- What we actually did: `sql/040_audit.sql`. Tests in `tests/test_audit_log.py`: update/delete/truncate fail for all 5 app roles, the owner and even the superuser (trigger).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: a superuser can disable triggers; the owner can re-grant itself rights. Audit rows written inside a failed request are rolled back, so the API writes the "deny" row in a second transaction; if that also fails, the deny is not logged.
- What else could be done: ship the log to append-only storage outside the database.
- How to change this decision later: `sql/040_audit.sql`.
- Status: done

### D-40: Nobody gets DELETE; facilities, users and consents are closed to app roles (scope cut)   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: The brief lists no rights for deleting, for facilities/users, or for managing consent.
- Options we considered: 1. Give nurses consent management. 2. Default deny (only the operator via `maint_role` changes consents).
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: "default deny, then explicit policies" means unlisted rights stay closed.
- What we actually did: no grants/policies for those; all such matrix cells expect and get deny.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: a real clinic needs a consent workflow (open question).
- What else could be done: a nurse/ASHA consent endpoint with its own audit action.
- How to change this decision later: `sql/020_rls.sql` + expected table.
- Status: done (scope cut)

### D-41: Authentication: PyJWT mock issuer, HS256 pinned   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How users prove who they are in the sandbox.
- Options we considered: 1. A real identity provider (cost/time). 2. A local mock issuer with strict verification.
- What we chose: Option 2: HS256 only (alg pinned; `none`, HS512 and RS/HS confusion rejected), `exp`, `iat`, `iss`, `aud`, `sub`, `role`, `fid` required, no leeway, expiry 300 s (max 900), signing key >= 32 chars from `.env`, and the token's user/role/facility must match the synthetic directory. Role is taken ONLY from the verified token. Login is rate-limited (5 per minute per IP+user, 30 per IP).
- Why: covers the classic JWT mistakes listed in the brief.
- What we actually did: `app/auth.py`, `app/ratelimit.py`, `seed/synthetic_users.json`; `tests/test_auth_jwt.py` (24 cases) + 143 token-attack matrix cases.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: one shared mock password; in-memory rate limit (resets on restart, one process only); no token revocation.
- What else could be done: asymmetric keys (RS256/EdDSA) from a real issuer; refresh tokens.
- How to change this decision later: `app/auth.py`.
- Status: done

### D-42: API hygiene: forbid extra fields, generic errors, 404 for hidden rows, audio after authorisation   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How the API avoids mass assignment, injection and information leaks.
- Options we considered: (1) default FastAPI behaviour (detailed error bodies echoing input); (2) strict models and generic errors.
- What we chose: (2). Every body model has `extra="forbid"`; server-owned fields (facility, author, approver, assigned ASHA, patient of an order) come from the token or the RLS-visible encounter. Only parameterised SQL (`psycopg`), identifiers via `psycopg.sql.Identifier` from fixed lists. Errors are `{"error": "<generic>"}`, no stack traces, no OpenAPI docs. Rows hidden by RLS return 404. Audio is served only after the encounter is visible to the caller; the file name is built from a parsed UUID.
- Why: OWASP A01/A03/A04/A05.
- What we actually did: `app/models.py`, `app/main.py`; tests `test_models_ratelimit.py`, `test_api.py`; matrix sqli (192), mass_assignment (20), idor_audio (26), id_guess (100) cases all pass.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: 403 (grant/WITH CHECK) vs 404 (hidden) can still reveal that a row exists when updating a visible row. No TLS (127.0.0.1 only).
- What else could be done: map every 403 on a write to 404; add request size limits.
- How to change this decision later: `app/main.py`.
- Status: done

### D-43: Access matrix E5: 1486 cases, 0 unauthorized successes   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to prove the rules hold without the test being circular.
- Options we considered: 1. Generate the expected outcomes from the SQL (circular: rejected). 2. Write the expected table by hand from the brief, before writing any policy.
- What we chose: Option 2. `tests/access_matrix_expected.yaml` was written by hand first. 960 base cases (5 roles x 12 resources x 4 actions x 4 ownerships) run at DB level through the same `request_tx` as the API, each rolled back; 526 attack cases run through the API (tampered/expired/alg-none/wrong-aud tokens, id guessing, SQLi in path ids, query params, login fields, enum/pattern fields and free-text JSON, mass assignment, audio IDOR incl. path traversal, GUC tampering via headers/params/body SQL and at DB level RESET ROLE / SET ROLE owner / SET SESSION AUTHORIZATION / garbage GUCs). Every case validates as access_case.v1.
- Why: rule of three: 0 failures in N trials gives a 95% upper bound of about 3/N.
- What we actually did: `matrix/generate.py`, `matrix/run.py`, `tests/test_access_matrix.py`. Result (2026-11-10, PostgreSQL 16.15 local): **unauthorized successes: 0 of 1385 deny-expected cases (1486 total); 95% upper bound 3/1385 = 0.0022.** Unexpected denials (availability bugs): 0 of 101 allow-expected. Integrity failures: 0 (SQLi text stored and read back byte-identical). Files: `results/m5_backend/access_matrix_results.jsonl`, `access_matrix_summary.json`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: the cases are not independent trials (many share one policy), so 3/N is optimistic. The matrix tests the rules we wrote; both the table and the SQL were written by the same AI agent from the same brief, so a shared misreading of the brief would not be caught. **Residual limit: anyone holding raw DB credentials can set GUCs / SET ROLE; credentials must stay out of reach (constraint 3).** CI run: UNVERIFIED.
- What else could be done: an independent human writes a second expected table; property-based fuzzing; run in CI on every push.
- How to change this decision later: edit the YAML by hand; never generate it from SQL.
- Status: done locally; CI pending

### D-44: Problems hit while building m5_backend (and fixes)   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to handle five problems found during the build.
- Options we considered: fix each at the root vs. work around it.
- What we chose: fix at the root.
- Why: each one could hide a real failure.
- What we actually did:
  1. `unzip 'pgsql/share/*'` skipped sub-folders, so initdb failed ("could not open directory .../share/timezonesets"); re-downloaded (same sha256) and extracted with `'pgsql/share/**'`.
  2. The server inherited the shell's output pipe, so the setup command never returned and stopping it killed Postgres; `db_up.sh` now redirects pg_ctl's stdin/stdout/stderr.
  3. Migration error "permission denied for function audit_write": EXECUTE was granted after ownership moved to `audit_writer`; the grant now comes first.
  4. First matrix run reported 3 "unauthorized successes": a **test bug**: the physician "role escalation" probe changed the role claim to "physician", i.e. it sent a genuine token. The probe now really changes the claim and asserts it did. Re-run: 0.
  5. After a lint refactor, FastAPI could not resolve a function-local `Annotated` dependency (with `from __future__ import annotations`) and returned 422 everywhere; the dependency is now module-level. The matrix caught this as unexpected denials.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: none known left from these; problem 4 shows the matrix itself can contain bugs, so a human should read `matrix/run.py`.
- What else could be done: a self-test that every attack probe differs from its control request.
- How to change this decision later: n/a.
- Status: done

### D-45: Fail-closed verifier with a rule registry   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How the safety checker should behave when something goes wrong inside it, and how to switch single checks off for the ablation experiment (E3).
- Options we considered: (1) Catch errors and ignore them: simple, but a crash would silently let a bad record through (unsafe). (2) Stop the whole program on any error: safe but one bad record blocks a batch. (3) Turn any error inside a check into a "flag" for that check and keep going: safe and keeps working. For switching checks off: (a) edit code, (b) a list of checks in a config file.
- What we chose: (3) plus (b). Every check is a plain function in `REGISTRY` (checks.py); the verifier calls it, and ANY exception becomes status "flag" with reason "check_error: ...". A record is "auto-acceptable" only if nothing flags AND at least one check actually passed.
- Why: "Fail-closed" means: when unsure, ask a human. A checker that says "fine" after crashing is the worst outcome. A config list (`disabled: [C3]`) lets E3 remove one check at a time without touching code.
- What we actually did: `m4_verify/verifier.py`, `m4_verify/checks.py`, `m4_verify/config/default.yaml`. Tests: `uv run pytest m4_verify/tests/test_checks.py -k "fail_closed or disable or garbage or malformed"` (forced exception in each of C1..C8 gives flag + check_error + auto_acceptable false).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: A check that returns "pass" by mistake (a logic bug, not an exception) is not caught by this mechanism; only tests can find that. If ALL checks are disabled the record is never auto-acceptable (by design).
- What else could be done: Run two independent verifiers and require both to pass; property-based tests.
- How to change this decision later: `m4_verify/verifier.py` (the try/except and the auto_acceptable rule).
- Status: done

### D-46: C1 span-exists   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to prove that the quoted evidence really is in the transcript.
- Options we considered: (1) Search the span anywhere in the transcript: tolerant, but a span could be "found" in the wrong turn (for example inside a question instead of the answer). (2) Compare the exact slice at the stated offsets after Unicode NFC: strict and simple.
- What we chose: (2), plus a check that the record's `source_transcript_id` equals the transcript's `audio_id`, plus a contract validation of both inputs.
- Why: Offsets decide WHICH clause (and so which negation) the span belongs to; a span at the wrong place could change the meaning.
- What we actually did: `checks.py::c1_span_exists`; tests `test_c1_*`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: An extractor that computes offsets on a different normalisation of the text (for example after removing ZWNJ) will be flagged everywhere; that is intended but costs review time.
- What else could be done: Report the nearest matching position to help the reviewer.
- How to change this decision later: `checks.py::c1_span_exists`.
- Status: done

### D-47: C2 span-supports-value, using the verifier's own parsers   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to check that a value (drug, dose, symptom) really follows from the quoted words, without trusting the extractor.
- Options we considered: (1) Re-use m3's parser: cheap, but the same bug would hide in both (not independent). (2) Write our own small normaliser, number parser and lexicon matcher: more work, independent. (3) Ask an LLM: not deterministic.
- What we chose: (2): `textnorm.py` (NFC, BOM/zero-width removal, ZWJ/ZWNJ removed for comparison only, Kannada digits to ASCII, `regex` tokeniser), `numbers.py` (digits + Kannada number words), `lexicon.py` (drug variants as token sequences). Free-text advice/follow-up gets "n/a" (meaning cannot be re-derived by rules).
- Why: Independence is the whole point of a safety check. Unknown drugs or symptoms FLAG instead of passing.
- What we actually did: `checks.py::c2_span_supports_value`; tests in test_checks.py and test_text_numbers_lexicon.py.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Kannada number words and symptom words were written by an AI agent (UNVERIFIED, not native-validated). Missing spoken variants cause false flags (safe direction, but review burden). Advice text is not checked for meaning.
- What else could be done: Native-speaker review of the number/symptom lists; English-Kannada advice paraphrase checks.
- How to change this decision later: `numbers.py`, `lexicon.py` (SYMPTOMS, UNIT_FORMS), `checks.py`.
- Status: done (word lists UNVERIFIED)

### D-48: C3 negation-consistency with independent, hand-coded rules   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to decide whether the words around a span say "not", and compare that with the record's `negated` / `present` field.
- Options we considered: (1) Read the shared table `.claude/skills/kannada-nlp/negation_table.yaml` (same as m3): a mistake in the table would then be invisible. (2) Hand-code our own rules from the brief and grammar knowledge: independent. (3) A statistical model: needs labelled Kannada data we do not have.
- What we chose: (2). Whole-word cues (ಇಲ್ಲ, ಇಲ್ಲಾ, ಅಲ್ಲ, ಬೇಡ, ಬೇಡಿ, ಬಾರದು, ಕೂಡದು), token-final suffixes (-ಿಲ್ಲ, -ುದಿಲ್ಲ, consonant+ದಿದ್ದರೆ, fused -ಬಾರದು/-ಕೂಡದು/-ಬೇಡ(ಿ)), -ದೆ only on a verb-stem list, soft casual -ಲ್ಲ, ಯಾವುದೇ...ಇಲ್ಲ, English no/not/never. Scope = the clause (split at punctuation and ಆದರೆ/ಮತ್ತು/ಹಾಗೂ/ಆದ್ದರಿಂದ). Cross-turn: a turn whose FIRST clause is only ಇಲ್ಲ/ಇಲ್ಲಾ/ಹೌದು answers the previous turn's question clause(s). Soft cues count as negation (conservative) and also raise C8.
- Why: Matching on whole tokens (never substrings) avoids the classic trap ಇಲ್ಲಿ (here) containing ಇಲ್ಲ. "First clause" (not "whole turn") was chosen because answers like "ಇಲ್ಲ, ಅವನು ಮಲಗಿದ್ದ." are common; this is a documented extension of the brief.
- What we actually did: `negation.py`; 125+ tests in `tests/test_negation.py` (every cue: positive, minimal-pair twin, trap; clause scope; cross-turn); audit in `negation_audit.md`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Not native-validated. Coordination ("A ಮತ್ತು B ಬೇಡ") is handled by a heuristic (bare item inherits the negation and C8 flags it). Questions without "?" (ASR text often has no punctuation) are not recognised as questions. Double negation fused with quotatives is missed (audit line 40).
- What else could be done: Native-speaker review of every rule; a held-out real-text audit; real spoken transcripts.
- How to change this decision later: `negation.py` (rule tables at the top) and its tests.
- Status: partly done (rules are hypotheses; needs human)

### D-49: Negation rules changed after the real-text audit   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: What to do with the errors found when running the rules on a real Kannada Wikipedia article and a news article.
- Options we considered: (1) Keep the brief's starting rules unchanged and only report errors. (2) Fix clear, narrow errors with tests, and report the rest honestly.
- What we chose: (2). Changes: ಇಲ್ಲವೇ/ಇಲ್ಲವೆ now mean "or" (clause split) unless "?" follows (was a frequent false positive); new strong rules S-VALLA (ರೋಗವಲ್ಲ, trap ಮಾತ್ರವಲ್ಲ), S-DIRU (ಬಾರದಿರುವುದು), S-ILLA-FUSED (ಮಾರ್ಗವಿಲ್ಲದೆ), S-DANTE (ಬರದಂತೆ), V-AAGADE (ಸಾಧ್ಯವಾಗದೆ), V-ISADE (ಗಣಿಸದೆ); ಇಲ್ಲದ/ಇಲ್ಲದೆ became SOFT cues.
- Why: The "or" reading of ಇಲ್ಲವೇ would have marked many harmless sentences as negated; the missed fused forms are real negations.
- What we actually did: `negation.py`, new test rows in `tests/test_negation.py`, `negation_audit.md`, `m4_verify/audit/`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The fixes were designed on the same 52 sentences they were measured on, so the after-fix numbers are optimistic. Formal written Kannada differs from spoken Kannada.
- What else could be done: Audit a second, unseen article and real conversational transcripts.
- How to change this decision later: `negation.py`; re-run `mingw32-make m4-audit`.
- Status: done (needs human re-check)

### D-50: C4 dose-range: blank lexicon range is a flag (fail-closed)   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: What to do when the lexicon has no verified dose range, when the unit is not in milligrams, and what counts as "unusual".
- Options we considered: (1) Blank range -> "n/a" (no review cost, but an unchecked dose can be auto-accepted). (2) Blank range -> "flag: no verified range" (safe, but every such medication needs a human). (3) Invent ranges: not allowed (no source).
- What we chose: (2), also for non-mass units (tablet, ml, sachet...), because lexicon ranges are in mg. Also: dose and unit must match the span; unit must be in the allowed set; dose x frequency must not exceed max daily; a dose inside the range but above the listed strengths passes with an "unusual" note (soft).
- Why: A wrong dose is a CRITICAL error. Cost we accept: with `data/lexicon/drugs.csv`, 12 of 34 rows have a blank range, so every dosed record for those drugs goes to a human (review burden).
- What we actually did: `checks.py::c4_dose_range`; config `blank_range_fails_closed: true`; tests `test_c4_*`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The lexicon has no "allowed units" column, so the allowed-unit list is a config default (contract gap, open_questions). The m1 lexicon gives paracetamol `1000-1000` mg, which flags a normal 500 mg tablet (lexicon question for m1/human).
- What else could be done: Add per-drug unit and per-dose-form columns to the lexicon; separate paediatric ranges.
- How to change this decision later: `config/default.yaml` (`blank_range_fails_closed`, `allowed_units`, `allowed_units_by_drug`), `checks.py`.
- Status: done

### D-51: C5 frequency and duration parse   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to check "times per day" and "number of days" against the span.
- Options we considered: (1) Any number in the span counts. (2) Parse phrases: number + ಬಾರಿ/ಸಲ with a per-day word (ದಿನಕ್ಕೆ), ಒಮ್ಮೆ, OD/BD/TDS; number + ದಿನ / ವಾರ (x7).
- What we chose: (2). Times-of-day words (ಬೆಳಿಗ್ಗೆ/ರಾತ್ರಿ) are NOT turned into a frequency: a record frequency based on them flags.
- Why: "ಮೂರು ದಿನದಿಂದ" (since three days) or "ದಿನಕ್ಕೆ" (per day) must not be read as a 3-day course.
- What we actually did: `lexicon.py::parse_frequencies/parse_durations`, `checks.py::c5_freq_duration`; tests.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Phrase list is AI-written, UNVERIFIED; "ವಾರಕ್ಕೆ ಎರಡು ಬಾರಿ" (twice a week) flags as "not per day".
- What else could be done: A native-reviewed phrase table from real prescriptions.
- How to change this decision later: `lexicon.py` (FREQ_MARK, PER_DAY, DAY_WORDS...).
- Status: done (phrases UNVERIFIED)

### D-52: C6 unsupported additions, omissions and the patient-link blind spot   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to catch values nobody said, drugs that were said but left out, and a record attached to the wrong patient.
- Options we considered: For patient links: (1) ignore; (2) check against an "encounter index" (which audio belongs to which patient) when one is given; (3) change the transcript contract to carry patient_ref (contract change, not ours to make).
- What we chose: Every drug, number and symptom in a value must occur in its span; English numbers/drugs in advice text must occur in the span; a lexicon drug said in the transcript but missing from the record is a "possible omission" (MAJOR) unless it sits inside an advice/follow-up span; patient link: option (2), and "n/a: blind spot" without an index.
- Why: transcript.v1 has no patient field, so text alone cannot detect a wrong patient. E4 shows it: 0/200 wrong-patient injections caught without the index, 200/200 with it.
- What we actually did: `checks.py::c6_unsupported`; CLI `--encounter-index`; tests `test_c6_*`; contract request in open_questions.md.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Symptom omissions are not checked (questions mention symptoms that are not findings). The index must come from a trusted source (m5 encounter table).
