- What else could be done: m5 passes the encounter index automatically.
- How to change this decision later: `checks.py::c6_unsupported`.
- Status: done (patient link needs integration)

### D-53: C7 ASR risk: threshold from DEV only, n/a when no confidences   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to warn about words the speech recogniser was unsure of, and what to do when it gives no confidence.
- Options we considered: (1) Flag every record with no confidence (huge review burden, no information). (2) Mark "n/a" and record it as a limitation. For the cutoff: (a) pick a number by hand; (b) tune it on DEV ASR output only.
- What we chose: (2) and (b). Critical words = drug, number, unit, negation cue. A second transcript (another ASR model or run), when given, is compared on drugs, doses, numbers, negation cues and bare answers. The cutoff 0.6 in `config/default.yaml` is an UNTUNED PLACEHOLDER until m2 produces DEV output; `python -m m4_verify tune-c7` does the tuning and refuses test paths.
- Why: Tuning on test data would leak (PROJECT_BRIEF 6.5).
- What we actually did: `checks.py::c7_asr_risk`, `tune_c7.py`, tests `test_c7_*`, `test_tune_c7.py`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Word confidences from Whisper-style models are often badly calibrated. C7 is the only check that can see an ASR-dropped negation, and only partly.
- What else could be done: Align the two transcripts word by word instead of comparing counts.
- How to change this decision later: `config/default.yaml` (`c7_conf_threshold`, `c7_threshold_status`), `checks.py`.
- Status: partly done (threshold UNTUNED)

### D-54: C8 ambiguity always flags   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: Which unclear situations should go to a human even when C3 agrees.
- Options we considered: (1) Let C3 decide everything. (2) A separate check that always flags known ambiguous patterns.
- What we chose: (2): soft cue; two cues in one clause; bare ಇಲ್ಲ with no resolvable question; bare answer after several questions or after a negative question; span inside a question nobody answered with yes/no; negation possibly shared across "A ಮತ್ತು B ಬೇಡ"; conflicting dose/frequency/duration values in one clause.
- Why: In these cases even a careful rule can be wrong; asking a human is cheaper than a negation flip.
- What we actually did: `checks.py::c8_ambiguity`, ambiguity notes from `negation.py`; tests `test_c8_*`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Adds review burden on real conversations (0/18 fixture fields flagged, but fixtures are few and clean).
- What else could be done: Measure C8's burden on m1 DEV data and narrow the rules that fire most.
- How to change this decision later: `checks.py::c8_ambiguity`, `negation.py`.
- Status: done

### D-55: Turn structure comes from newlines in the transcript text   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: Cross-turn negation needs to know where turns start, but transcript.v1 has no turn list.
- Options we considered: (1) Ask for a contract change (turn list in transcript.v1). (2) Use the existing convention: conversation text = turns joined by "\n" (gold.v1, fixtures/build_fixtures.py, contracts/validate.py::conversation_text).
- What we chose: (2), and logged (1) as an open question.
- Why: No contract change is needed now; gold and gold-style transcripts already follow it.
- What we actually did: `negation.py::_split_clauses` splits turns on "\n".
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Real ASR output on one audio file has no newlines: then the whole conversation is one turn and cross-turn answers are not resolved (a bare ಇಲ್ಲ inside one long line is simply a negation cue in its clause).
- What else could be done: Speaker diarisation in m2, or turn timestamps in transcript.v2.
- How to change this decision later: `negation.py` and a contract bump by the architect.
- Status: done (contract gap open)

### D-56: E4 planted-mistake generator design   (Phase B / m4_verify / safety-engineer / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to create >= 200 planted mistakes per type, honestly, when only 6 fixture conversations exist.
- Options we considered: (1) Only unique mutations (few per type). (2) 200 per type with repeats, reported with the number of UNIQUE mutations next to it. (3) Wait for m1 DEV gold.
- What we chose: (2) now, with a configurable gold source so it re-runs on m1 DEV gold (DEV only; any path with "test" is refused). Seed 20261109. Every injection is logged (id, type, field_path, before, after, note, unique_key). Record level tests the verifier; transcript level edits the transcript and rebuilds the record as a faithful extractor would, then is scored text-only and with a second (clean) transcript.
- Why: Repeating a mutation 50 times is not 50 independent trials; Wilson CIs are therefore also reported on unique mutations.
- What we actually did: `planted.py`, `evaluate.py`, `results/m4_verify/e4/`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Transcript-level edits use the verifier's own cue finder to locate what to delete (some circularity in where errors are planted). Word confidences were not simulated (inventing them would be circular), so C7's confidence path is not evaluated by E4.
- What else could be done: Re-run on m1 DEV gold (`mingw32-make m4-e4-data` after DEV gold exists, with `--gold-dir`); add natural ASR errors from m2.
- How to change this decision later: `planted.py` (TYPES, value lists), `evaluate.py`.
- Status: done on fixtures; DEV re-run pending

### D-57: Where the drug lexicon's facts come from, and the "no invented dose" rule   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: Which sources to trust for each column of `data/lexicon/drugs.csv` (34 drugs), especially dose ranges, which a safety check (m4 C4) will compare against.
- Options we considered:
  1. Write doses from memory. Pro: fast. Con: forbidden by the brief (an invented dose range could hide a real dosing error); not citable.
  2. India's NLEM 2022 only. Pro: Indian, official. Con: NLEM lists tablet strengths, not dose ranges.
  3. NLEM for strengths + WHO AWaRe portal for antibiotic class + official drug labels (openFDA, US) for dose ranges; blank + UNVERIFIED when nothing was fetched. Pro: every number has a fetched source. Con: US labels are not Indian practice.
- What we chose: Option 3.
- Why: A blank field that says UNVERIFIED is honest; a made-up number looks trustworthy and is dangerous. Dose range = lowest and highest single adult oral dose written in the label.
- What we actually did: `m1_data/lexicon_source.py` (facts + URLs), `m1_data/lexicon.py` (build), output `data/lexicon/drugs.csv`; 22 rows have a label dose range, 12 are blank (UNVERIFIED). Rebuild: `uv run --extra data python -m m1_data build-lexicon`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: NLEM rows were read by automated extraction of a 109-page PDF (four rows only by an LLM-based JSON extraction: ibuprofen, ondansetron, metformin, iron-folic acid); US label ranges do not match Indian practice (e.g. paracetamol 1000-1000 mg from a US OTC label, so a spoken "500 mg" is outside the range). UNVERIFIED until a human spot-checks.
- What else could be done: Use the Indian National Formulary or ICMR treatment guidelines for ranges; ask a pharmacist to review (clinical-reviewer agent is only simulated).
- How to change this decision later: edit `m1_data/lexicon_source.py`, rebuild, regenerate (`make data`; the test split will need `--refreeze` only if doses used in conversations change).
- Status: done

### D-58: Kannada spellings of drug names: rule-based transliteration instead of IndicXlit   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to write English drug names in Kannada script (with common spelling variants) without a native speaker.
- Options we considered:
  1. AI4Bharat IndicXlit (MIT, verified on GitHub). Pro: learned model, natural spellings. Con: needs fairseq + torch; fairseq is hard to install on Windows/Python 3.11; heavy for 34 words.
  2. `indic-transliteration` 2.3.82 (MIT, verified on PyPI): rule-based conversion of a phonetic Latin respelling (ISO 15919) to Kannada. Pro: tiny, deterministic, pinned. Con: the respelling (e.g. "amāksisilin") is written by an AI agent, so the spelling is only as good as that guess.
  3. Type Kannada spellings by hand. Pro: none over option 2. Con: not reproducible, typo-prone.
- What we chose: Option 2, with 2-4 hand-written variant respellings per drug (vowel length, aspiration, short forms like ಅಮಾಕ್ಸಿ).
- Why: Reproducible and light; the real risk (unnatural spelling) is the same for all options without a native reviewer.
- What we actually did: optional extra `data = ["indic-transliteration==2.3.82"]` in pyproject.toml (added under the uv mutex); `m1_data/lexicon.py::_iso_to_kn`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: spellings and variants are not native-validated; real spoken variants may be missing.
- What else could be done: run IndicXlit on Colab to produce top-k variants and merge them; collect variants from Kannada pharmacy web pages.
- How to change this decision later: change `iso` / `iso_variants` in `m1_data/lexicon_source.py` or replace `_iso_to_kn`.
- Status: done

### D-59: Templates as a small mini-language with computed gold spans and a negation self-check   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to produce >= 200 conversations whose gold labels and character offsets are exactly right.
- Options we considered:
  1. Write conversations and labels by hand. Pro: natural. Con: offsets are error-prone; does not scale.
  2. Let an LLM write conversations and labels. Pro: varied. Con: the brief forbids an LLM as ground truth; labels would be unverifiable.
  3. Templates with typed slots (`{D1.name}`, `{N1}` ...) and span markers (`[[m1|...]]`); the generator computes offsets while rendering and checks that the negation cues it finds (with the shared table) are exactly the ones the template declared.
- What we chose: Option 3: 59 templates, 9 categories, 22 families (`data/templates/`).
- Why: Gold is correct by construction and checked twice (offsets by contracts.validate, negation by the self-check).
- What we actually did: `m1_data/render.py`, `templates.py`, `generate.py`; sources in `data/templates/SOURCES.md`; `results/m1_data/unattested_tokens.json` lists template words NOT seen in any fetched source (164 of 221 Kannada words).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: sentences are AI-composed, repetitive and not native-validated; many words are unattested; some twins are semantically odd on purpose.
- What else could be done: native-speaker review sheet per template; add real-text-derived frames from more sources.
- How to change this decision later: edit `data/templates/*.yaml`; run `make data` (test templates changing requires `--refreeze`).
- Status: done

### D-60: How the dev/test split is cut (template families inside each category + dosed drug-dose combinations)   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to split dev/test "by template family and drug-dose combination" so near-duplicates never straddle.
- Options we considered:
  1. Random split of conversations. Con: near-duplicate sentences from one template in both splits (leakage); forbidden.
  2. Treat the 9 categories (a)-(i) as the families. Pro: strictest. Con: whole phenomena (e.g. cross-turn negation) would exist in only one split, so the test could not measure them.
  3. Several template families per category (22 in total, each a group of templates sharing sentence frames); half of each category's families (seeded) go to test. Dosed combinations (drug, value, unit) alternate dev/test per drug (seeded); bare drug mentions without a dose may appear in both.
- What we chose: Option 3.
- Why: No sentence frame and no dosed drug string is in both splits, while every phenomenon is in both.
- What we actually did: `m1_data/split.py`; `data/splits/{dev,test}.txt`, `split_plan.json`; tests `m1_data/tests/test_split.py` (no family / dosed combo in both splits; test = 264 conversations, 352 medication entries). Counts for categories e and g were raised from 8 to 16 per template to pass 300 test medication entries.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: drug NAMES (without dose) occur in both splits; families within one category still share vocabulary. This reading of "family" is my interpretation: open question for the orchestrator.
- What else could be done: also hold out whole drugs for test; or use categories as families for a second, stricter evaluation.
- How to change this decision later: `plan_families` / `plan_combos` in `m1_data/split.py`, then `python -m m1_data generate --refreeze` + a new decision entry.
- Status: done

### D-61: Freezing the test split with a hash lock that the generator refuses to break   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to make sure nobody silently edits test files or gold labels.
- Options we considered:
  1. Lock file only, checked in CI. Con: a regeneration would rewrite files and lock together.
  2. Lock file + generator guard: `make data` refuses to write a different `test.lock` unless `--refreeze` is given.
- What we chose: Option 2, same `<sha256>  <path>` format as `infra/scripts/check_split_lock.py`.
- Why: Accidental re-freezing becomes impossible; deliberate re-freezing leaves a visible trace.
- What we actually did: `data/splits/test.lock` (530 lines: 264 gold + 264 transcripts + twins_test.jsonl + test.txt); tests `m1_data/tests/test_split_lock.py`; root `make split-lock` already passes. Proof it fails: editing one label in a temp copy gives "data/gold/m1-a5-000.json: CHANGED since the test split was frozen", exit 1.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: the lock was created and regenerated (--refreeze) during this build before anything was committed; once committed, any change needs a decision entry.
- What else could be done: sign the lock; store it outside the repo.
- How to change this decision later: `python -m m1_data generate --refreeze` + decision entry.
- Status: done

### D-62: Gold convention: a medicine stopped before finishing is "negated"   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: What the gold record says for "ಕೋರ್ಸ್ ಮುಗಿಸದೆ ನಿಲ್ಲಿಸಿದೆವು" (stopped without finishing) and "ಇದು X ಅಲ್ಲ" (this is not X).
- Options we considered:
  1. negated: false (the drug was taken for a while). Con: the -ದೆ cue in the same clause would then contradict the record.
  2. negated: true meaning "not being taken as prescribed / not this drug".
- What we chose: Option 2, documented in docs/annotation_guide.md section 4.
- Why: It matches the clause-level negation rule the extractor and verifier use.
- What we actually did: templates c1-c5, e5.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: clinically, "stopped early" differs from "never taken"; the record schema cannot express it. Open question for the clinical reviewer.
- What else could be done: add an `adherence` field in a future record.v2.
- How to change this decision later: items in `data/templates/c_stopped_early.yaml`.
- Status: needs human

### D-63: Problems hit while building m1 data and how they were fixed   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-09)
- What we needed to decide: How to handle three problems found while testing.
- Options we considered: (a) Windows briefly locked a file during regeneration (WinError 32): fail, or retry; (b) the first tamper test passed for the wrong reason (Windows text mode rewrote line endings, the intended label edit did nothing): keep or fix; (c) the ORS row had no fetched source URL: drop ORS or cite the fetched Kannada ORS page for the name only.
- What we chose: (a) retry up to 5 s and only rewrite changed files; (b) the test now edits bytes and asserts the edit happened; (c) cite the Kannada ORS page for the name, dose stays UNVERIFIED.
- Why: Each fix makes a check honest instead of lucky.
- What we actually did: `m1_data/generate.py::_retry`, `m1_data/tests/test_split_lock.py`, `lexicon_source.py` (`name_source`).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: none known.
- What else could be done: run the tests on Linux CI too.
- How to change this decision later: same files.
- Status: done

### D-64: Accepting the "sub-family" reading of the dev/test split and freezing the test split   (Phase B / m1_data / orchestrator / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: The brief says "split by template family". The data builder made 9 categories (a to i, like "pharmacist dispensing") and, inside them, 22 smaller families of templates. Which of these is the "family" that must not appear in both dev and test?
- Options we considered: (1) Category = family. Pro: the strictest separation. Con: whole phenomena (for example cross-turn negation, category b) would exist only in dev or only in test, so the test could not measure them at all. (2) Sub-family = family, and every drug-dose combination also stays on one side. Pro: every phenomenon is tested; sentences that are near-copies of each other (same sub-family) never straddle the split. Con: dev and test share the broad sentence style of each category, so test scores may be a bit optimistic compared with brand-new kinds of sentences.
- What we chose: Option 2, as the builder proposed.
- Why: The point of the split is to stop near-duplicate sentences leaking from dev into test. Sub-families do that. Removing whole categories from test would make the main safety hypotheses (negation, doses) partly untestable. We accept that test sentences share a "style" with dev sentences and will say so in the report.
- What we actually did: Checked `uv run pytest m1_data/tests` (32 passed) and `mingw32-make split-lock` (OK). Committed data/ including data/splits/test.lock. From this commit on, the test split is FROZEN: any change to a test file or test gold label makes the split-lock check fail.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Test performance may overstate how well the system handles sentence types it has never seen. All Kannada text is not native-validated. Audio will be added later (task B1b); that pass may only ADD lines to test.lock.
- What else could be done: Hold out one entire category as an extra "unseen category" stress test and report it separately.
- How to change this decision later: data/splits/split_plan.json and m1_data's split code; any re-split must be a new, documented refreeze BEFORE the first `make eval-test` run, never after.
- Status: done

### D-65: Using a cheaper AI model for routine reviews and fix passes   (Phase B / orchestration / orchestrator / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: The free usage allowance ran out twice (each "window" is about 5 hours). About 5-6 more windows of work were estimated. Which AI model should do which job so the allowance lasts longer?
- Options we considered: (1) Strongest model (Opus) for everything, as the brief asks for reviewers. Pro: deepest reviews. Con: uses the allowance fastest; the project takes more days. (2) Cheaper model (Sonnet) for routine reviews and fix passes; strongest model kept for the safety verifier (m4) and the two auditors (scientific-integrity-auditor, security-auditor). Pro: each window does more work; the most safety-critical judgement stays with the strongest model. Con: the cheaper Kannada and clinical reviews may catch fewer problems. (3) Cheaper model for everything. Pro: fastest. Con: weaker safety checks and audits; not acceptable for the scientific core.
- What we chose: Option 2. The student asked for this on 2026-11-10.
- Why: Saving usage matters, but the verifier and the audits are where a missed problem would change the scientific result, so they keep the strongest model. This is a deviation from the brief, which asked for the strongest model for all reviewers.
- What we actually did: From now on: Sonnet for kannada-language-reviewer, clinical-reviewer and every fix pass (except m4); Opus for m4_verify work, scientific-integrity-auditor and security-auditor. Builders keep the model they started with when they are resumed. Each review report's file says which model wrote it.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The Kannada and clinical reviews are already SIMULATED (ai_simulated_review), and a cheaper simulation may miss more. This makes the real human review sheets (HUMAN_TODO item b) even more important.
- What else could be done: Re-run the Kannada and clinical reviews with the strongest model at the end, if usage remains.
- How to change this decision later: Pass a different `model` when starting the agent; the `model:` line in .claude/agents/<name>.md sets the default.
- Status: done

### D-66: Build the review app in Streamlit with our own palette, CSS and bundled font   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: Which tool to build the review screens with, and how to stop it looking like a default template.
- Options we considered:
  1. Streamlit with custom CSS (the brief's default). Pro: already a base dependency, pure Python, has a built-in test tool (AppTest). Con: limited control over layout; some styling depends on Streamlit's internal HTML names, which can change.
  2. Gradio. Pro: quick demos. Con: not the default, weaker page structure for a multi-screen app, no reason found that Streamlit cannot do the job.
  3. A hand-written web page (HTML + JavaScript) on FastAPI. Pro: full control. Con: much more code, more security work, slower to build and test.
- What we chose: Option 1, Streamlit 1.67 with a theme in `m6_ui/.streamlit/config.toml` plus a small CSS file.
- Why: It runs today with no new packages and AppTest lets us test the important behaviours automatically. We accepted that the CSS hooks into Streamlit's internal class names and may need fixing after an upgrade.
- What we actually did: `m6_ui/app.py`, `m6_ui/theme.py` (palette = single source of colours, WCAG contrast maths), `m6_ui/static/style.css`, `m6_ui/.streamlit/config.toml` (baseFontSize 18, minimal toolbar, no usage stats). Run `mingw32-make m6-run`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: CSS selectors such as `.stButton button` are not a public API. The look at 360 px was NOT confirmed on a real phone (headless screenshots of the live app came out blank; see the screenshot entry).
- What else could be done: pin the Streamlit version in uv.lock and add a visual regression test with a real browser (Playwright) when one is available.
- How to change this decision later: replace `app.py`; the logic in `review.py`, `highlight.py`, `gate.py`, `verifier.py` has no Streamlit code and can be reused.
- Status: done

### D-67: Flagged items first, never colour alone, reasons always visible   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to show the verifier's flags so a busy health worker cannot miss them.
- Options we considered:
  1. One list in record order with a red mark on flagged items. Pro: simple. Con: a flag at the bottom can be missed; colour alone fails for colour-blind users and in sunlight.
  2. Flagged items first, each with red colour AND a "!" icon AND a text label ("CHECK THIS"), and the reason shown directly (not behind a click). Pro: hard to miss. Con: longer screen.
  3. A separate "problems" tab. Pro: tidy. Con: hides flags behind a click (forbidden by the brief).
- What we chose: Option 2. Rows: one per symptom, per advice item, per follow-up, and one per medication attribute (drug, dose, times per day, days, take/do-not-take). A flag that does not fit any row goes on a "Whole record" row at the very top, so it is never dropped. A missing verdict or an empty check list becomes a fail-closed flag.
- Why: The app's one job is to make a dangerous extraction error impossible to miss. Each flag shows the check's plain reason in BOTH Kannada and English plus the verifier's raw English message.
- What we actually did: `m6_ui/review.py` (`build_rows`, `flags_from_verdict`), `_row_head` in `app.py`; tests `test_review.py`, `test_app.py::test_flagged_fields_are_rendered_first_with_colour_icon_and_label`, `::test_flag_reasons_are_visible_without_a_click_in_both_languages`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The plain-language reasons per check id (C1..C8) are our own wording, not native-validated, and the Kannada wording may be unclear to ASHA workers.
- What else could be done: test the wording with real users; add a short spoken explanation (TTS) for low-literacy users.
- How to change this decision later: `review.py` (row granularity), `i18n/*.json` (`reason.C1`..`reason.C8`).
- Status: done

### D-68: Approve stays locked until every flag is answered; the server decides "saved"   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: When the Approve button is allowed, and who decides that a record is saved.
- Options we considered:
  1. Allow Approve any time and rely on the server. Pro: simple. Con: invites click-through; the brief forbids it.
  2. Lock Approve until every flag is individually answered ("Yes, this is correct" or "No, change it"), then call the server gate and show its answer. Pro: matches the brief; the server stays the authority. Con: more taps.
- What we chose: Option 2. A flag is identified by check id + field path + reason, so a NEW flag created by a re-check after an edit is not counted as answered. The approve callback checks the rule again (it does not trust the disabled button). Approve calls `approve` then `save` on the gate; any refusal is shown in red with icon, title, reason, the server's own message and "Record status: NOT saved". Records with no flags still need a human click (never auto-approved).
- Why: Nothing may be saved without a human decision, and the server, not the browser, enforces the final rule.
- What we actually did: `review.approve_enabled`, `review.mark_row`, `app._approve`, `app._gate_result`; tests `test_app.py::test_approve_disabled_until_every_flag_is_individually_resolved`, `::test_server_refusal_is_shown_and_record_is_not_saved`, `::test_forced_server_refusal_is_handled`, `::test_clean_record_saves_only_after_click`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: With m5's documented rule (save refused while any check flags), a human "Yes, this is correct" can NEVER lead to "saved" while the verifier still flags. The UI shows this refusal honestly, but the workflow has no "human override with reason" yet (open question).
- What else could be done: a contract change (for example a `review.v1` document with the reviewer's answer per flag) so m5 can accept a confirmed flag with an audit entry.
- How to change this decision later: `review.py`, `gate.py`, and (with the architect) contracts/m5.
- Status: partly done (needs a human/architect decision on override)

### D-69: Server-side gate client: mock that mirrors m5's rules, HTTP client for live mode   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How the UI talks to the save gate without importing m5 code and while m5 may not be running.
- Options we considered:
  1. Import m5's FastAPI app. Con: forbidden (no cross-module imports).
  2. A `MockGate` that copies the rules written in m5_backend/CONTRACT.md, plus an `HttpGate` for the real API. Pro: works alone; same interface. Con: the mock can drift from the real server.
- What we chose: Option 2. MockGate refuses: no approval, invalid verdict, record_id mismatch (422), empty checks / auto_acceptable false / any flag (409); `force_refuse` (env `M6_MOCK_GATE_REFUSE=1` or a checkbox in mock mode) refuses every save for tests and demos. HttpGate logs in with `POST /auth/token`, keeps the token in server-side memory and sends it only in the `Authorization` header; it accepts only loopback URLs; it maps 409/422/401/403/404/network errors to labelled refusals.
- Why: The UI must be testable alone and must handle refusal visibly.
- What we actually did: `m6_ui/gate.py`; `test_gate.py` (HttpGate tested with `httpx.MockTransport`; asserts the token never appears in a URL).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Live mode was NOT run against a real m5 + PostgreSQL (UNVERIFIED). Live mode also needs a map from record.v1 `record_id` to m5's database UUID (`M6_SERVER_IDS` JSON file), which is a stop-gap.
- What else could be done: an integration test in Phase C with m5's database running.
- How to change this decision later: `m6_ui/gate.py` (`make_gate`, status mapping).
- Status: partly done (live UNVERIFIED)

### D-70: Edits re-run the verifier: mock stand-in in mock mode, m4 command line in live mode   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How "edits re-run the verifier" works without importing m4.
- Options we considered:
  1. Import m4_verify. Con: forbidden.
  2. Live: run `uv run python -m m4_verify verify --record R --transcript T --out O` as a subprocess (fixed argument list, no shell, timeout). Mock: a small stand-in. Pro: keeps modules independent. Con: subprocess is slow (seconds); the stand-in is not the real logic.
  3. Do not re-check at all. Con: violates the brief.
- What we chose: Option 2. The stand-in clears the original flags only on the exact field the reviewer edited, then re-checks the new value against its evidence span (digits, Kannada digits converted to ASCII, a starter list of Kannada number words from `m6_ui/number_words.json`; units; text), re-checks every span against the transcript, and ALWAYS flags a yes/no (negation) edit for confirmation. Any subprocess failure, timeout or invalid output gives a fail-closed verdict with a `check_error` flag.
- Why: The reviewer sees immediately whether the new value is supported by what was said, and a broken verifier can never silently pass a record.
- What we actually did: `m6_ui/verifier.py`; `test_verifier.py`; `test_app.py::test_edit_reruns_verifier_and_new_flag_must_be_resolved` (editing 50 mg to 500 mg on the ASR-error fixture clears C4/C7 but raises a new C2 flag, because the ASR transcript says 50; the reviewer must confirm it).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The stand-in is NOT m4's logic and the number words are not native-validated. The live subprocess path was tested only with a fake command, not with real m4 (UNVERIFIED).
- What else could be done: run m4 in live mode in Phase C integration; cache a long-running verifier process instead of starting `uv` per edit.
- How to change this decision later: `m6_ui/verifier.py` (`make_verifier`, `SubprocessVerifier.cmd_prefix`).
- Status: done (live path UNVERIFIED)

### D-71: Escape all transcript text before adding highlight markup (XSS)   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to highlight evidence spans safely, since transcripts and records are untrusted text.
- Options we considered:
  1. Insert `<mark>` tags into the raw text, then render as HTML. Con: any `<script>` or `<img onerror>` in the text becomes live code (cross-site scripting, XSS).
  2. Cut the text at span boundaries, escape EVERY piece with `html.escape(quote=True)`, then add our own fixed tags. Pro: data can never become markup. Con: slightly more code.
- What we chose: Option 2, and every other data value (field values, reasons, card text, file contents) also goes through `esc()` before it touches HTML. Data is never passed to `st.markdown`.
- Why: The brief requires it, and Streamlit's own HTML sanitising is a second layer, not the first.
- What we actually did: `m6_ui/highlight.py`; security fixture `m6_ui/fixtures/{transcript,record,verdict}/` with `<script>`, `<img onerror>`, `<b onmouseover>` payloads in the transcript, drug name, advice, patient_ref and verdict reasons; tests `test_highlight.py`, `test_app.py::test_xss_payload_in_transcript_and_fields_renders_inert`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: A future developer could add an unescaped f-string. The test covers the screens that exist today only.
- What else could be done: a lint rule or test that every `st.html` call receives output of an escaping helper.
- How to change this decision later: `m6_ui/highlight.py`, `esc()` calls in `app.py`.
- Status: done

### D-72: Every UI string in versioned i18n files; Kannada is the default language   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: Where UI text lives and how the Kannada/English toggle works.
- Options we considered:
  1. Strings in the Python code. Con: forbidden; impossible to review.
  2. `m6_ui/i18n/kn.json` and `en.json` with identical keys and a `_meta.version`; a missing key raises an error (no silent English fallback). Two big language buttons at the top of every screen.
- What we chose: Option 2, Kannada first.
- Why: Users read Kannada more easily; reviewers can check one file.
- What we actually did: `m6_ui/i18n.py`, `i18n/{kn,en}.json` (version 1.0.0, `native_validated: false`); `test_i18n.py` greps every `m6_ui/**/*.py` for Kannada characters U+0C80..U+0CFF, checks identical keys, identical `{placeholders}`, and that every `t("...")` key used in code exists; `test_app.py::test_role_picker_is_first_and_language_toggle_switches_every_label`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: ALL Kannada strings are AI-drafted and NOT native-validated; some words (for example the transliterated "ಆ್ಯಪ್", "ಸರ್ವರ್") may be unnatural. Data text (drug names, card topics, verifier messages) is shown as it is and may be English.
- What else could be done: a native speaker reviews `kn.json` (HUMAN_TODO).
- How to change this decision later: edit both JSON files together and bump `_meta.version`.
- Status: needs human

### D-73: Bundle Noto Sans Kannada v2.006 (slim variable font, SIL OFL 1.1) locally   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: Which Kannada font to ship and how to load it without a CDN.
- Options we considered:
  1. Rely on the phone's system font. Con: older/cheap phones may lack good Kannada shaping; inconsistent look.
  2. Load from Google Fonts at runtime. Con: needs internet, leaks usage to a third party, brief says local.
  3. Bundle the official notofonts release. Pro: offline, consistent. Con: ~210 KB to download.
- What we chose: Option 3: `NotoSansKannada[wght].ttf` from the "full/slim-variable-ttf" folder of release NotoSansKannada-v2.006 (one file, weights 100-900, 210,928 bytes), renamed `m6_ui/static/fonts/NotoSansKannada-VF.ttf`, served by Streamlit static serving and declared both in `config.toml` (`theme.fontFaces`) and by `@font-face` in the CSS.
- Why: One small file covers every weight; the licence allows bundling.
- What we actually did: fetched on 2026-11-10 `https://api.github.com/repos/notofonts/kannada/releases` and `https://github.com/notofonts/kannada/releases/download/NotoSansKannada-v2.006/NotoSansKannada-v2.006.zip` (sha256 `902b1b92018f7c96862d68d0a39ef03b978203cd4c3f55d6760f597657b51c44`); font sha256 `b062c3ec551cee95b88cbbc470036c81b69c788516fd8910ba6ae6e3c70d9bb2`; licence text `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/notofonts/kannada/main/OFL.txt` (SIL Open Font License 1.1, copy in `static/fonts/OFL.txt`); GitHub licence API reports `OFL-1.1`. `test_design.py` checks the hash, the licence text and (with fontTools) that every Kannada character in `kn.json` and `conjuncts.json` is in the font.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Variable fonts are widely supported but very old Android WebViews may not support them (UNVERIFIED).
- What else could be done: also ship static Regular + Bold as a fallback.
- How to change this decision later: replace the file in `m6_ui/static/fonts/`, update `FONT_SHA256` in `test_design.py`, `config.toml` and the licence row.
- Status: done

### D-74: Conjunct rendering test page, checked by eye only in headless Edge on Windows   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to check that Kannada conjuncts (joined consonants) draw correctly.
- Options we considered:
  1. Automated tests only. Con: tests can check strings and font coverage, not how letters are drawn.
  2. A test page plus a real screenshot looked at by eye. Pro: catches dotted circles and broken joins. Con: needs a browser per platform.
- What we chose: Both: a "Letter test" screen in the app and a standalone `results/m6_ui/conjunct_test_page.html` (written by `make m6-mock`) listing 17 items (ಕ್ಷ, ಜ್ಞ, ತ್ರ, ಶ್ರೀ, ಸ್ತ್ರೀ, ರ್ಕ, ಕ್ಕೆ, ಳ್ಳ, ದ್ವ, drug words, two ZWNJ cases, Kannada digits) with their code points.
- Why: Rendering is a real risk for Kannada and cannot be proven by unit tests.
- What we actually did: headless Microsoft Edge 157 on Windows 11 screenshot `results/m6_ui/screenshot_conjuncts_edge.png`; looked at by the AI agent: the conjuncts appear joined, no dotted circles, ರ್ಕ shows the raised repha form. We could not prove the bundled font (not the Windows system font) was the one used.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Android and Linux rendering is UNVERIFIED; the AI agent is not a native reader; the expected look of the ZWNJ cases is UNVERIFIED.
- What else could be done: a native reader checks the page on a cheap Android phone and on Linux (HUMAN_TODO).
- How to change this decision later: edit `m6_ui/conjuncts.json`.
- Status: needs human

### D-75: Synthetic tone WAV for "play this part"; real recording state is Streamlit's own widget   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to test "play this part" when fixtures have no real audio, and how to show the recording state.
- Options we considered:
  1. Real Kannada speech audio. Con: we have none in fixtures; data rules.
  2. A synthetic 12 s, 8 kHz tone with a beep every second (stdlib `wave`, deterministic), cut to `audio_start..audio_end`. Pro: tiny (192 KB), reproducible, testable (clip length is checked). Con: not speech.
- What we chose: Option 2; "No timing available for this part" when either time is null. For recording we use `st.audio_input` (its own red recording indicator) plus our status panel ("No audio yet" / "Audio received (N seconds)") and a file upload.
- Why: Lets the cut logic be tested without any speech data.
- What we actually did: `m6_ui/audio.py` (`make_tone_wav`, `cut_wav`), `m6_ui/fixtures/audio/tone_fx.wav`; tests `test_audio.py`, `test_app.py::test_play_this_part_cuts_audio_or_says_no_timing`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Our app cannot see the "recording in progress" moment (it happens in the browser widget); how that widget behaves on cheap Android phones is UNVERIFIED. In mock mode, recorded audio is not transcribed (said on screen).
- What else could be done: connect m2_asr in live mode in Phase C.
- How to change this decision later: `m6_ui/audio.py`, `_page_record` and `_player` in `app.py`.
- Status: done

### D-76: Counselling tab is a placeholder on fixture cards with a clear "ask a doctor" state   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: What screen 5 shows while m8_helper does not exist.
- Options we considered:
  1. Hide the tab. Con: the "ask a doctor" state would not be designed or tested.
  2. Show fixture cards as big topic buttons, a big "Not sure / my question is not here" button that opens a red ask-a-doctor panel, and a voice input next to them that says voice needs m8.
- What we chose: Option 2. Each card shows its Kannada and English text, its status ("machine-drafted, not checked by a native speaker") and "Not checked by a doctor".
- Why: The safe fallback ("ask a doctor") must be obvious; we do not answer questions ourselves.
- What we actually did: `_page_help` in `app.py`; tests `test_app.py::test_counselling_placeholder_and_ask_a_doctor_state`, `::test_counselling_card_shows_not_validated_status`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Card topics are English data; not a real helper.
- What else could be done: call m8 when it publishes an interface.
- How to change this decision later: `_page_help` in `app.py`.
- Status: partly done (waits for m8)

### D-77: "Where checks failed" panel reads real E4 misses from m4's published results   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: Which failure cases screen 6 shows.
- Options we considered:
  1. A hand-made list. Con: not real.
  2. Read published result files: any `results/*/failure*.md`, and the rows of `results/m4_verify/e4/catch_rates.csv` where caught < n, with up to 2 example injections each; fall back to a hand-made list marked "Example only (not measured)".
- What we chose: Option 2. Today it shows real misses such as "transcript: negation drop, text_only, caught 0 of 200" and "record: wrong patient_ref, no_encounter_index", plus the known blind spot sentence.
- Why: Users should see where the green badge can be wrong.
- What we actually did: `m6_ui/failures.py`; `test_failures.py`, `test_app.py::test_failure_panel_lists_cases`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: These are planted mistakes on synthetic fixture text, not real-world error rates; the numbers are m4's, not re-checked here.
- What else could be done: read m7_eval's failure report when it exists.
- How to change this decision later: `m6_ui/failures.py`.
- Status: done

### D-78: Bind to 127.0.0.1 only; no tokens in URLs; Streamlit chrome hidden   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to keep the app private and simple.
- Options we considered:
  1. Defaults. Con: Streamlit may listen on all network interfaces and shows developer menus.
  2. `server.address = "127.0.0.1"` in `m6_ui/.streamlit/config.toml` AND `--server.address 127.0.0.1` in `make m6-run`; the app refuses to run if started with another address; XSRF protection on; usage statistics off; toolbar "minimal"; error details hidden.
- What we chose: Option 2. Demo start settings are environment variables (`M6_DEMO_*`, mock only), never URL parameters.
- Why: Brief section 2.4 and AGENT 7 security rules.
- What we actually did: started the server on 2026-11-10: `netstat` showed `LISTENING 127.0.0.1:8517` only; `/_stcore/health` 200; the bundled font served at `/app/static/fonts/NotoSansKannada-VF.ttf` (200, 210,928 bytes). `test_design.py::test_server_binds_to_loopback_and_hides_chrome`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Streamlit reads `m6_ui/.streamlit/config.toml` only when started from inside `m6_ui/` (the make target does this; the command-line flag covers the address either way).
- What else could be done: a reverse proxy with authentication if this ever leaves the laptop (out of scope).
- How to change this decision later: `m6_ui/.streamlit/config.toml`, `m6_ui/module.mk`.
- Status: done

### D-79: Headless screenshots of the live Streamlit app came out blank   (Phase B / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to show proof of the 360 px layout.
- Options we considered:
  1. Headless Microsoft Edge `--screenshot` at 360 px width. Pro: no new tools. Con: Streamlit draws its page with JavaScript over a websocket; headless capture may happen before that.
  2. Playwright. Con: a new dependency plus a browser download (disk is short).
  3. Check the layout by hand on a phone.
- What we chose: Tried option 1; the screenshot showed only the page background (the theme colour), so the layout is UNVERIFIED. Option 3 goes to HUMAN_TODO.
- Why: We do not claim what we could not see.
- What we actually did: `results/m6_ui/screenshot_app_360px_review_xss.png` (blank apart from background, kept as evidence of the attempt). Layout rules are tested only as CSS rules in `test_design.py` (font sizes >= 18 px, min-height >= 48 px, max-width 100%).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: real overflow at 360 px may exist.
- What else could be done: Playwright with a waiting step, or Chrome DevTools device mode by a human.
- How to change this decision later: add a screenshot script under `m6_ui/`.
- Status: needs human

### D-80: A flag the reviewer confirmed must count as "resolved" when saving   (Phase B / m5_backend + m6_ui / orchestrator / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: The UI (m6) lets a reviewer answer each flag with "Yes, this is correct" or by editing the field. But the server (m5) refuses to save any record whose verdict still contains a flag. So a record where the reviewer looked at a flag and confirmed it can never be saved. Who is right?
- Options we considered: (1) Keep the server as it is. Pro: strictest. Con: the reviewer must invent an edit to get past a flag that was actually fine; that teaches people to make pointless edits and breaks the brief's "confirmed OR edited" rule. (2) The save request carries a list of resolutions (which check, which field, "confirmed" or "edited", by whom). The server accepts the save only if EVERY flag in the latest verdict has a matching resolution from the approving physician, and writes each one to the audit log. Pro: matches the brief; every override leaves a trace. Con: needs a change in m5's API and tests. (3) Let the UI drop flags before saving. Pro: easy. Con: the UI could be bypassed; the safety rule must live on the server.
- What we chose: Option 2, to be built in the next m5 fix pass (after the security audit). This is an API change, not a contract change: record.v1 and verdict.v1 stay the same.
- Why: The brief says Approve unlocks when every flagged field is "individually confirmed or edited", and that the server enforces the gate. A confirmed flag is a human decision, so it must be allowed and recorded, not hidden.
- What we actually did: Logged it in m6_ui/handoff/open_questions.md (by the m6 builder) and here. Until it is built, the UI shows the server's refusal honestly ("NOT saved").
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Reviewers may click "confirm" without really checking (automation bias). The E7 user-study kit is designed to measure exactly this.
- What else could be done: Require a short reason or a "play the audio" step before a critical flag can be confirmed.
- How to change this decision later: m5_backend save endpoint and its tests; m6_ui gate client.
- Status: not done (planned for the m5 fix pass)

### D-81: Rules first: a hand-written rules pipeline is the MAIN extractor   (Phase B / m3_extract / nlp-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to turn a Kannada transcript into a structured record (symptoms, medicines with dose / frequency / duration, advice, follow-up) where every value points at the words that support it.
- Options we considered:
  1. Hand-written rules with word lists. Pro: same input always gives the same output, every decision can be logged and explained, runs in milliseconds, $0. Con: only knows the words and patterns we wrote, so new wording is missed; needs Kannada knowledge we do not have natively.
  2. A local LLM as the main extractor. Pro: copes with new wording. Con: slow on our laptop (30-60 s per conversation), can invent values, harder to explain; must still be checked.
  3. Train a small tagger. Con: we have no real labelled Kannada clinical data; training on our own synthetic templates would learn the templates.
- What we chose: Option 1 as MAIN (the brief requires it); option 2 as the E2 comparison.
- Why: A safety experiment needs a predictable extractor whose mistakes we can list and reproduce. Missing something (an omission) is visible to the reviewer; inventing something is worse.
- What we actually did: `m3_extract/rules.py` with `textnorm.py`, `lexicon.py`, `numbers.py`, `units.py`, `negation.py`, word lists in `m3_extract/data/`. Every record goes through the evidence check and record.v1 before it is saved. Run: `mingw32-make m3-run-dev`. Output: `results/m3_extract/rules/*.json`, logs in `results/m3_extract/rules/logs/`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The rules were written by reading synthetic template conversations; they will miss real speech, other dialects and wording not in the lists. Kannada lists are not native-validated.
- What else could be done: Grow the lists with real reviewers; add morphology (inflected forms like ಜ್ವರವಿದೆ); use the LLM only to PROPOSE items that the rules then verify.
- How to change this decision later: `m3_extract/rules.py`, `m3_extract/data/*.yaml`.
- Status: done

### D-82: Keep a "dev_holdout" inside DEV to measure over-fitting of hand-written rules   (Phase B / m3_extract / nlp-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to give an honest dev score for rules that were written while looking at dev conversations.
- Options we considered:
  1. Report the score on all of dev. Pro: simple. Con: the rules were written from these examples, so the score is "in-sample" and too optimistic.
  2. Before writing any rule, set aside some dev templates and never look at them while writing rules. Pro: an honest estimate of how the rules handle unseen wording, without touching the frozen test split. Con: fewer examples to learn from; still synthetic.
  3. Use the test split. Not allowed (frozen, section 6.5).
- What we chose: Option 2. One template per category, chosen with `random.Random(20261110)`: a3 b4 c5 d2 e6 f3 g5 h6 i3 (88 conversations). The other 240 are "dev_tune".
- Why: Hand-written rules over-fit easily; this exposes it cheaply.
- What we actually did: `HOLDOUT_TEMPLATES` in `m3_extract/splits.py`; every score row in `results/m3_extract/dev_scores.csv` has a `dev_part` slice. The holdout was first scored on 2026-11-10 13:32 (+05:30) with frozen rules v1.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Holdout templates share vocabulary and sentence styles with dev_tune because one generator wrote all of them, so even the holdout score is optimistic for real speech.
- What else could be done: Real recorded conversations; a second holdout created later by m1.
- How to change this decision later: `m3_extract/splits.py` (`HOLDOUT_TEMPLATES`).
- Status: done

### D-83: Rules v2: fix "bare ಇಲ್ಲ answers the whole question sentence" (found on dev_holdout)   (Phase B / m3_extract / nlp-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: What to do after the first holdout score showed 7 CRITICAL negation flips, all in template c5: "ನನಗೆ ಜ್ವರ ಇದೆ, ಮನೆಯಲ್ಲಿ ಇದ್ದ X ತೆಗೆದುಕೊಳ್ಳಬಹುದಾ? / ಇಲ್ಲ." ("I have fever, can I take X? / No."). Rules v1 applied "No" to fever too, recording fever as ABSENT.
- Options we considered:
  1. Leave the bug and only report it. Pro: the holdout stays clean. Con: the MAIN pipeline would knowingly keep a negation-flip bug that downstream modules use.
  2. Fix it, keep the v1 scores as the honest holdout estimate, and label the v2 holdout score as contaminated. Pro: better pipeline and an honest record. Con: the v2 holdout number is no longer an unseen-data estimate.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: A negation flip is the most dangerous error class; the fix is general (a comma clause ending in ಇದೆ-type words is a statement, not part of the question), not specific to c5.
- What we actually did: `_is_question` in `m3_extract/rules.py`; tests `test_statement_clause_inside_question_sentence_is_not_answered` and `test_listed_nouns_in_question_stay_question`. v1 scores: `results/m3_extract/rules_v1_frozen/`. Version bumped to 0.2.0. Missed advice / follow-up phrases on the holdout were NOT added (that would just copy the holdout).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Comma clauses ending in other statement verbs (not in our short list) are still treated as part of a question.
- What else could be done: Use punctuation plus a list of question clitics (-ಆ, -ಾ?) per clause; ask a native speaker.
- How to change this decision later: `EXIST_WORDS` and `_is_question` in `m3_extract/rules.py`.
- Status: done

### D-84: Negation flag uses ONLY the shared table, with three documented interpretations   (Phase B / m3_extract / nlp-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How the rules pipeline decides "negated / absent", given that m4_verify must check it independently.
- Options we considered:
  1. Read `.claude/skills/kannada-nlp/negation_table.yaml` and apply it per token and per clause. Pro: one shared, reviewable list; m4 writes its own rules so the check is not circular. Con: inherits the table's gaps.
  2. Write m3's own extra cues. Con: two hidden rule sets; against the brief.
- What we chose: Option 1, with three interpretations: (a) NEG-S03 "-ದಿದ್ದರೆ (if not)" marks a CONDITIONAL, it never sets `negated` on an item; (b) a soft cue (casual -ಲ್ಲ) on a stem that is not in `verb_stems` still negates but is logged as `soft_negation`; (c) the punctuation splitter skips abbreviation dots (ಎಂ.ಜಿ, ಒ.ಆರ್.ಎಸ್).
- Why: (a) "if the fever does not come down, come back" does not deny any symptom; (b) treating a probable "not" as "yes" is the more dangerous mistake; (c) otherwise "1000 ಎಂ.ಜಿ ಬೇಡ" would put the dose and the "no" in different clauses.
- What we actually did: `m3_extract/negation.py`; tests in `m3_extract/tests/test_negation.py` (15 cue / minimal-pair twins, 9 traps, clause scope, cross-turn). No import of m4 code.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Every table entry is a hypothesis (not native-validated). ಇಲ್ಲದೆ ("without") is not in the table at all.
- What else could be done: Add `type: conditional_negative` and an abbreviation rule to the table (open question).
- How to change this decision later: the table (with a decision entry) and `m3_extract/negation.py`.
- Status: done

### D-85: Evidence = smallest supporting span; the evidence check rejects the whole record   (Phase B / m3_extract / nlp-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: What span to quote for each value and what to do when a quote is wrong.
- Options we considered:
  1. Quote the whole sentence. Pro: easy. Con: a reviewer cannot see which words support the dose; a wrong value hides inside a long quote.
  2. Quote the smallest span: drug name through the last dose / frequency / duration word, plus the "not" word if in the same clause; for a cross-turn answer, the item in the QUESTION turn (annotation guide). Pro: precise. Con: more code.
  3. On a failing quote, repair it (fuzzy search). Con: hides model or code errors.
- What we chose: Option 2, and reject (never repair) any record with a failing span.
- Why: The verifier (m4) and the human reviewer rely on spans; a record with one fake quote cannot be trusted at all.
- What we actually did: `m3_extract/evidence.py` (`check_evidence`, `evidence_for`, `locate_quote`). Rejected records go to `results/m3_extract/<pipeline>/rejected/` with reasons and are scored as empty records. Tests: `test_forged_span_text_is_rejected`, `test_forged_offsets_are_rejected`, `test_forged_quote_rejects_whole_record`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Our spans start at the drug name, gold spans sometimes start earlier (ಇದು ...); we report overlap with gold spans, not exact equality (`evidence_overlap_dev.csv`).
- What else could be done: Score span precision / recall in characters.
- How to change this decision later: `m3_extract/evidence.py`, span logic in `m3_extract/rules.py`.
- Status: done

### D-86: Scoring: what a "field" is and how advice is classed   (Phase B / m3_extract / nlp-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to count fields and errors for "errors per 1,000 fields", and which class to give advice errors that docs/severity.md does not list.
- Options we considered:
  1. Medication 6 fields, symptom 2, advice 2, follow-up 1; a missed item = 1 error; an extra item = +1 field and 1 error. Pro: simple, every gold value counted once. Con: a missed medication with 5 values counts as 1 error.
  2. Count every field of a missed item as an error. Con: double counts (severity.md: a missed drug is ONE major error).
- What we chose: Option 1. Missed / extra advice and follow-up are MAJOR; advice text that differs from gold is MA-WORDING-UNJUDGED (MAJOR) because "same meaning" cannot be judged by code (severity rule 5). patient_ref / encounter_type are supplied context, not extracted, so not scored.
- Why: Follows the "one field, one class" rule and the "when unsure, MAJOR" rule.
- What we actually did: `m3_extract/scoring.py` (Wilson CI with hand-checked values 0/50 -> [0, 0.0713], 3/50 -> [0.0206, 0.1622] in `tests/test_scoring.py`). Outputs `results/m3_extract/dev_scores.csv` (sliced by dev part, category, code_switched, has_negation, cross_turn_negation).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Other modules may count fields differently (open question). The LLM writes its own English glosses, so it gets many MA-WORDING-UNJUDGED errors that a human might call "same meaning".
- What else could be done: Human judgement of advice wording on a sample; a shared scorer in m7_eval.
- How to change this decision later: `m3_extract/scoring.py`.
- Status: done (needs architect / m7 confirmation)

### D-87: LLM comparison (E2) with qwen3:4b-instruct on a seeded 40-conversation dev subset   (Phase B / m3_extract / nlp-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: Which local open LLM to compare with the rules, how to make it reproducible, and on how many conversations.
- Options we considered:
  1. qwen3:4b-instruct through Ollama (already installed, 2.5 GB Q4_K_M). Pro: $0, local, supports JSON-schema output. Con: does not fit fully in the 4 GB GPU (Ollama ran it 45% CPU / 55% GPU), so it is slow; a 4B model is weak at Kannada.
  2. A larger model (7-8B). Con: would not fit in 4 GB VRAM; much slower on CPU; extra download on a nearly full disk.
  3. A Hugging Face transformers model. Con: new heavy dependency, same memory limits.
- What we chose: Option 1. Revision = Ollama digest 0edcdef34593eac1aa2be9c7d06c432dcf81945adca5eca2f27662c18f168ba0; temperature 0, top_k 1, seed 20261110, num_ctx 8192, max 1,536 output tokens; JSON schema in Ollama `format` with drug and symptom names as fixed lists; 3 few-shot examples from fixtures (never dev or test). The model returns quotes; our code finds them verbatim and runs the same evidence check as the rules. Subset: `random.Random(20261110).sample` of 40 DEV conversations (`results/m3_extract/llm_subset.txt`, asked for by the orchestrator to save time); the rules are scored on the same 40.
- Why: It is the only model that fits our hardware and budget today. The same subset, same vocabulary and same evidence check make the comparison fair.
- What we actually did: `m3_extract/llm.py`; ran `mingw32-make m3-run-llm-dev` on 2026-11-10 (server 127.0.0.1:11434). Results: `results/m3_extract/llm/` (36 records, 4 rejected), `llm/latency.csv`, `llm/run_summary.json`. On the 40 conversations: LLM critical 6/508 fields = 11.8 per 1,000 (Wilson 95% CI 5.4-25.5), major 59/508 = 116.1 (91.1-146.9), 4 records rejected by the evidence check; rules v2 critical 0/488 (0-7.8), major 4/488 = 8.2 (3.2-20.9). Latency p50 47 s, p95 253 s per conversation.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: 40 conversations is small (wide CIs). The rules were tuned on most of these templates (in-sample), the LLM was not, so the comparison favours the rules; on the 8 holdout conversations in the subset both pipelines miss things. Many LLM "major" errors are advice worded differently (MA-WORDING-UNJUDGED) and may be the same meaning. Latency depends on other load on the laptop.
- What else could be done: Run on all 328 dev conversations overnight (`uv run python -m m3_extract run-llm --all-dev`); a larger model on a free Colab GPU; use the LLM only to propose items for the rules to verify.
- How to change this decision later: `MODEL`, `EXPECTED_DIGEST`, options and prompt in `m3_extract/llm.py`; subset size in `m3_extract/splits.py`.
- Status: done

### D-88: Scope cut: no extraction from ASR transcripts yet   (Phase B / m3_extract / nlp-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: AGENT 4 step 5 asks to run both pipelines on ASR outputs "when available".
- Options we considered:
  1. Wait for m2_asr. Pro: real ASR errors. Con: not available on 2026-11-10 (`results/m2_asr/` does not exist).
  2. Make fake ASR errors ourselves. Con: that is m1/m7's planted-error job (E4) and would mix planted and natural errors.
- What we chose: Option 1; the runner already accepts `--transcripts DIR`.
- Why: Honest scope: nothing is claimed about ASR input.
- What we actually did: `uv run python -m m3_extract run-rules --transcripts <dir>` writes to `results/m3_extract/rules_<dir name>/`; scoring compares values with gold (offset overlap is skipped because the text differs). The ASR fixture test (`test_asr_fixture_keeps_audio_times_and_faithful_value`) shows audio times are filled from word timestamps.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Real ASR text (wrong spellings, missing punctuation) will break exact-token matching; expect many more misses.
- What else could be done: Edit-distance matching for Kannada drug names (now Latin only), punctuation-free clause splitting.
- How to change this decision later: run the command above, then extend `run.score_dev` with an ASR plan.
- Status: not done (needs m2 outputs)

### D-89: The extractor builder saw one template file that also contains a test family   (Phase B / m3_extract / orchestrator / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: The m3 builder reported that, while reading templates to learn the phrasing, it opened data/templates/a_asha_fever_cough.yaml, which also holds the sentence frames of a TEST family (a_fever_cough__f3). It did not open any test gold or test transcript. Does this break the frozen-test rule, and what do we do?
- Options we considered: (1) Ignore it. Pro: no work. Con: hides a possible leak; dishonest. (2) Throw away m3 and rebuild it without template access. Pro: cleanest. Con: costs a lot of usage; the builder still needs to know the general phrasing from somewhere. (3) Keep m3, disclose the exposure, and have the integrity auditor check whether any rule or phrase list in m3 matches wording that appears ONLY in test families. If a test-only phrase is found, remove it and re-score dev. Pro: proportionate; makes the risk measurable. Con: a subtle influence (for example word order ideas) cannot be fully ruled out.
- What we chose: Option 3.
- Why: Template files are not gold labels, and the leak is limited to one family's sentence frames. The honest fix is to disclose it and check for test-only wording, then report the test score of that family separately so a reader can see whether it looks suspiciously good.
- What we actually did: Checked that no test conversation id appears anywhere in results/m3_extract (0 found) and that the test lock is intact (`mingw32-make split-lock` -> OK). Added this check to the integrity auditor's job list. Also recorded the builder's honest dev-score caveat: 240 dev conversations were used to write the rules (in-sample, 0 critical errors), and 88 held-out dev conversations are the fair estimate for new wording; that holdout is no longer clean after the v2 fix (v1 scores kept in results/m3_extract/rules_v1_frozen/).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The test score for the a_fever_cough__f3 family may be optimistic. The in-sample dev score (0 critical) must never be quoted as the system's accuracy.
- What else could be done: Future builders get a template view with test families removed (a filtered copy), so they cannot see test frames at all.
- How to change this decision later: m1_data could publish data/templates_dev_only/; m7 should report the a_fever_cough__f3 family separately in E2/E3 test results.
- Status: partly done (auditor check pending)

### D-90: Integrity audit of m1, m3 and m4: what we accept, and the correction to D-60/D-64   (Phase B / m1_data, m3_extract, m4_verify / orchestrator / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: The simulated scientific-integrity auditor (reviews/{m1_data,m4_verify,m3_extract}/2026-11-10-integrity.md) failed all three module gates. For each finding: accept, reject or defer? And does the test split need to be refrozen?
- Options we considered: For the shared "dose sentence" frame: (1) Refreeze now, adding 1-2 sentence frames that exist only in test. Pro: gives a clean "never-seen frame" test. Con: touches the frozen test lock; more work. (2) Keep the freeze, correct our written claim, and have m7 report test results separately for turns whose frame was also seen in dev versus frames never seen in dev. Pro: no change to frozen data; about 86% of test turns already use frames not in dev, so the "unseen" slice is large. Con: the "seen frame" slice may look better than real life.
- What we chose: Option 2 (no refreeze). All other findings are ACCEPTED. None rejected.
- Why: The audit confirmed the important things hold: the test lock is intact, only the freeze commit touched test files, no family or drug-dose combination crosses the split, the generator is byte-identical on re-run, all 872 dev spans are correct, and no test id appears in m3. The shared dose frame is a real overclaim in our own text (D-60 and D-64 said no sentence frame crosses the split; that is FALSE: one dosing frame covers 13.9% of test turns and 18.1% of test clauses), so we correct the text and measure the effect instead of hiding it.
- What we actually did: Recorded the decision for each finding. CRITICAL M4-1 (accepted): m4's test guard only refuses paths containing the word "test", so `e4 --gold-dir data/gold` would read the 264 test gold files. Confirmed by reading m4_verify/planted.py (refuse_test_split). It has NOT been triggered (no E4 run on data/gold so far). Rule until fixed: nobody runs m4 E4 or tune-c7 on data/. MAJOR m4 (accepted, for the m4 fix pass): count a planted error as "caught" only when a flag lands on the injected field (5/30 wrong-patient catches came from an unrelated paracetamol flag); README must quote unique-mutation figures (e.g. 4/4, CI 0.51-1.0), not 200/200; review burden with the project lexicon is 1/18, not 0/18; the second-transcript (C7) result uses a perfect transcript and must be labelled an upper bound. MAJOR m1 (accepted): this entry corrects D-60/D-64; docs/annotation_guide.md must stop printing 8 words that occur only in test templates. MAJOR m3 (accepted): remove 3 test-only words, including the regex branch for ಡ್ರೆ that can only fire on test family i_traps__f2, then re-score dev; label that 32 of the 40 rules-vs-LLM conversations are in-sample for the rules; stop quoting the pooled "0 critical on full dev" headline. For m7 (accepted): field-level catch attribution with unique N, review burden next to recall, and separate test slices for a_fever_cough__f3, c_stopped_early__f1, i_traps__f2 and seen-frame vs unseen-frame turns.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Removing test-only words from m3 after seeing them is itself a decision informed by the test templates; we disclose it. The auditor did not rerun E4 or m3 scoring, and its masking method may miss some shared frames (UNVERIFIED). The auditor is simulated (ai_simulated_review).
- What else could be done: The optional refreeze (option 1) is still possible, but ONLY before the first `make eval-test`.
- How to change this decision later: Add a new entry; the fix passes for m1, m3 and m4 are listed in PROGRESS.md.
- Status: partly done (fix passes pending)

### D-91: Clinical review of m1 and m4, and one planned refreeze of the test split (REFREEZE-1)   (Phase B / m1_data, m4_verify / orchestrator / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: The simulated clinical reviewer (reviews/m1_data/2026-11-10-clinical.md and reviews/m4_verify/2026-11-10-clinical.md, label ai_simulated_review) found two CRITICAL problems: (1) five templates (a3, e8, h2, h3, i5) show a child ("ಮಗುವಿಗೆ") being given ADULT doses (paracetamol 1000 mg or 2 tablets up to 4 times a day; ibuprofen 400 mg); (2) the verifier's dose check C4 uses adult ranges for everyone, so it lets those child doses pass. Two of these templates (h2, h3: 16 conversations) are in the FROZEN test split. Do we change frozen test data?
- Options we considered: (1) Keep the data as it is and only fix C4. Pro: the test lock is untouched. Con: our "gold" (correct answer) data would keep teaching that an adult dose for a child is a normal record; a reader could copy unsafe example sentences; and every correct C4 flag on those fields would wrongly count as "review burden". (2) Do ONE documented refreeze now, before any test-split run: fix the child templates (child turns get no dose or a paediatric liquid in ml with an age band), fix the lexicon rows the reviewer corrected, then regenerate and relock. Pro: the data stops showing unsafe dosing; the scores measure the verifier, not our own data mistakes. Con: the lock changes once; we must show in git that no test result existed before the change. (3) Delete the child templates. Pro: simple. Con: loses the child/paediatric case, which is exactly where real dosing danger is.
- What we chose: Option 2, called REFREEZE-1. It will be done by the m1 fix pass AFTER the Kannada review of the templates, so that the test data changes only once.
- Why: The brief forbids tuning on test and changing test data after evaluation. It does not forbid fixing a clinically unsafe data error before ANY test evaluation has happened (D-64 allows a documented refreeze before the first `make eval-test`). results/test_access_log.md shows no test run yet. Fixing data safety beats keeping an early lock.
- What we actually did: Validated both clinical reports against review_report.v1 (the reviewer had no shell): both valid. Accepted findings (to fix in the m1 and m4 fix passes): paracetamol range 1000-1000 -> 500-1000 mg and max daily 3000 -> 4000 mg (NHS page fetched by the reviewer); ondansetron 8-24 -> 4-24; metformin 500-850 -> 500-1000; ibuprofen 300-800 -> 200-800 (openFDA labels fetched by the reviewer); ORS needs a unit (sachet or ml); iron-folic acid needs per-component units; add the "625" alias for amoxicillin-clavulanate; remove the "levo" alias (it often means levocetirizine; reviewer judgement only); template e2 must not say ciprofloxacin can be taken together with zinc or iron; C4 must treat doses for a child as "not checkable" (flag); C4's "blank range" flag gets its own reason code ("cannot verify") and is reported separately, so scores measure the verifier and not gaps in our drug list; add max_daily where a fetched source gives it; restrict allowed units per drug; E4 drug swaps must pick confusable drugs from the lexicon; docs/severity.md gets new critical classes for route, child/weight-based dose and liquid strength (a pre-registration document change, logged before any test run). DEFERRED (UNVERIFIED, human must check): metronidazole 200-2000 and cotrimoxazole 400-800 (not re-fetched); all NLEM rows (every NLEM PDF fetch failed); ISMP confusable pairs (PDF unreadable); the AWaRe edition year. Confirmed correct: the AWaRe class of all 18 antibiotics on the WHO portal.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: A refreeze can look like moving the goalposts. Mitigation: it happens before any test result exists, it is one commit with this entry in the message, and the old lock stays in git history. The reviewer is simulated, not a real clinician: the clinical sheet (reviews/human_sheets/clinical_review_sheet.csv, 34 drugs + 13 findings) must still go to a real pharmacist or doctor.
- What else could be done: Add a paediatric weight-based dose table from a fetched national source (for example the IAP or NLEM paediatric doses) so C4 can really check child doses instead of only flagging them.
- How to change this decision later: data/templates/{a,e,h,i}_*.yaml, data/lexicon/drugs.csv, m4_verify C4 config, docs/severity.md; regenerate with `mingw32-make data` and relock in ONE commit.
- Status: partly done (fix passes pending)

### D-92: Security audit of m5: one real hole in the save gate, seven small ones   (Phase B / m5_backend / orchestrator / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: The simulated security auditor (reviews/m5_backend/2026-11-10.md, label ai_simulated_review) made 121 concrete break attempts against the database and API. What do we fix, and how?
- Options we considered: For the main hole (F1): (1) Keep accepting the verdict from the client and add a signature to it. Pro: small change. Con: the client still decides what the verifier said; signatures add key handling. (2) The server stores every verdict itself when the verifier runs, and the save request only refers to that stored verdict by id; the server then checks that every flag in the STORED verdict has a resolution from the approving physician (this merges with D-80). Pro: the client can no longer invent a clean verdict; matches the brief ("the API rejects saving a record whose verdict has unresolved flags"). Con: more work in m5 (a verdicts table, policies, tests).
- What we chose: Option 2 for F1, built together with D-80 in the m5 fix pass. All findings ACCEPTED; none rejected. F8 is partly deferred (see below).
- Why: F1 is the only attack that succeeded: in attempt SG-1 a physician saved a never-approved draft by sending a hand-made "all checks pass" verdict and got "200 saved". The safety gate is the heart of the project, so it must not trust the client.
- What we actually did: Validated the report against review_report.v1 (valid) and confirmed the sandbox database is stopped. Planned fixes for the m5 pass: F1 server-stored verdicts (with D-80 and the auditor's 8 conditions: server stores the verdict; each resolution matches exactly one flag; an edited field is re-verified; the resolver comes only from the token; everything written in one append-only transaction); F2 the audit function takes user and role from the database session role, not from settings the caller can change; F3 research export suppresses small groups (any combination of age band, sex, month and visit type with fewer than 5 patients is dropped or merged) - with our tiny synthetic seed this may leave the export nearly empty, which we will say honestly; F4 a consent withdrawal removes that patient's research rows immediately (trigger) instead of waiting for the next export; F5 the connection pool resets every connection when it is returned (so an injected `SET ROLE` cannot reach the next user); F6 the API refuses to start if its database login is a superuser, has BYPASSRLS or owns the tables; F7 .env.example stops using owner_role as the Docker superuser name; F8 patient_ref stops being the first 8 characters of the patient UUID (use a random or keyed reference). DEFERRED (documented residual limit): F8's row-count leak through EXPLAIN ANALYZE and table statistics, because it needs direct SQL access, which only someone holding raw database credentials has.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Confirmed holding (but by a simulated auditor): researcher and pharmacist could not reach base tables or other patients; all JWT attacks returned 401; mass assignment, SQL injection and audio IDOR were refused; the audit log could not be changed; 0 unauthorized successes in 1385 matrix cases; pip-audit clean. NOT done: gitleaks was not run (only a regex and exact-match scan of .env secrets, which found nothing); the Docker path was not run. The same AI wrote the expected-access table and the SQL, so the human should re-read the table.
- What else could be done: Add k-anonymity checks to the matrix tests; run gitleaks in CI (already in the workflow file, never run).
- How to change this decision later: m5_backend migrations, app save endpoint, export job, pool setup, .env.example.
- Status: partly done (m5 fix pass pending)

### D-93: Kannada review of m4, m1 and m3: gaps in the negation rules and wording fixes for REFREEZE-1   (Phase B / m4_verify, m1_data, m3_extract / orchestrator / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: The simulated Kannada reviewer (reviews/{m4_verify,m1_data,m3_extract}/2026-11-10-kannada.md, label ai_simulated_review, run on the cheaper Sonnet model per D-65) found missing negation forms, a clause-splitting bug and several unnatural sentences. Which findings do we accept, and where are they fixed?
- Options we considered: (1) Accept everything and fix it in the coming fix passes. Pro: one round of fixes. Con: some suggestions rest only on the model's own knowledge (low confidence). (2) Accept only findings backed by a fetched source. Pro: strict. Con: only ONE source page was fetched (en.wiktionary pages returned 404), so almost everything would be ignored, including clear bugs. (3) Accept findings that are either source-backed OR testable bugs (a sentence our own data or a plain example shows is mishandled), mark the rest "low confidence", and send all of them to the human Kannada sheets.
- What we chose: Option 3. All "major" findings are accepted because each one is a concrete, testable case.
- Why: A rule that misses ತಗೋಬೇಡಿ ("don't take") or marks fever as absent in "ಜ್ವರ ಇದೆ ಕೆಮ್ಮು ಇಲ್ಲ" ("fever yes, cough no") is a safety bug whether or not a web page says so. But we must not pretend these fixes are native-validated.
- What we actually did: Validated all three reports against review_report.v1 (all valid; the reviewer had no shell). Assigned the fixes. m4 fix pass (Opus): ಬೇಡ/ಬೇಡಿ attached directly to a verb (ತಗೋಬೇಡಿ, ಕೊಡ್ಬೇಡಿ); missing whole-word cues ಬೇಡಾ, ಬೇಡವಾ, ಇಲ್ರೀ, ಇಲ್ಲಪ್ಪ; the colloquial negative question -ಲ್ವಾ (backed by a fetched grammar page); split clauses when a second "subject + ಇದೆ/ಇಲ್ಲ" starts without punctuation; cross-turn answers of more than one word ("ಇಲ್ಲ ಅಕ್ಕ", "ಹೌದ್ರೀ"). Shared table .claude/skills/kannada-nlp/negation_table.yaml (orchestrator/architect, before the m3 fix pass): stop matching ಬೆಲ್ಲ (jaggery), add the negative-question cue and ಆಗದೆ, do not split clauses at the dots inside abbreviations like ಎಂ.ಜಿ. m1 REFREEZE-1 (Sonnet): use the reviewer's child-syrup wording for a3, e8, h2, h3, i5 and "ಎರಡೂ ಔಷಧಿ ಒಟ್ಟಿಗೆ ಕೊಡಬಹುದಾ?" for e2; replace "ಕೊಡುವುದು ಬೇಡಿ" with "ಕೊಡಬೇಡಿ" in _forms.yaml; fix the nonsense positive twins of h5, e7 and h2; spell ORS with ಓ in the annotation guide; consider generating ಸಲ. m3 fix pass (Sonnet): accept ಆಗಿದ್ದರೆ in the fever-follow-up pattern, fused symptom negation like ಜ್ವರವಿಲ್ಲ, and ಪ್ಯಾಕೆಟ್ as a sachet unit.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Only one external source was fetched, so most findings are "model knowledge only, low confidence". The 30% template sample did not use the requested seed; the reviewer used a reproducible every-third-template rule instead (20 templates plus 4 extra), listed in the m1 JSON. The m1 .md contains one garbled phrase about the sampling method (the JSON is correct). Adding new negation cues may raise false flags; the trap tests must stay at zero false triggers.
- What else could be done: Real native reviewers fill reviews/human_sheets/kannada_review_sheet.csv (63 rows) and negation_rules_review_sheet.csv (51 rows); they should also rule on ಇಲ್ಲವೇ ("or" versus a spoken question), how a negative question is answered, and dialect forms (ಇಲ್ರೀ, ಗುಳಿಗೆ, ದಿವಸ).
- How to change this decision later: m4_verify/negation.py, .claude/skills/kannada-nlp/negation_table.yaml, data/templates/*.yaml (only inside REFREEZE-1, before any test run), m3_extract phrase files.
- Status: partly done (fix passes pending)

### D-94: The server stores every verdict; saving needs one resolution per flag (F1 + D-80 built)   (Phase B / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-10)
- What we needed to decide: How to stop a physician's client from sending a hand-made "all checks pass" verdict (audit attempt SG-1 saved a never-approved draft with HTTP 200), while still letting a reviewer who looked at a flag and confirmed it (or fixed the field) save the record.
- Options we considered:
  1. Sign the client's verdict. Pro: small change. Con: the client still decides what the verifier said; needs key handling.
