  1. Indic Parler-TTS (Apache-2.0). Pro: named Kannada speakers (Suresh, Anu, Chetan, Vidya), good quality. Con: the Hugging Face repo is gated; without a logged-in account that accepted the terms the download fails with HTTP 401 (checked 2026-11-11). We have no token.
  2. A third-party mirror of Parler weights (some accounts re-upload it ungated). Pro: downloads today. Con: it bypasses the owner's access terms and the weights come from an unknown uploader. Rejected.
  3. MMS-TTS Kannada (facebook/mms-tts-kan), the brief's named fallback. Pro: not gated, small (about 150 MB safetensors), CPU is enough, loads with the standard transformers library (no remote code). Con: ONE speaker only, CC-BY-NC-4.0 (non-commercial), plainer sound.
  4. indic-tiny-tts-kannada (male and female voices, CC-BY-4.0). Pro: two real speakers, ONNX, tiny. Con: needs running about 600 lines of third-party repo code. I read it (numpy + onnxruntime only) but the tool permission system refused to run it, so it was not run.
- What we chose: Option 3 in code (m1_data/tts_model.py), with option 4 and the Parler upgrade written down as human decisions.
- Why: It is the only model we may use today without tokens or approval, and the brief named it. The weakness is accepted and written down: one speaker.
- What we actually did: m1_data/tts_model.py (pinned revision 30e3c5d5, safetensors only), m1_data/tts_pronounce.py (MMS skips Latin letters, so English drug words are respelled in Kannada script for the TTS input only; gold text is untouched, the respelling is stored as `tts_input` in data/audio_index/tts_index.jsonl), tests in m1_data/tests/. The real synthesis run is NOT done (see the disk decision).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The respelling table (WORDS in tts_pronounce.py) is not native-validated. MMS audio sounds flat; ASR scores on it will not match real speech. The licence of the generated audio is UNVERIFIED. Torch CPU float output may differ in the last bits between machines, so audio hashes are only guaranteed on this machine (see the reproducibility entry).
- What else could be done: HF token then Parler (real speakers), or approve running indic-tiny-tts (two speakers).
- How to change this decision later: replace the class in m1_data/tts_model.py, keep the signature `model(text_kn, seed) -> float32 16 kHz`, bump AUDIO_VERSION in m1_data/audio_build.py, rebuild with `mingw32-make m1-audio`.
- Status: partly done (code and tests done, audio not yet built)

### D-132: Two "voices": the real MMS voice and a pitch-shifted copy held out for test   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The brief wants at least 2 distinct voices and one voice used only for the test split, but MMS has one speaker.
- Options we considered:
  1. Wait for a real second speaker (token or approval). Pro: honest, real. Con: blocks the audio work.
  2. Make a second voice by lowering the pitch of the same synthetic speaker (ffmpeg asetrate + atempo, ratio 0.84). Pro: deterministic, free, still tests "voice the dev work never saw". Con: it is NOT a different speaker model; it only sounds lower.
  3. Use seed changes only. Pro: simple. Con: same timbre, not a voice at all.
- What we chose: Option 2, named `mms_kan_pitch084`, used ONLY for test. `mms_kan_base` is used for dev and also for test (clean files only).
- Why: It satisfies the "held-out voice" rule in the weakest sensible form and keeps the pipeline moving. Reports must call it a pitch-shifted copy.
- What we actually did: m1_data/audio_dsp.py `shift_pitch` (tested: 400 Hz tone becomes 320 Hz, same duration), m1_data/audio_build.py plan(): test = held-out voice with ALL 9 variants plus base voice clean for every test conversation; dev = base voice, 9 variants, seeded subset of 80 conversations. A test (test_plan_on_real_data_respects_the_split_rules) proves the held-out voice never appears in dev.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Generalisation to a new voice is only weakly tested. Pitch shift also moves formants, so it may sound odd.
- What else could be done: Parler speakers Suresh (test only) and Anu (dev), or the two voices of indic-tiny-tts.
- How to change this decision later: edit BASE_VOICE, HELD_OUT_VOICE, PITCH_RATIO in m1_data/audio_build.py and the plan() function.
- Status: partly done

### D-133: Audio variants, file layout, subset sizes and the size cap   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Which variants, how many conversations, and where files go, inside a 2.5 GB cap.
- Options we considered:
  1. Every variant for every conversation and both voices: about 4 GB. Over the cap.
  2. Full variants only for the held-out voice on test and a seeded dev subset: about 1.5 GB. Fits.
  3. Clean only: about 0.2 GB. Too thin for the noise and speed experiments.
- What we chose: Option 2. Variants: clean; white and pink noise at SNR 20, 10, 5 dB (SNR measured over the whole clip, gaps included, tested within 0.05 dB); speed 0.9 and 1.1 (ffmpeg atempo, pitch kept). Clean audio is peak-normalised to 0.5 so noise never clips. One WAV per conversation (turns joined by 0.5 s silence), 16 kHz mono PCM16, path data/audio/tts/<split>/<voice>/<variant>/<conv>.wav.
- Why: Whole-test coverage keeps the test numbers honest; a seeded dev subset keeps dev tuning cheap. Plan size: 3360 files (264 test conversations x 10 + 80 dev x 9), estimated 1.5 GB (the 14 s per conversation is an assumption, UNVERIFIED until built).
- What we actually did: `uv run python -m m1_data audio-plan` prints the counts. Index with snr, noise type, speed, voice, seed, turn offsets and TTS input text: data/audio_index/tts_index.jsonl (tracked, written by the build). Manifest rows go into data/manifest.jsonl; audio is git-ignored.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Turn offsets for the pitch voice are approximate (within a few ms). Speed 0.9 and 1.1 are applied to clean only (not combined with noise).
- What else could be done: more dev conversations if disk allows; combined noise and speed.
- How to change this decision later: VARIANTS and DEV_SUBSET_N in m1_data/audio_build.py.
- Status: partly done (planned and tested with a fake synthesiser, not yet built for real)

### D-134: Disk blocker: audio and FLEURS not built; the pagefile ate 9 GB   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Whether to run the real synthesis when free disk is at the floor.
- Options we considered:
  1. Run anyway and dip below the 4 GB floor. Pro: finishes. Con: breaks the orchestrator's hard rule and risks the ASR work.
  2. Delete other people's caches (uv cache 5.7 GB, HF 2.1 GB, codex-runtimes 1.8 GB). Pro: space. Con: not my files; may break running agents (`uv cache clean` itself waited for another uv process).
  3. Stop, finish code, tests and docs, and hand over exact steps. Pro: safe. Con: no audio yet.
- What we chose: Option 3.
- Why: Free space went from 13.6 GB to about 4.0 GB while I installed torch; I found C:\pagefile.sys at 24.8 GB (grown at 04:46). Nothing I downloaded explains it (my CUDA venv was deleted, my uv cache additions are small). A pagefile only shrinks after a restart.
- What we actually did: Built everything with a free-disk guard (`--min-free-gb`, default 4.0; unit-tested) and resumable steps. FLEURS is streamed (no 615 MB tarball). Nothing large was downloaded. The old CUDA torch environment and the Parler worker were removed.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The deliverables "audio files, FLEURS subset, test.lock audio lines" do not exist yet. The final free-disk target (>= 9 GB) is unreachable until the pagefile shrinks or caches are cleaned.
- What else could be done: restart Windows; or run on another machine and copy only the manifest/lock lines; or Colab.
- How to change this decision later: run `mingw32-make m1-audio` after freeing space (see human_todo.md).
- Status: needs human

### D-135: Public real speech: Kannada FLEURS test split, streamed, split by sentence   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Where to get real speech, how to load it now that script loaders are gone, how to split it.
- Options we considered:
  1. FLEURS kn_in via the datasets library script loader. Con: scripts are no longer supported.
  2. FLEURS files straight from the repo (data/kn_in/test.tsv and audio/test.tar.gz, revision 70bb2e84). Pro: works with plain HTTP. Con: the tarball is 615 MB (streamed, so never stored).
  3. Common Voice, IndicVoices, Kathbath. Con: gated or no clear licence page (see licences); skipped.
- What we chose: Option 2. 838 utterances in the official test split, 344 sentences. Seeded selection by whole sentence until at least 250 utterances, sentences dealt alternately to our dev and test, so one sentence is never in both. Labelled source=public_real; never pooled with TTS.
- Why: At least 200 utterances needed; sentence-level grouping avoids near-duplicate leakage between dev and test. CC-BY-4.0.
- What we actually did: m1_data/fleurs.py (tested offline with a fake tarball), `python -m m1_data fleurs`. The transcripts of the subset go to data/public_real/fleurs_kn_in_test_subset.jsonl; test-assigned files get lines appended to test.lock. NOT RUN yet (disk). I did fetch test.tsv: 838 rows, 7 columns, mean 13.6 s per utterance (about 110 MB for 250).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Speaker ids are not in the data, so speaker overlap between dev and test cannot be ruled out. FLEURS text is read news/Wikipedia sentences, not clinical speech.
- What else could be done: Common Voice or Kathbath with a token.
- How to change this decision later: N_MIN and SEED in m1_data/fleurs.py.
- Status: partly done

### D-136: Test lock may only gain lines; the text generator keeps audio rows   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: How audio hashes join data/splits/test.lock and data/manifest.jsonl without breaking the frozen text split.
- Options we considered:
  1. A second lock file. Pro: no change to the old one. Con: the brief and CI read one file.
  2. Append audio lines after a comment line at the end of test.lock; make `generate` preserve that tail and the audio manifest rows. Pro: old lines stay byte-identical; `git diff` shows additions only. Con: generate.py needed a small edit.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: Keeps one lock; proves "only gains lines" with a plain diff.
- What we actually did: m1_data/split.py (`split_audio_tail`; missing git-ignored audio under data/audio/ is skipped, changed audio still fails), m1_data/generate.py (keeps `data/audio/` manifest rows and the lock tail), m1_data/audio_build.py `append_lock` (raises if an already locked file changed). Tests: m1_data/tests/test_audio_lock_integration.py, test_audio_build.py.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: A fresh clone has no audio, so the audio lock lines are only checked after `make m1-audio`.
- What else could be done: ship a few tiny fixtures.
- How to change this decision later: edit append_lock and split_audio_tail.
- Status: done (code); lines will appear when the audio is built

### D-137: Reproducibility of audio hashes   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Can `make` regenerate audio with identical sha256?
- Options we considered: lock only the hashes we can reproduce, or lock everything and accept re-lock on drift.
- What we chose: Deterministic design: one torch seed per utterance, CPU only, noise from numpy with a per-file seed, ffmpeg with -bitexact. Verified with the fake synthesiser (two builds give identical hashes). NOT verified for the real MMS model: needs two real runs.
- Why: GPU runs are avoided; CPU torch is usually repeatable on one machine but not guaranteed across machines or versions.
- What we actually did: test_build_is_deterministic_and_rows_validate. `audio-verify` compares the manifest with files on disk.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: UNVERIFIED that two real runs give the same hashes. If they differ, the test.lock audio lines would flag a rebuild as a change; then rebuild once, accept the hashes as the frozen reference and say so.
- What else could be done: lock the 16 kHz clean turn files' decoded samples with a tolerance instead of a byte hash.
- How to change this decision later: m1_data/audio_build.py (seeds), `mingw32-make m1-audio-verify`.
- Status: partly done

### D-138: Retrieval method: Ollama nomic-embed-text with a lexical fallback, no generation   (Phase C / m8_helper / counselling-helper-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: How to find the right card for a question, locally, without downloading a new model, and without letting a model write medical advice.
- Options we considered:
  1. multilingual-e5 / LaBSE / BGE-M3 (named in the brief). Pro: built for many languages. Con: needs a new download; disk is tight; not allowed in this task.
  2. Ollama nomic-embed-text (already installed). Pro: no download, local. Con: mainly English, so Kannada quality is doubtful.
  3. Our own TF-IDF (word counts) with Kannada 3-letter pieces. Pro: no model, runs anywhere, used for mock mode and tests. Con: weak on meaning, noisy on Kannada word endings.
  4. Let qwen3 paraphrase the card. Pro: friendlier text. Con: new risk of unsupported sentences; not required.
- What we chose: Options 2 and 3 side by side; the answer is always the stored card text. Option 4 not built.
- Why: Option 2 scored better on the dev questions (10 of 20 in-scope answered vs 7 of 20); option 3 works with no daemon. Quoting the card verbatim means no sentence can be unsupported.
- What we actually did: `m8_helper/helper.py` (TfidfRetriever, OllamaRetriever), `m8_helper/evaluate.py`. Re-run: `mingw32-make m8-eval`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Only about half of in-scope questions get answered, far below the 90% target (H4). Kannada embedding quality is UNVERIFIED beyond these 80 questions. Option 1 was not tried.
- What else could be done: try multilingual-e5 or BGE-M3 when disk allows; add card paraphrases; native-written questions.
- How to change this decision later: `make_retriever` in `evaluate.py`, `build` and THRESHOLDS in `__main__.py`.
- Status: partly done

### D-139: Abstain rule: safety gate first, then a similarity threshold tuned on dev only   (Phase C / m8_helper / counselling-helper-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: When must the helper refuse, and what number is the similarity cut-off.
- Options we considered:
  1. Threshold only. Pro: simple. Con: a dose question naming "amoxicillin" can still match a card strongly.
  2. Safety gate (regex rules) before retrieval, then threshold. Pro: unsafe questions never reach a card, even at threshold 0. Con: hand-written rules can miss new wording.
  3. An LLM judge. Pro: flexible. Con: another unverified model inside a safety path.
- What we chose: Option 2. Gate categories: dosing, pregnancy, diagnosis or symptom report, stop-early, requesting a drug, child plus drug, leftover or sharing tablets, prompt injection. Threshold: the highest value with zero wrong dev answers that maximises correct dev answers: tfidf 0.28, ollama 0.75.
- Why: The brief says 100% of dosing and diagnosis requests abstain; a gate gives that by construction, a threshold cannot. The gate also over-blocks (for example any "can I take ...?"); we accept that.
- What we actually did: `safety_gate` in `m8_helper/helper.py`; tests in `m8_helper/tests/test_helper.py` check abstention even at threshold 0. Results: `results/m8_helper/eval_*.json`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Rules were written knowing the adversarial categories, so the test split measures new wording only. Kannada and romanised-Kannada rules are not native-validated. The Kannada stop-word list was chosen after printing scores for ALL rows (including test), so test is not pristine for that choice.
- What else could be done: native Kannada reviewer pass on the rules; more adversarial wording.
- How to change this decision later: `_RULES` in `helper.py`; re-run `mingw32-make m8-eval` and update THRESHOLDS.
- Status: done

### D-140: Cards are AI-authored drafts; normal mode serves none   (Phase C / m8_helper / counselling-helper-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Where card text comes from and which cards may be shown.
- Options we considered:
  1. Wait for clinician-written cards. Pro: safest. Con: nothing to demo.
  2. AI-write cards from fetched WHO pages and mark them draft. Pro: demoable and traceable. Con: still unreviewed.
  3. Write the brief example topics (finish the course, no antibiotics for colds) from memory. Con: breaks the rule against unfetched claims.
- What we chose: Option 2, only statements found in the three fetched pages (curl 2026-11-12: who.int antimicrobial-resistance, pneumonia, diarrhoeal-disease). card.v1 has no "approved" field, so approved means clinician_reviewed=true. Normal mode serves only those (none exist); demo mode (explicit flag) serves drafts with a DRAFT label.
- Why: Honest and safe by default; the demo still works.
- What we actually did: 16 cards in `m8_helper/cards/` (the brief asked for 20 to 30). Generator `m8_helper/author_cards.py`. Validated: 16/16 against card.v1.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Brief topics NOT covered because the fetched pages do not state them: finishing a course, never using leftovers, "antibiotics do not treat colds", when to see a doctor for a fever. Nothing was fetched from ICMR, NCDC or MoHFW, and no official Kannada page was found or looked for (UNVERIFIED whether one exists), so all Kannada is machine-drafted, not native-validated.
- What else could be done: fetch ICMR/MoHFW/NCDC pages and an official Kannada leaflet; clinician review; add the missing topics.
- How to change this decision later: edit `author_cards.py`, regenerate, re-run eval; set clinician_reviewed=true only after a real clinician signs.
- Status: needs human

### D-141: Evaluation design for the helper   (Phase C / m8_helper / counselling-helper-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: How to measure answering and abstaining honestly with a small set.
- Options we considered:
  1. One pooled accuracy. Pro: one number. Con: hides unsafe answers.
  2. Separate rates with Wilson intervals and a dev/test split by topic. Pro: honest about small N. Con: wide intervals.
- What we chose: Option 2. 80 AI-written questions: 30 in-scope, 30 out-of-scope, 20 adversarial; English, Kannada, romanised Kannada. Dev topics tune the threshold; test topics (diarrhoea, dengue, cancer, fever, skin, pregnancy, diagnosis, stop, request, leftover) are scored once at the frozen threshold.
- Why: The split is by topic as the brief asks; N and intervals show how little 80 questions prove.
- What we actually did: `m8_helper/eval/build_eval.py`, `m8_helper/evaluate.py`, results in `results/m8_helper/`. Wilson 95% results: tfidf dev unsafe answered 0/8 [0, 0.32], test 0/10 [0, 0.28]; wrong answers dev 0/46, test 0/34; in-scope answered with the right card dev 7/20, test 6/10. ollama: dev 10/20, test 6/10, same zero wrong answers. Abstain recall 26/26 dev and 24/24 test (both backends).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: "0 of 10" only bounds the unsafe-answer rate near 30%. Questions are not from real users. H4 (answer 90% of in-scope) is NOT met. No clinical or educational impact is claimed.
- What else could be done: more questions from a native speaker; real user wording.
- How to change this decision later: `eval/build_eval.py` then `mingw32-make m8-eval`.
- Status: done

### D-142: m8 helper accepted as a safe-but-weak first version; H4 is not met and its own test questions are not pristine   (Phase B / m8_helper / orchestrator / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The m8 builder (Sonnet) delivered a helper that only quotes a card or abstains. It answers about half of in-scope questions, below the 90% target in hypothesis H4, and it chose Kannada stop-words after seeing scores that included its own held-out questions. Accept, rebuild, or cut?
- Options we considered: (1) Accept as it is and report H4 as not met. Pro: honest; the safety part (0 unsafe answers) holds; m8 is first on the brief's scope-cut list anyway. Con: weak coverage. (2) Rebuild with more cards and a stronger multilingual embedding model. Pro: better coverage. Con: needs new downloads (disk is nearly full) and fetched sources for topics like "finish the course", which we do not have yet. (3) Cut m8. Pro: saves effort. Con: loses a working, safe component.
- What we chose: Option 1.
- Why: The brief puts m8 first in the scope-cut order; a helper that abstains safely is better than one that answers more by guessing.
- What we actually did: Re-ran the checks myself: `pytest m8_helper` 34 passed; 16/16 cards valid against card.v1; ruff clean; only m8_helper and results/m8_helper changed; 0 cards are clinician-reviewed, so normal mode abstains on everything and demo mode shows DRAFT cards. Builder's numbers (80 AI-written questions, Wilson 95%): unsafe questions answered 0/8 dev and 0/10 held-out; wrong answers 0/46 and 0/34; in-scope answered with the right card 7/20 and 6/10 (TF-IDF), 10/20 and 6/10 (Ollama embeddings).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: H4 (90% in-scope coverage) is NOT met. The helper's held-out questions are contaminated: stop-words were chosen after printing scores for all rows, and the safety rules were written knowing the attack categories; the report must say so. This m8 split is separate from the frozen data test split (data/splits), which m8 never read. Cards are AI-written from three WHO fact sheets fetched 2026-11-12; WHO site terms and the nomic-embed-text licence are UNVERIFIED.
- What else could be done: Fetch an official source for course completion and leftovers, write a fresh held-out question set nobody has looked at, and get a clinician to approve the cards.
- How to change this decision later: m8_helper/cards, m8_helper/helper.py thresholds (dev only), m8_helper/eval.
- Status: done

### D-143: How m7 scores extraction errors: its own scorer, the same "field" unit as m3, plus precision and recall per item   (Phase C / m7_eval / research-scientist-eval / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: How the evaluation module counts errors in the records that m3 extracts, so the numbers can be checked independently of m3's own scoring.
- Options we considered:
  1. Copy m3's scores (results/m3_extract/dev_scores.csv). Pro: no work. Con: m7 would just repeat m3's claims; the brief says every number must be recomputed from raw outputs; m7 may not import m3 code.
  2. Write m7's own scorer with a NEW field definition. Pro: fully independent. Con: numbers not comparable with m3's tables; readers get confused.
  3. Write m7's own scorer but use the SAME documented field unit as m3 (medication 6 fields, symptom 2, advice 2, follow-up 1), and add item-level precision, recall and F1. Pro: independent code, comparable numbers, a built-in cross-check. Con: the m7 author read m3's scoring code, so a shared misunderstanding is possible.
- What we chose: Option 3.
- Why: Two separate programs that agree are stronger evidence than one. They do agree on dev: 3624 fields, 0 critical and 48 major errors (40 missed advice, 8 missed follow-up) for the m3 rules; a test fails if they ever disagree.
- What we actually did: m7_eval/scoring.py (score_record, summarise); tests in m7_eval/tests/test_scoring.py (hand-made records for every class) and test_pipeline.py (agreement with m3). The scorer already handles record.v2 + gold.v2: CR-ROUTE, CR-LIQUID-STRENGTH, CR-PAEDIATRIC, status flips as CR-NEG, other status changes as MA-STATUS, other new fields as MA-MED-ATTRIBUTE (docs/severity.md v2 note). With v1 inputs those three classes are reported as "not measurable", never as zero. Tables: results/m7_eval/dev/tables/e2_extraction_*.csv. Run: `mingw32-make eval`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Advice and follow-up wording differences are not judged (code MA-WORDING-UNJUDGED; a human must decide MI-WORDING). The extra codes MA-MISSED-ADVICE, MA-EXTRA-ADVICE, MA-MISSED-FOLLOWUP, MA-EXTRA-FOLLOWUP are not in docs/severity.md yet (D-122 accepted them in principle). All dev numbers are in-sample: the m3 rules were fixed on dev.
- What else could be done: A third, blind scorer written from docs/severity.md only, by someone who never read m3's code.
- How to change this decision later: m7_eval/scoring.py and its tests; re-run `mingw32-make eval`.
- Status: done

### D-144: Confidence intervals: Wilson for proportions, cluster bootstrap by template family for extraction rates, paired bootstrap for comparisons, unique mutations for planted errors   (Phase C / m7_eval / research-scientist-eval / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Which uncertainty ranges (confidence intervals, "CI": a range that would contain the true value in about 95% of repeated experiments) m7 prints next to every number.
- Options we considered:
  1. Wilson interval for everything. Pro: simple, standard, works at 0 and 100%. Con: assumes every field is independent; our errors come in groups (one template that the rules miss produces the same miss in all its conversations), so the range is too narrow (D-120, D-121).
  2. Cluster bootstrap: resample whole template families (12 on dev) with replacement and recompute the error rate 1000 times. Pro: respects the grouping. Con: with only 12 clusters the range is rough, and with 0 errors it collapses to [0, 0].
  3. Show both: cluster bootstrap as the PRIMARY range for extraction error rates, Wilson next to it labelled "assumes independence", and the rule of three (95% upper bound about 3/N) whenever the count is 0.
- What we chose: Option 3. Plus: paired bootstrap (resample the per-item differences between two systems on the SAME items) for model comparisons, and planted-mistake (E4) counts over UNIQUE mutations, as m4 decided in D-115.
- Why: Honest ranges must be wide when the data are clumpy. Example on dev: the 48 major m3 errors give 13.2 per 1,000 fields; Wilson says [10.0, 17.5] but the cluster bootstrap says [4.8, 23.3]. A zero is never shown without "0 of N, upper bound about 3/N".
- What we actually did: m7_eval/stats.py (wilson, rule_of_three, proportion, per_1000, bootstrap_mean_ci, paired_bootstrap, cluster_bootstrap_rate). Unit tests against hand-computed values: Wilson 0/50 = [0, 0.0713], 3/50 = [0.0206, 0.1622], rule of three, and seed reproducibility (m7_eval/tests/test_stats.py). Seed 20261109, 1000 resamples, in m7_eval/configs/eval.yaml.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: 12 dev families (10 test families) is a small number of clusters, so the cluster range itself is uncertain. With 0 errors the cluster bootstrap is degenerate; we then rely on the rule of three. Bootstrap percentile intervals can be slightly too narrow with small N.
- What else could be done: A cluster bootstrap by template (finer than family), or a Bayesian beta-binomial model per family.
- How to change this decision later: `cluster_unit`, `seed`, `n_resamples` in m7_eval/configs/eval.yaml; functions in m7_eval/stats.py.
- Status: done

### D-145: How m7 keeps the test split closed   (Phase C / m7_eval / research-scientist-eval / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: How to make sure `make eval` never reads a test file, and that `make eval-test` reads them only after the access is logged.
- Options we considered:
  1. Trust each function to pass only dev ids. Pro: no extra code. Con: one wrong glob silently reads test files; nothing proves it did not happen.
  2. A single guarded reader (SplitGuard) that every read goes through, refusing test files, plus a test that patches Python's `open` and checks every opened path. Pro: one place to check; the test is independent proof. Con: small extra code; files that mix dev and test rows need a rule.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: The test split is frozen (PROJECT_BRIEF 6.5). A guard that refuses, plus a test that watches every file opened, is evidence rather than a promise.
- What we actually did: m7_eval/guard.py. In a DEV run it refuses: any path in data/splits/test.lock; any file whose name contains a test conversation id; any file inside a folder named `test`; the held-out test voice `mms_kan_pitch084`. It reads dev.txt, test.txt and test.lock only to know which ids to avoid. The test path (`python -m m7_eval eval-test`, registered as `m7-eval-test` in EVAL_TEST_TARGETS) refuses unless ALLOW_TEST=1, PURPOSE is set and results/test_access_log.md already has a row for the current git commit; that row is written by infra/scripts/eval_test_guard.py, which the root `make eval-test` runs first. Tests: m7_eval/tests/test_guard.py and test_pipeline.py (a full real DEV run with `open` patched: 0 test files opened; the CLI eval-test exits 3 with "REFUSED" and creates no log). manifest.json lists every input with its sha256 and a `test_files_opened` list (empty). Nobody ran eval-test; results/test_access_log.md does not exist.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Two module outputs mix dev and test rows in one file: results/m8_helper/predictions_*.jsonl (m8's own question split) and, once built, data/public_real/fleurs_kn_in_test_subset.jsonl. m7 opens them and drops the other split's rows before scoring; the rows are parsed but never used. A test file with an unusual name that is not in test.lock would not be caught by name.
- What else could be done: Ask m1 and m8 to write one file per split (open question); hash-check every opened file against test.lock as m4 does.
- How to change this decision later: m7_eval/guard.py and its tests; `held_out_voices` in m7_eval/configs/eval.yaml.
- Status: done

### D-146: E1 (speech recognition) input format, and "m2 not run yet" instead of a failure   (Phase C / m7_eval / research-scientist-eval / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: m2_asr is not built, but `make eval` must still run end to end and be ready for m2's output.
- Options we considered:
  1. Skip E1 until m2 exists. Pro: no work now. Con: the format would be decided later under time pressure; nothing tested.
  2. Define the file format m7 expects, write and test the metrics on fixtures, and print "m2 not run yet" in the table when the files are missing.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: The brief wants one command that always runs; an empty but honest table is better than a crash or a silent gap.
- What we actually did: Format (m7_eval/CONTRACT.md): results/m2_asr/<run>/<split>/transcripts.jsonl, one transcript.v1 per line, audio_id `<conversation>__<voice>__<variant>` (m1's audio naming, D-133) or the FLEURS file stem for real speech. m7_eval/asr_metrics.py computes CER (character error rate on normalised text: the share of characters that must be changed), grapheme CER, WER, CER without punctuation (secondary), critical-term recall per class (drug names from data/lexicon/drugs.csv, numbers, units, negation cues from the shared negation table), latency p50/p95 and real-time factor. Results are split by model, source (tts / public_real never pooled), variant (noise level, speed) and voice; models are compared with a paired bootstrap on the common subset (listed in tables/e1_common_subset__*.txt). Tested on a fake tree (m7_eval/mock.py). Today the table says "m2 not run yet".
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: m2 may choose another layout; then m2 or m7 must adapt (open question). The critical-term lists are UNVERIFIED starter lists (not native-validated). Gold text has punctuation, ASR output usually does not; the primary CER counts punctuation as the metrics document says, so we also print CER without punctuation.
- What else could be done: Read latency and hardware from a per-run run.json written by m2.
- How to change this decision later: m7_eval/experiments.py (e1_asr), m7_eval/configs/critical_terms.yaml, m7_eval/configs/eval.yaml `variants`.
- Status: partly done (pending m2)

### D-147: The check-removal experiment (E3) is computed from m4's saved per-check results, not by re-running m4   (Phase C / m7_eval / research-scientist-eval / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: How to show what happens when each safety check C1..C8 is switched off, while `make eval` needs no re-run of other modules.
- Options we considered:
  1. Re-run m4 eight times with one check disabled. Pro: the real experiment. Con: m7 may not import m4 code; slow; writes into m4's folder.
  2. Use m4's saved raw rows: for every planted mistake m4 stored WHICH checks flagged the right field. Removing check X = asking whether any OTHER check flagged it. Same for the flags on error-free fields. Pro: instant, reproducible, uses saved raw outputs. Con: only correct if checks do not read each other's results.
- What we chose: Option 2, labelled as derived.
- Why: It answers the question from saved data in seconds; the label tells readers what was assumed.
- What we actually did: e3_e4_verifier in m7_eval/experiments.py; table results/m7_eval/dev/tables/e3_ablation.csv and plot plots/e3_ablation.png. Dev, planted errors, in-sample (DEV): with all checks, 1054 of 1491 unique transcript-level text-only mistakes escape [0.683, 0.730] while 271/1200 error-free fields are flagged [0.203, 0.250]; removing C4 lets 1458/1491 escape [0.969, 0.984] but cuts the flag rate to 63/1200 [0.041, 0.067]. Record level: removing C3 drops the catch rate most (see the CSV). Natural errors: m3 made 0 critical errors on dev, so verifier recall on natural errors is NOT MEASURABLE (0/0), and m4 has not yet been run on m3's output.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: UNVERIFIED that the checks are independent; if one check uses another's result, the derived numbers differ from a real re-run. Planted errors on template text are easier than real ones.
- What else could be done: m4 adds a registry option to disable one check and writes eight runs; m7 then reads them and compares with this derivation.
- How to change this decision later: m7_eval/experiments.py (e3_e4_verifier).
- Status: done (derived; real re-run pending)

### D-148: Seen-frame versus unseen-frame marker from template text   (Phase C / m7_eval / research-scientist-eval / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: D-90 asked m7 to report test results separately for test turns whose sentence frame also appears in dev ("seen") and frames never seen in dev, without opening test conversations.
- Options we considered:
  1. Compare test conversation text with dev conversation text. Pro: exact. Con: opens test files outside eval-test.
  2. Compare TEMPLATE text only: replace every slot like {D1.dose} with one placeholder, unwrap gold-span markers, normalise, and check if the same frame exists in a dev template. Turns that are only a slot (a yes/no answer) get a third label, "slot_only". Pro: no test conversation is opened; the marker is ready before the test run. Con: works at template-turn level, not on generated variations.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: It follows the test-split rule and still gives the slice D-90 asked for.
- What we actually did: m7_eval/frames.py; tests in m7_eval/tests/test_frames.py. Result (template turns): test 83 = 15 seen, 56 unseen, 12 slot-only; the most common shared frame is the dosing frame of 10 template turns, which matches D-90's finding. Table: results/m7_eval/dev/tables/frame_overlap_templates.csv. In eval-test, every scored field gets the frame label of its gold turn (E2) and every planted mistake of its injected item (E4).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Exact frame match misses near-identical frames (one word different), so "unseen" may include almost-seen frames. Counts are per template turn, not per conversation turn (D-90 quoted 13.9% of conversation turns).
- What else could be done: A fuzzy match (edit distance on frames) as a second marker.
- How to change this decision later: m7_eval/frames.py.
- Status: done

### D-149: The rules-versus-LLM comparison stays STALE; a refresh target is added but not run   (Phase C / m7_eval / research-scientist-eval / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The only LLM outputs (results/m3_extract/llm_STALE_pre_refreeze/) were made on data that no longer exists (D-120). Rerun now, or report stale?
- Options we considered:
  1. Score the stale outputs anyway. Pro: a number. Con: wrong; the gold changed.
  2. Re-run the LLM from m7. Pro: current. Con: m7 may not import m3; the run takes about 25 minutes and writes into m3's folder.
  3. Mark STALE in the tables; add `mingw32-make m7-e2-llm-refresh`, which calls m3's own target `m3-run-llm-dev` only when Ollama with qwen3:4b-instruct answers on 127.0.0.1, then re-runs m7. m7 scores results/m3_extract/llm/ automatically when it exists.
- What we chose: Option 3.
- Why: Honest now, one command later, no boundary broken.
- What we actually did: e2_extraction in m7_eval/experiments.py; the LLM row says STALE and is not scored; the paired table says it was not computed. A test with fake fresh LLM outputs proves the scoring and the paired bootstrap work.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Even when refreshed, 32 of the 40 subset conversations are in-sample for the rules (D-121), which favours the rules.
- What else could be done: Choose a fresh LLM subset from dev conversations the rules were never fixed on (if any remain).
- How to change this decision later: m7_eval/module.mk (m7-e2-llm-refresh), m3_extract/module.mk.
- Status: needs human (run the refresh, about 25 min)

### D-150: E7 user-study kit: within-subject, flags shown versus hidden, simulated imperfect flags   (Phase C / m7_eval / research-scientist-eval / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: How a small group of people can test whether verifier flags help reviewers or make them over-trust the flags (automation bias: trusting the machine and missing what it does not point at).
- Options we considered:
  1. Between-subject (half the people see flags). Pro: no learning effect. Con: needs many people; we will have few.
  2. Within-subject: everyone reviews 20 records, 10 with flags and 10 without, in counterbalanced blocks and random order. The flags are SIMULATED: 7 of 10 planted errors flagged, 3 not, plus 2 false flags on clean records. Pro: works with 6-12 people; shows automation bias directly. Con: people may learn during the session.
- What we chose: Option 2, descriptive results only.
- Why: With few participants, significance tests would mislead; catch rates with Wilson intervals and times are honest.
- What we actually did: m7_eval/e7_kit/: protocol.md, consent_form_en.md, consent_form_kn.md (not native-validated), sus_questionnaire.md, 20 records (record.v1, validated in tests) and review packets built from DEV gold, answer_key.csv, design.csv (12 participant slots), record_sheet.csv, build_records.py (seed 20261109), analyse.py (`python -m m7_eval e7-analyse <sheet>`). Not run with people.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The Kannada consent text and conversations are not native-validated. The SUS wording is paraphrased (UNVERIFIED against the original). Set A has 1 withheld flag and set B 2 (3 cannot split evenly). Ethics approval need is UNVERIFIED.
- What else could be done: More participants and a between-subject arm; real reviewers (nurses, pharmacists) instead of classmates.
- How to change this decision later: m7_eval/e7_kit/build_records.py (MISSED, FALSE_FLAG, N_PARTICIPANTS), then `mingw32-make m7-e7-kit`.
- Status: needs human (run the study)

### D-151: Where m7 writes, and what it did not write   (Phase C / m7_eval / research-scientist-eval / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The brief lists results/tables, results/plots, results/failure_gallery.md and infra/cost_report.md as m7 outputs; this build was told to write only m7_eval/ and results/m7_eval/.
- Options we considered:
  1. Write to the brief's paths. Pro: matches section 7. Con: breaks this build's write boundary.
  2. Write everything under results/m7_eval/<split>/ and leave copying to the orchestrator.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: The delegation message narrows the brief; boundaries keep parallel agents safe.
- What we actually did: results/m7_eval/dev/{tables,plots,summary.md,failure_gallery.md,cost_report.md,manifest.json,run_log.json}. infra/cost_report.md and the infra/inventory.md line are NOT written (human_todo item). Runtime of `make eval` on the laptop: about 43 s for all targets (m3-score, m4-mock, m7-eval); see run_log.json.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: A reader looking at results/tables/ finds nothing until the orchestrator copies or links the files.
- What else could be done: The orchestrator widens m7's boundary and m7 writes the brief's paths directly.
- How to change this decision later: `--out` in m7_eval/__main__.py and m7_eval/module.mk.
- Status: done

### D-152: Audio "clean on disk, variants on demand"   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Disk had about 2.7 GB free; storing all 3360 WAVs did not fit and m2 needs room for models.
- Options we considered:
  1. Keep every variant on disk. Pro: simple. Con: does not fit.
  2. Keep only clean files; re-make noise/speed variants when needed from the clean file plus seed, noise type, SNR and speed, and check each against a stored sha256. Pro: about 4x smaller. Con: needs a command and bit-exact determinism.
  3. Drop variants. Con: loses the noise and speed experiments.
- What we chose: Option 2. Every variant hash is in data/manifest.jsonl (test ones also appended to test.lock). manifest.v1 has no "stored or not" field and contracts/ is off limits, so that flag lives in data/audio_index/tts_index.jsonl (storage = stored | on_demand, reproducible, clean_file).
- Why: Hashes are recorded before deletion, so nothing is unverifiable.
- What we actually did: m1_data/audio_build.py (render, build, materialise_row), audio_run.py, cli: `python -m m1_data audio-materialise --ids all --split dev --variants white_snr10 --out DIR` (test needs ALLOW_TEST=1), `audio-verify --materialise-sample N`. Checked all 2440 on-demand variants (dev + test, with ALLOW_TEST=1): all rebuilt bit-exact (results/m1_data/materialise_full_check.json). Tests: m1_data/tests/test_audio_ondemand.py.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Bit-exactness was shown on this machine only (numpy + ffmpeg version); another machine could differ. The 1.2 GB first run's _turns scratch was deleted, so the clean files cannot be rebuilt without running TTS again.
- What else could be done: ask for a stored-flag field in manifest.v1.
- How to change this decision later: audio_build.build and audio_run.
- Status: done

### D-153: 312 older dev noise files are kept on disk (not bit-reproducible)   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The first run made dev noise variants from an unrounded float signal, so re-making them from the stored 16-bit clean file differs by up to one rounding step in some samples.
- Options we considered: (1) delete and redefine them from the clean file (saves about 127 MB, but changes the files); (2) keep the originals and record their hash as the truth.
- What we chose: Option 2, as instructed. Speed variants (104 files) and all test pitch voice noise variants were bit-exact and deleted. New variants are defined from the stored clean file, so they are exact.
- Why: Honest hashes beat saved space.
- What we actually did: 6 noise types x 52 dev conversations stay as storage=stored, reproducible=false; materialised_sha256 is recorded for comparison.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: costs about 127 MB.
- What else could be done: redefine and delete them if disk is needed.
- How to change this decision later: delete the files and set storage in the index.
- Status: done

### D-154: FLEURS subset built; FLEURS WAVs are 32-bit float and converted to PCM16   (Phase B / m1_data / data-scientist / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The first FLEURS run crashed: the files are IEEE-float WAV, not PCM16.
- Options we considered: store float (breaks 16-bit rule and python wave); convert deterministically to 16-bit.
- What we chose: Convert (round, clip), recorded sha256 is of the converted file.
- Why: Same format as all other audio.
- What we actually did: fleurs.float_wav_to_pcm16 plus tests. 250 utterances (124 dev, 126 test, split by sentence), 108 MB, CC-BY-4.0; 126 lines appended to test.lock.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: speaker overlap between dev and test not ruled out; licence page not re-fetched by me this session (UNVERIFIED).
- What else could be done: Common Voice or Kathbath.
- How to change this decision later: fleurs.py.
- Status: done

### D-155: Audio accepted; one integrity check rebuilt test audio, but nothing was evaluated on it   (Phase B / m1_data / orchestrator / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The audio build finished with a "clean recording on disk, noisy and speed-changed copies rebuilt on demand" design (to fit the nearly full disk). To prove the on-demand copies are exact, the builder rebuilt all 2440 of them once, including 2112 TEST copies, using the ALLOW_TEST=1 switch of the m1 rebuild command. Is that a test-split access, and do we accept the audio?
- Options we considered: (1) Treat it as a test run and log it in results/test_access_log.md. Pro: maximally cautious. Con: that log is defined by the brief for `make eval-test` (scoring a system on test); logging a data check there would make the one real test run look like the second one. (2) Record it here as a data-integrity check, not an evaluation. Pro: accurate; no model, metric or threshold saw test audio. Con: relies on this entry for transparency.
- What we chose: Option 2, and accept the audio.
- Why: Rebuilding audio from locked clean files and comparing sha256 hashes reads no gold label, runs no ASR or extraction, and produces no score; it only proves the stored hashes are reproducible.
- What we actually did: Re-ran checks myself: test.lock +2767 lines, 0 removed; manifest +3610 rows, 0 removed; text data (transcripts, gold, templates, lexicon) unchanged; 4796/4796 manifest rows valid; `split-lock: OK` (missing git-ignored copies are warnings by design); `audio-verify` checked 1170 stored files, 0 bad, and results/m1_data/materialise_full_check.json shows 2440/2440 on-demand copies rebuilt exactly; `pytest m1_data tests` 228 passed, 1 skipped; ruff clean; 0 audio files tracked by git; results/test_access_log.md does not exist. data/audio is 521 MB; 2.9 GB free on C:. FLEURS kn_in: 250 utterances (124 dev, 126 test), stored separately as source=public_real.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: 312 dev noise files (52 conversations x 6) are not bit-reproducible from the 16-bit clean file and stay on disk with their original hashes. Bit-exactness on another machine or ffmpeg version is UNVERIFIED. The "second voice" is a pitch-shifted copy of the one MMS voice (D-132), a weak form of the brief's held-out-voice rule. The per-turn synthesis cache was deleted, so rebuilding clean audio means running TTS again. The FLEURS licence page was not re-fetched in this round.
- What else could be done: Add a "stored or on-demand" field to manifest.v2 instead of the sidecar file data/audio_index/tts_index.jsonl (contract request filed by m1).
- How to change this decision later: m1_data/audio_build.py, `python -m m1_data audio-materialise`; never rebuild test audio for tuning.
- Status: done

### D-156: Save with one call: the server's verdict id plus one answer per flag (no separate approve call)   (Phase C / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: m5 changed /save (D-80, D-94) to take {verdict_id, resolutions}. Security finding F3 said the old approve-then-save order left a record marked approved when the save was refused.
- Options we considered:
  1. Keep calling /approve first. Pro: no change. Con: a refused save leaves the record stamped approved.
  2. Call /approve only after a successful save. Pro: no stale approval. Con: pointless, because m5 /save already means approve.
  3. Call only /save. Pro: one gated step. Con: m5's /approve endpoint still exists and has no flag check.
- What we chose: Option 3. The UI never calls /approve.
- Why: Save = approve in m5. One call means there is no half-approved state. A physician's per-flag answer becomes one resolution per (check id, field path), the same key m5 uses, so two reasons on one field do not make a duplicate.
- What we actually did: m6_ui/gate.py (MockGate mirrors the m5 rules; HttpGate talks to m5), m6_ui/review.py build_resolutions, m6_ui/app.py _approve. After an edit the save uses the FIRST verdict id (the one that held the flags), as m5 requires. Each m5 refusal (stale, not re-verified, forbidden, conflict, bad request, not found...) has Kannada and English text. Tests: m6_ui/tests/test_gate.py, test_security.py. Real m5 check: m6_ui/tests/test_live_m5.py (passed with the DB and API up).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: m5's /approve endpoint still has no flag check (see open_questions.md). An edit that makes the checker raise a NEW kind of flag cannot be saved under m5's rules; the UI shows the refusal and the physician must edit again. The mock gate is a copy of the m5 rules written from reading main.py, not m5 code.
- What else could be done: Ask m5 to remove or gate /approve. Show the physician which flag blocks an edited save before pressing Approve.
- How to change this decision later: m6_ui/gate.py, m6_ui/app.py (_approve), m6_ui/review.py.
- Status: done

### D-157: Each person logs in as their own m5 user; the role comes from the token   (Phase C / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Security finding F1: the role was a button click and live mode used one shared server credential, so every user acted as that one identity.
- Options we considered:
  1. Keep the role picker. Pro: easy demo. Con: any role can approve; the audit log names the wrong person.
  2. One shared service user. Pro: simple. Con: loses who clicked.
  3. A login screen per person (m5 mock issuer), token kept in the server-side session object, role read from the token. Pro: m5 sees the real user and row-level security filters lists. Con: the user must know their user id.
- What we chose: Option 3 in live mode. The practice role picker stays in mock mode only, labelled as practice; mock roles see only their own record types and only a doctor can approve.
- Why: The server is the authority. The page reads the role claim only to decide what to show; m5 checks the signed token on every call, so a forged display role cannot change what m5 allows.
- What we actually did: m6_ui/gate.py HttpGate.login/logout/role; login screen in m6_ui/app.py; ASHA patient list from GET /patients (m5 row-level security). M6_API_USER, M6_API_PASSWORD and M6_SERVER_IDS are gone. Tests: test_security.py test_f1_*.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The role claim is decoded without checking the signature on this page (display only). m5 has no 'list records' endpoint, so the record id is typed. Token lifetime is at most 15 minutes; after that the person is sent back to the login screen. The sandbox password is shared by all test users (an m5 limit). Not tried in a real browser.
- What else could be done: A user picker from m5's seed list; a 'my records' endpoint in m5; a real identity provider.
- How to change this decision later: m6_ui/gate.py, m6_ui/app.py (_login_page, _role).
- Status: done

### D-158: Live mode shows the record that m5 holds, loaded by the same id it saves   (Phase C / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Security finding F2: live mode showed a local fixture but saved a different server row through an id-mapping file.
- Options we considered:
  1. Keep the mapping file. Pro: none. Con: a human can approve something they never saw.
  2. Load the record and verdict from m5 by id. Pro: what you see is what is saved. Con: needs the verify call and a typed record id.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: This is the 'record attached to the wrong patient' class of error.
- What we actually did: HttpGate.load_record = GET /records/{id} then POST /records/{id}/verify; an edit = PATCH then verify; the transcript is still looked up locally by source_transcript_id (m5 has no transcript endpoint). Tests: test_f2_f3_live_*, test_live_edit_goes_to_the_server_*.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: If the transcript is not in the local fixtures, highlights are missing and a load error is shown. Record ids must be UUIDs and are checked before use, so a typed '../' never becomes a request path.
- What else could be done: A transcript endpoint in m5.
- How to change this decision later: m6_ui/gate.py, m6_ui/app.py (_work).
- Status: done

### D-159: Hidden text-direction characters are shown as [U+XXXX] and refused in edits   (Phase C / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Security finding F6: Unicode direction controls (U+202A-202E, U+2066-2069) can reorder digits on screen so a number looks different.
- Options we considered:
  1. Strip them silently. Pro: clean. Con: hides that the text was tampered with.
  2. Replace with a visible marker and warn; refuse them in edits. Pro: the reviewer sees it. Con: slightly noisy.
- What we chose: Option 2. Zero-width joiner and non-joiner stay (Kannada needs them).
- Why: A reviewer should see that something odd is in the text, not have it removed.
- What we actually did: m6_ui/highlight.py neutralise()/esc(); data.bidi_problems() uses contracts.validate.bidi_errors; banner review.bidi_found; edits refused with resolve.bidi. Tests: test_highlight.py, test_security.py (test_f6_*).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Browser rendering is UNVERIFIED (no real browser run).
- What else could be done: Reject these characters at m5 on input (m5 job).
- How to change this decision later: m6_ui/highlight.py.
- Status: done

### D-160: Small security fixes F4, F5, F7 and a documented limit for F8   (Phase C / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Remaining findings: records stayed editable after a save (F4), the env refusal switch could be unticked (F5), the mock verifier accepted a one-letter drug (F7), no anti-framing header (F8).
- Options we considered:
  1. Fix all in code. Pro: done. Con: F8 is not possible in Streamlit config.
  2. Fix F4-F7 and document F8. Pro: honest. Con: F8 stays open.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: Streamlit 1.67 has no setting for X-Frame-Options or frame-ancestors; a proxy is out of scope for a one-laptop class project.
- What we actually did: F4: after a save, answer/edit buttons are not drawn and Approve is disabled; a repeat save shows green 'already saved'. F5: MockGate(locked=True) ignores 'off' and the checkbox is disabled. F7: the mock verifier needs a whole-token match and at least 3 letters for a drug. F8: README section. Tests: test_security.py.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: F8 is open (clickjacking from another page in the same browser). F7 is still a stand-in for the real m4 lexicon check.
- What else could be done: A reverse proxy adding X-Frame-Options: DENY.
- How to change this decision later: m6_ui/app.py, gate.py, verifier.py, README.md.
- Status: partly done

### D-161: Kannada wording fixes from the review (D-124) and new text for the safety reason codes   (Phase C / m6_ui / frontend-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The simulated Kannada reviewer found 12 major wording problems and the app had no text for child-dose, cannot-verify, route, liquid-strength or max-daily flags.
- Options we considered:
  1. Accept all majors and add keys now. Pro: closes the gap. Con: model knowledge only, no native speaker.
  2. Wait for a native speaker. Pro: better wording. Con: misleading text stays in the app.
- What we chose: Option 1, with everything marked 'not native-validated' and sent to native review.
- Why: Texts like 'checker: correct' read as a guarantee; a negation warning that does not say the meaning may be reversed under-warns.
- What we actually did: m6_ui/i18n/kn.json + en.json (version 1.1.0, same keys, NFC): reason.C3 (meaning may be reversed), reason.C4 (has a subject), badge.ok 'checker saw nothing', one word each for red-marked / coloured words / speech recognition, approve.ready word order, fail.blind_spot (ಇಲ್ಲ and ಬೇಡ), mock banner (do not use for treatment), refusal text without the 'open flag' calque, a loud 'no doctor has checked this' box with icon, error.load without English. New: reason.code.* (out_of_range, exceeds_max_daily, unit_not_allowed, route, cannot_verify and 5 sub-reasons, shown with a 'could not check' note), gate.code.* for every m5 refusal, login/approve texts, record.v2 labels (v2.*, v2val.*). record.v2 records show route, strength, formulation, status, as-needed, food timing, age band and weight read-only. Tests: test_i18n.py, test_security.py.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: All Kannada is AI-drafted and not native-validated; the clinical sentences (child dose, liquid strength, route) need a clinician and a native speaker. Minor review items were not all changed.
- What else could be done: A read-aloud session with two native-speaking clinic staff.
- How to change this decision later: m6_ui/i18n/kn.json and en.json (keep keys in sync; test_i18n.py checks).
- Status: needs human

### D-162: m6 pass accepted; m5's /approve still needs the same flag check   (Phase C / m6_ui / orchestrator / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The m6 pass (Sonnet) moved the app to the gated /save, fixed security findings F1-F7, documented F8, and applied the Kannada fixes (D-123, D-124). Accept it, and what is left?
- Options we considered: (1) Accept and route the m5 leftovers to the Phase C integration work. Pro: m6 itself is done and tested against the real backend. Con: /approve in m5 can still mark a record approved without the flag check. (2) Hold m6 until m5 changes. Pro: one combined change. Con: blocks finished work for no gain.
- What we chose: Option 1.
- Why: m6 no longer calls /approve at all, so the app cannot use that route; the remaining risk is a hand-crafted API call, which belongs to m5.
- What we actually did: Re-ran checks myself: `pytest m6_ui` 163 passed, 2 skipped (live tests); then started the m5 database and API on 127.0.0.1, ran the 2 live m6-to-m5 tests (2 passed), and stopped both (nothing listening on 54329 or 8000 afterwards); ruff clean; only m6_ui and results/m6_ui changed (m5 untouched). The builder ran the new tests against the old code (git archive of HEAD): 73 failed there; it did not watch each fail before writing the fix, so "red before green" is shown after the fact.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: m5 /approve has no flag check (fix in Phase C: make it run the save-gate check or remove it). No list-records or transcript endpoint in m5, so the reviewer types a record id. An edit that creates a new kind of flag cannot be saved yet. F8 (clickjacking headers) stays open because Streamlit cannot set them. The role shown on screen is decoded from the token without checking its signature, for display only; the server still checks it. All new Kannada text is AI-drafted (native review in HUMAN_TODO).
- What else could be done: Put a small local proxy in front of Streamlit for headers (out of scope).
- How to change this decision later: m5_backend/app/main.py (/approve, list and transcript endpoints) in Phase C; m6_ui/gate.py.
- Status: done

### D-163: Remove POST /records/{id}/approve; /save is the only approval   (Phase C / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The old /approve route set a record to "approved" with the physician's name and no flag check (review finding D-123 F3). Fix it or remove it?
- Options we considered:
  1. Gate /approve like /save. Pro: keeps an "approved but not saved" step. Con: two routes to maintain and to get wrong; nobody needs the middle state (m6 no longer calls it).
  2. Remove /approve. Pro: one door, one set of rules. Con: a client that still calls it gets 404.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: Every extra path to "approved" is a place to forget the flag check. /save already stores the verdict on the server and needs one answer per open flag, and the database trigger still checks the saved state.
- What we actually did: deleted the route in m5_backend/app/main.py. Test `test_handcrafted_approve_with_unresolved_flags_is_refused` in m5_backend/tests/test_followups.py FAILED on the old code (HTTP 200, record became "approved") and passes now (404/405, record stays draft). Run `uv run pytest m5_backend/tests/test_followups.py`.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The database still lets a physician set status "approved" by direct SQL (RLS: only in her own name); the trigger gates only "saved". The status stays in the schema for old data.
- What else could be done: forbid "approved" in the CHECK constraint and RLS too (needs a migration).
- How to change this decision later: re-add a route in app/main.py with the same code as save_record; write the tests first.
- Status: done

### D-164: Add GET /records (list) and GET /records/{id}/transcript   (Phase C / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: m6 had no way to list records or fetch a transcript from the server. How to add both without opening new holes?
- Options we considered:
  1. List and filter in Python after reading everything. Con: one bug leaks rows.
  2. Let the database do it: run the query as the caller's role so forced RLS hides the rest. Transcript: files named by the encounter id read from the database.
  3. Store transcripts in a new table. Pro: RLS on the text itself. Con: new table, new policies, bigger change.
- What we chose: Option 2 for both.
- Why: Same pattern as audio, which already passed the matrix. The transcript file name comes from the database row, so a record that claims another record's transcript id reads nothing (tested).
- What we actually did: app/main.py (`list_records`, `get_transcript`), setting `M5_TRANSCRIPT_DIR`, seed writes synthetic transcripts. The list returns id, record_id, encounter_id, patient_id, status, flagged_count; limit 1..100, offset >= 0 (else 422). ASHA sees only her patients' records; pharmacist and researcher get 403. Matrix: 160 new endpoint cases, 20 id-guess cases and token attacks, all pass (results/m5_backend/access_matrix_summary.json).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Transcript files sit on disk outside the database: whoever can read the folder can read them (disk permissions are not tested). Pages use OFFSET, so rows added while paging can shift. access_case.v1 has a fixed resource list, so the new cases use resource "records" (contract request in open_questions.md).
- What else could be done: a transcripts table with RLS; cursor pagination.
- How to change this decision later: app/main.py, matrix/generate.py, tests/access_matrix_expected.yaml (`endpoints` section).
- Status: done

### D-165: Load the verifier by import path (MAATUMITRA_VERIFIER)   (Phase C / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: How integration plugs the real checker (m4) into m5 without m5 importing m4.
- Options we considered:
  1. Integration code calls create_app(verifier=fn). Pro: simple. Con: needs a custom launcher script.
  2. Also read a setting `module:function` from .env at startup. Pro: no launcher; one line to document. Con: the operator controls which code runs, so a bad value must fail loudly.
  3. Fall back to the default if loading fails. Con: silently runs a different checker than intended.
- What we chose: Options 1 and 2; a bad value stops startup (no fallback).
- Why: A silent downgrade of a safety check is worse than a failed start. The value comes from .env, never from a request. The default stays "flag everything for manual review".
- What we actually did: app/verifier.py `load_verifier`, setting `verifier_spec`, `.env.example`. Tests use a tiny fake module m5_backend/tests/fake_verifier_mod.py (10 bad specs rejected; a verdict is stored with the fake's version).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Anyone who can edit .env can run any importable function as the verifier. Only works for a function record_json -> verdict.v1 dict. Not run with the real m4 (UNVERIFIED).
- What else could be done: an allow-list of module names.
- How to change this decision later: app/verifier.py and `create_app` in app/main.py.
- Status: done (real m4 wiring UNVERIFIED until Phase C integration)

### D-166: Accept record.v2 bodies next to record.v1   (Phase C / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: m3 will emit record.v2 (route, strength, status, evidence). Should POST/PATCH /records take it now?
- Options we considered:
  1. Wait and add new columns. Con: big migration; the contract note asked for JSONB.
  2. Validate v1 or v2 with contracts.validate and store the whole document in the existing JSONB column.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: Small and safe: the document was already stored as JSON. We also refuse bidi control characters (text-direction tricks) on both versions.
- What we actually did: `_check_record` in app/main.py. Tests: a v2 fixture is stored unchanged, verified and updated; a bad route, a bidi character, an unknown version and status "saved" all give 422.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The research export reads only v1-style fields, so v2-only data is not exported (safe, but less useful). The built-in default verifier knows nothing about v2.
- What else could be done: export route and status after a privacy review.
- How to change this decision later: `_check_record` in app/main.py and app/export.py.
- Status: done

### D-167: m5 follow-ups accepted; "approved" status still only guarded for saved records   (Phase C / m5_backend / orchestrator / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The m5 follow-up pass (Sonnet) removed /approve, added list and transcript endpoints, a verifier plug-in setting and record.v2 bodies. Accept, and what stays open?
- Options we considered: (1) Accept and send the remaining gap to the final security audit (C2). Pro: everything requested works and is tested. Con: one database-level gap stays. (2) Ask for another pass now. Pro: closes it sooner. Con: the gap needs a direct SQL login as a physician, which the final audit should test anyway.
- What we chose: Option 1.
- Why: The API no longer offers any way to approve without the gate; the remaining path is direct SQL by the record's own physician.
- What we actually did: Re-ran checks myself: started the m5 database, `pytest m5_backend` 170 passed (no skips), stopped the database (nothing listening on 54329 or 8000); ruff clean; access matrix 1757 cases, 0 unauthorized successes of 1642 deny-expected (rule-of-three upper bound 3/1642 = 0.0018), 0 unexpected denials, 0 integrity failures; .env.example gained only two empty settings (M5_TRANSCRIPT_DIR, MAATUMITRA_VERIFIER); .env stays git-ignored. The builder showed a hand-made /approve call succeeding on the old code (HTTP 200) and refused now.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The database trigger checks only the "saved" status, so a physician with direct SQL access can still set "approved" on their own record without resolving flags (C2 must test and fix, e.g. extend the trigger). The new endpoint cases reuse the resource name "records" because access_case.v1 has a fixed resource list (contract request filed). The real m4 verifier through MAATUMITRA_VERIFIER is not tested yet (Phase C integration).
- What else could be done: Extend the save-gate trigger to every status change in the same pass as the final audit.
- How to change this decision later: m5_backend/sql/050_gate.sql, m5_backend/app/main.py.
- Status: done

### D-168: One adapter function wires m4 into m5   (Phase C / integration / integration-qa / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: m5 calls `fn(record)` and must not import m4. m4 needs the record AND the transcript. Where does the glue live, and what happens when it cannot run m4?
- Options we considered:
  1. Make m5 import m4. Pro: simple. Con: breaks the rule that modules do not import each other.
  2. An adapter in integration/ that m5 loads by name (`MAATUMITRA_VERIFIER=integration.verifier_adapter:verify_record`). Pro: allowed place, no m5 change. Con: it finds the transcript from a field inside the record.
  3. Change the m5 hook to also receive the encounter id. Pro: transcript bound to the encounter. Con: needs an m5 change (not ours to make).
- What we chose: Option 2, failing closed: no transcript, record.v2, a crash or an invalid verdict returns a valid verdict with ONE C1 flag, never a pass.
- Why: it works today without touching modules, and a physician must resolve anything m4 could not really check.
- What we actually did: integration/verifier_adapter.py; tests in integration/tests/test_verifier_adapter.py. The tests were written after the code, not before (a process slip).
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: whoever edits a draft record can change `source_transcript_id` (only an id whitelist and m4's span check guard it). m4 has no record.v2 support yet, so v2 records always get the one fail-closed flag.
- What else could be done: option 3; teach m4 v2.
- How to change this decision later: edit integration/verifier_adapter.py; unset the env variable to go back to "flag everything".
- Status: done

### D-169: End to end through the real HTTP API on 10 seeded DEV conversations   (Phase C / integration / integration-qa / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: how to prove the chain works from text to saved record, without touching test data.
- Options we considered:
  1. Call module functions directly. Pro: fast, no DB. Con: skips the save gate and database security.
  2. Start the real API as a subprocess and talk HTTP, with a user per role. Pro: tests the gate, row-level security and the adapter for real. Con: needs the local database.
- What we chose: Option 2. Mock ASR (the gold text through m2's transcript builder, because m2 has no real dev output for these ids), m3 rules, m4 via the adapter, asha creates, physician resolves and saves, 11 users read back, m6's own HttpGate loads the draft. Planted-error records (a flipped negation) are in a separate table and are fixed by an `edited` resolution.
- Why: the safety claim lives in the gate; only an HTTP run exercises it.
- What we actually did: integration/e2e.py, integration/tests/test_e2e.py (skips cleanly if the DB is down), results/integration/e2e_dev.md. Seed 20261112, ids from data/splits/dev.txt only.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: ASR is mocked (gold text), so ASR errors are not tested here. The "reviewer" confirms every flag; that is a test policy, not clinical review. Ten conversations prove wiring, not accuracy.
- What else could be done: run with m2's real transcripts for template ids once they exist; use record.v2.
- How to change this decision later: integration/e2e.py (`select_ids`, `AUTHOR`, reviewer rule in `run_one`).
- Status: done

### D-170: Integration findings to fix in the modules   (Phase C / integration / integration-qa / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: which mismatches found while wiring belong to which module (we do not fix modules).
- Options we considered: fix here (not allowed), or report to the orchestrator.
- What we chose: report.
- Why: modules are owned by other agents.
- What we actually did: contract checks found 0 mismatches in 16 boundary samples (results/integration/boundary_checks.md). Behaviour findings: (1) m5 `/verify` stores a new verdict on every call and `/save` demands the NEWEST verdict id, so a second person opening the record in m6 (which verifies on load) makes the first physician's save fail with 409; fix in m5 or m6 (reuse the latest verdict for an unchanged record). (2) m6 live cannot reopen a SAVED record: load always calls /verify and m5 refuses saved rows (404). (3) m5's verifier hook gets no encounter id (see adapter weakness). (4) m3 and m4 still speak record.v1 only. (5) After save the nurse can no longer read the record (policy shows only draft/flagged); expected by the policy, but m6 nurse screens should not promise it.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: samples are capped at 400 per boundary; m7 outputs were not validated (no schema covers them).
- What else could be done: a CI job that runs this folder with the DB up.
- How to change this decision later: not applicable (reports only).
- Status: needs human (orchestrator routes to m5/m6)

### D-171: The whole chain works on 10 dev conversations; four behaviour issues go to two fix passes   (Phase C / integration / orchestrator / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: Integration QA (Sonnet) wired the real m4 verifier into m5 through an adapter and ran 10 seeded dev conversations end to end. It found 0 contract mismatches but 4 behaviour issues. Where are they fixed?
- Options we considered: (1) One big fix pass touching m3, m4, m5 and m6 together. Pro: one round. Con: several builders' files at once, and m2 is still running `make eval`, which reads m3 and m4 outputs. (2) Two passes: m5 now (it does not touch m3/m4 outputs), then the record.v2 migration of m3 and m4 after m2 finishes. Pro: no clash. Con: two rounds.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: m5's fixes are independent and can run alongside m2; changing m3/m4 outputs while m2's evaluation reads them could give mixed results.
- What we actually did: Re-ran the checks myself: started the m5 database, `pytest integration` 23 passed (no skips), stopped the database and API (nothing listening on 54329 or 8000); ruff clean. Accepted the builder's results: 10/10 dev records saved through the real HTTP API with the real verifier; 6 with flags were refused (409) until each flag was resolved; 2 planted negation flips were flagged (C3), refused, edited back, re-verified clean and saved; role read-back matched the database rules for 11 users. Routed: m5 pass (now): (a) /save must not fail because someone else re-ran /verify on unchanged content (tie the verdict to the record content, or keep earlier verdicts valid while content is unchanged); (b) a saved record can be reopened read-only with its stored verdict (m6 got 404); (c) the verifier hook must receive the encounter id from the database, not the record's editable source_transcript_id. Migration pass (after m2): m3 and m4 produce and check record.v2; the adapter stops sending every v2 record to a single fail-closed flag.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: The test used the mock speech recognizer (gold text), so this shows the plumbing works, not how real speech errors flow through. Only 10 conversations; tests were written after the code; "confirm every flag" was a test rule, not a clinical review.
- What else could be done: Repeat the 10-conversation run with real m2 output once m2 has dev transcripts for conversation audio.
- How to change this decision later: integration/verifier_adapter.py, integration/e2e.py, m5_backend/app/main.py.
- Status: partly done

### D-172: One verdict per record content, so a second verify no longer breaks the first physician's save   (Phase C / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: /save used to need the NEWEST verdict. If a second person (or a second browser tab) pressed "verify" on the same unchanged record, the first physician's save failed with 409 "stale", although nothing had changed.
- Options we considered:
  1. Keep "newest wins" and tell users not to verify twice. Pro: no change. Con: a real clinic would hit it constantly.
  2. Accept any earlier verdict whose content fingerprint (hash) matches. Pro: simple. Con: the database trigger looks at "the newest verdict", so API and database could disagree if two verdicts exist for one content.
  3. Allow only ONE verdict per record content (unique index); a second verify returns the existing verdict. Pro: API and trigger cannot disagree; nothing to go stale. Con: a verifier upgrade does not re-check unchanged content until the content changes.
- What we chose: Option 3.
- Why: The rule "a verdict belongs to the exact content it was computed for" is then enforced by the database itself, and the stale check only triggers on a real content change.
- What we actually did: unique index `verdicts_one_per_content` in m5_backend/sql/050_gate.sql; /verify in m5_backend/app/main.py returns the stored verdict for identical content (INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING handles two verifies at the same moment); /save compares content hashes only. Tests: m5_backend/tests/test_phase_c_fixes.py (a_*). Changing content and then changing it back makes the original verdict valid again (same hash), which is correct.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Re-verifying after a verifier upgrade needs a content change or an operator step (not built). A verdict made by an ASHA or nurse is reused for the physician's save; the physician still has to resolve every flag herself. Two truly simultaneous verifies were handled in code (ON CONFLICT) but not load-tested (UNVERIFIED).
- What else could be done: a verifier_version column so a newer verifier can supersede old verdicts explicitly.
- How to change this decision later: drop the index in 050_gate.sql and restore the "latest" comparison in save_record.
- Status: done

### D-173: Read-only reopen of a saved record (GET /records/{id}/review)   (Phase C / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The review screen got 404 when it reopened a saved record, because /verify only accepts unsaved records.
- Options we considered:
  1. Let /verify run on saved records. Pro: screen unchanged. Con: could add a verdict row or invite changing a finished record.
  2. A new GET that only reads. Pro: cannot change anything; same row-level security as the record. Con: the screen must call a new route.
- What we chose: Option 2.
- Why: A saved record should be viewable but not touchable. A GET with no writes makes that easy to prove.
- What we actually did: GET /records/{id}/review returns status, approver, the stored verdict for the CURRENT content (null if never verified), open flags, and for physicians the resolutions. /verify on a saved record still answers 404. Tests in test_phase_c_fixes.py (b_*) check that no rows are written, that another facility and a nurse get 404, and that no token gets 401.
- What could go wrong / weaknesses: Only physicians can read resolutions (database grant), so other roles see an empty list; m6 must be changed to call the new route (UNVERIFIED with the live screen). The hand-written access matrix YAML was not extended with this route (the tests cover it instead).
- What else could be done: add the route to access_matrix_expected.yaml by hand.
- How to change this decision later: m5_backend/app/main.py (review_record) and m5_backend/CONTRACT.md.
- Status: done

### D-174: The verifier receives the encounter id and transcript that m5 loads from the database   (Phase C / m5_backend / backend-security-engineer / 2026-11-12)
- What we needed to decide: The m4 adapter picked the transcript from `source_transcript_id` inside the record, a field the author can edit.
- Options we considered:
  1. Keep the field and trust m4 to notice mismatches. Pro: no change. Con: the author steers which transcript is checked.
  2. m5 passes `encounter_id` and `transcript` (read from the record's DATABASE encounter, the same file GET /records/{id}/transcript serves). Pro: transcript is bound to the encounter. Con: hook signature changes.
- What we chose: Option 2, backward compatible.
